🎯 What This Document Is

Your complete CAPA (Certified Argo Project Associate) exam preparation system. 200 MCQs spanning all 4 CNCF Argo domains, each with detailed explanations, ASCII diagrams, and real-world context from the anihpj/jobpost Django application running on a 6-cluster Kubernetes lab. Every question builds progressive mastery: foundational concepts (Q1-Q100) → advanced production patterns (Q101-Q200).

✅ Daily Study Routine
  • ☀️ Morning (30 min): Answer 10-15 MCQs. Read ALL explanations — even for correct answers. Focus on one domain.
  • 🌤️ Afternoon (30 min): Re-read diagrams and CLI patterns from morning. Type the commands from memory on your 6-cluster lab.
  • 🌙 Evening (45 min): Practice on lab: ksw → 1-6. Submit workflows, create Applications, configure Rollouts, test EventSources.
  • 📅 Weekend (2 hrs): Full review of the week's domains. 50+ MCQs back-to-back. Identify and drill weak areas.

🎯 Scoring Strategy — The Math Behind Passing CAPA

✅ PASSING SCENARIO (75%)

  Workflows:  62/72 (86%) ████████████████████░
  Argo CD:    58/68 (85%) ████████████████████░
  Rollouts:   26/36 (72%) ██████████████████░░░
  Events:     16/24 (67%) █████████████████░░░░
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────
  TOTAL:     162/200 = 81% → COMFORTABLE PASS

⚠️ MINIMUM VIABLE (66%)

  Workflows:  54/72 (75%) ███████████████████░░░
  Argo CD:    51/68 (75%) ███████████████████░░░
  Rollouts:   18/36 (50%) ████████████░░░░░░░░░░
  Events:     10/24 (42%) ██████████░░░░░░░░░░░░
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────
  TOTAL:     133/200 = 66.5% → BARELY PASSING

💡 Key insight: If you score 80%+ on Workflows + CD (70% of the exam), you can score just 50% on Rollouts + Events (30% of the exam) and still pass comfortably. Prioritize domains 1 & 2.

🏆 Skills Transformation — Before vs After This Guide

❌ BEFORE — Common Knowledge Gaps

  • ❌ "What's the difference between a Workflow Template and a Step?"
  • ❌ "Why does Argo CD show OutOfSync when my app runs fine?"
  • ❌ "How do I pass artifacts and parameters between Workflow steps?"
  • ❌ "Blue/Green vs Canary — which strategy for which scenario?"
  • ❌ "How do EventSource, Sensor, EventBus, and Trigger connect?"
  • ❌ "Can one Argo CD instance really manage 6+ clusters?"
  • ❌ "What's the difference between sync waves and hooks?"

✅ AFTER — Skills You'll Have Mastered

  • ✅ Design complex DAG workflows with artifact passing & conditional steps
  • ✅ Configure GitOps with auto-sync, self-heal, prune, and sync windows
  • ✅ Implement canary deployments with Prometheus analysis + auto-rollback
  • ✅ Build event-driven pipelines: S3→EventSource→Sensor→Workflow
  • ✅ Manage multi-cluster deployments with ApplicationSets & hub-spoke CD
  • ✅ Apply RBAC, Dex SSO, and security best practices across Argo suite
  • ✅ Debug sync failures, stuck rollouts, and workflow errors systematically

🚨 CAPA Exam — Critical Do's & Don'ts
  • ✅ DO: Read EVERY option before selecting. CAPA uses "mostly correct" distractors that sound plausible.
  • ✅ DO: Prioritize Workflows + Argo CD questions. These two domains are 70% of your score.
  • ✅ DO: Eliminate obviously wrong answers first. Options contradicting GitOps/declarative philosophy are usually wrong.
  • ❌ DON'T: Spend more than 1 minute on any single question. Flag it, move on, return later.
  • ❌ DON'T: Leave any question blank. CAPA has no penalty for wrong answers — always guess if unsure.
  • ❌ DON'T: Second-guess yourself obsessively. Your first instinct is usually correct if you've prepared well.

Every question in this guide references this real Django application deployed across 6 clusters. Understanding this architecture connects theory to practice:

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

📊 Exam Overview & StrategySTRATEGY

CAPA Exam Format & Preparation Strategy

Exam Duration: 2 hours | Format: Performance-based (hands-on CLI) | Passing Score: 66% | Cost: $395 (includes one free retake) | Certification Validity: 3 years

4 Official Domains: Argo Workflows (36%), Argo CD (34%), Argo Rollouts (18%), Argo Events (12%). Argo Workflows + Argo CD = 70% of your score — prioritize these two domains heavily in your study.

Exam Strategy: Read all questions first (5 min) → Solve easy questions first → Flag and return to hard ones → Use argocd --help and argo --help liberally → Always verify sync with argocd app diff and argocd app get. The exam environment provides full access to Argo project documentation.

Your 6-cluster lab (below) mirrors real exam conditions. Use ksw to switch between clusters. Each scenario in Part 3 specifies which cluster to use.

📚 Pre-Learning: Kubernetes FoundationsF.1–F.5

🟢 Novice-Friendly: Building Your Mental Model

F.1 What Problem Does Kubernetes Solve? — From bare metal to VMs to containers to orchestration. The "it works on my machine" problem and how Kubernetes provides a consistent deployment platform.

F.2 Containers 101 — Docker & OCI — What IS a container? Dockerfile anatomy, image layers, registries. Multi-stage builds and distroless images for production.

F.3 Kubernetes Core Concepts — The "Big Picture" mental model: Cluster = Control Plane + Worker Nodes. Pods, declarative vs imperative, desired state reconciliation, kubectl basics.

F.4 The anihpj/jobpost Application Stack — Web tier (Django templates), API tier (Django REST Framework), Database tier (PostgreSQL 15), Redis (Celery + caching). Service discovery via DNS.

F.5 Kubernetes vs Docker Compose — Mental bridge: docker-compose.yml → Kubernetes YAML mapping. Why Kubernetes when Docker Compose works? The "Day 2" problem: self-healing, scaling, rolling updates.

If you're new to Kubernetes: Read through F.1–F.5 before diving into Part 1. These foundations will give you the mental models needed to understand the MCQs, troubleshooting issues, and lab scenarios. The entire guide uses the anihpj/jobpost Django application as its running example.

📝 Part 1: 100 Multiple Choice Questions

Complete ArgoCD/CAPA MCQ Bank — Organized by the 4 official CNCF exam domains. Each question includes a detailed explanation, diagrams, and real-world context using anihpj/jobpost.

100Questions
4Categories
100Explanations

⚙️ Category 1: Argo Workflows (36%)Q1-Q36

🟢 Novice-Friendly: Understanding Argo Workflows

This category is about defining, running, and orchestrating parallel jobs on Kubernetes using Argo Workflows. Think of Argo Workflows like an assembly line — each step (YAML template) performs a specific task, steps can run in sequence or parallel, and the output of one step can feed into the next. For anihpj/jobpost, Argo Workflows runs the CI pipeline: lint code → run tests → build Docker image → push to ECR — each step as an isolated container on your cluster.

Q1-Q36 36 Questions 🟢→🔴 Progressive

1.1 Workflow Basics — Pods, Steps & TemplatesQ1-Q12

Argo Workflows Q1 of 200
In Argo Workflows, what is the fundamental building block that defines a single unit of work?
A) A Kubernetes Pod defined directly in the Workflow spec
B) A Template — which defines the container(s), inputs, outputs, and execution behavior for one step
C) A Step — which links multiple templates together in a DAG sequence
D) A WorkflowTemplate — the reusable top-level YAML that defines the entire pipeline
✅ Correct Answer: B

In Argo Workflows, a Template is the atomic unit of execution. Templates are referenced by Steps and DAGs — a Step does NOT define execution; it invokes a template with arguments.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Argo Workflows Hierarchy — Template Is the Atom
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                        WORKFLOW                                  │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │                    entrypoint: main                        │  │
  │  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │  │
  │  │  │              DAG / Steps (Orchestration)             │  │  │
  │  │  │  ┌─────────┐    ┌─────────┐    ┌─────────┐          │  │  │
  │  │  │  │ Step A  │───▶│ Step B  │───▶│ Step C  │          │  │  │
  │  │  │  │template:│    │template:│    │template:│          │  │  │
  │  │  │  │  lint   │    │  test   │    │  build  │          │  │  │
  │  │  │  └────┬────┘    └────┬────┘    └────┬────┘          │  │  │
  │  │  │       │              │              │                │  │  │
  │  │  │  ┌────▼──────────────▼──────────────▼────────────┐  │  │  │
  │  │  │  │            TEMPLATES (Execution)              │  │  │  │
  │  │  │  │  container: python:3.11                      │  │  │  │
  │  │  │  │  command: [pytest]  args: [--cov]            │  │  │  │
  │  │  │  │  resources: {cpu:1, mem:2Gi}                 │  │  │  │
  │  │  │  │  outputs.artifacts: [{name: report}]         │  │  │  │
  │  │  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │  │  │
  │  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  Key: Template = DEFINES | Step/DAG = INVOKES | Workflow = TOP-LEVEL

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: In the anihpj/jobpost CI pipeline, the same test template runs against Python 3.10, 3.11, and 3.12 in parallel — three pods, one template definition.

Argo Workflows Q2 of 200
In a Workflow CRD YAML, which field specifies the name of the first template to execute when the Workflow is submitted?
A) spec.templates[0] — the first template defined in the spec.templates list always runs first
B) spec.entrypoint — a string naming the template that starts the entire Workflow execution
C) spec.initialTemplate — the designated starting template for the DAG
D) spec.arguments.template — passed as a parameter to select the entry template
✅ Correct Answer: B

The spec.entrypoint field is the Workflow's starting point. It holds a string that MUST match the name of one of the templates defined in spec.templates[]. Order in the templates list does NOT matter — entrypoint is the sole determinant.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Workflow CRD Anatomy — The 4 Key Fields
  apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
  kind: Workflow
  metadata:
    generateName: anihpj-ci-
  spec:
    ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
    ║  entrypoint: ci-pipeline    ← Q2 ANSWER: names the     ║
    ║                                template to run first    ║
    ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝
    
    ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
    ║  arguments:                   ← Q6: Workflow-level      ║
    ║    parameters:                     parameters           ║
    ║    - name: repo-ref                                      ║
    ║      value: main                                         ║
    ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝
    
    ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
    ║  templates:                   ← The recipe book         ║
    ║  - name: ci-pipeline              (order irrelevant)    ║
    ║    steps:                                                ║
    ║    - - name: lint                                       ║
    ║          template: lint-code                            ║
    ║      - name: test                                       ║
    ║          template: run-tests                            ║
    ║  - name: lint-code                                      ║
    ║    container:                                            ║
    ║      image: python:3.11                                 ║
    ║      command: [flake8]                                  ║
    ║  - name: run-tests                                      ║
    ║    container:                                            ║
    ║      image: python:3.11                                 ║
    ║      command: [pytest]                                  ║
    ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝
    
    ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
    ║  volumes:                      ← Shared storage for     ║
    ║  - name: workdir                    all steps           ║
    ║    emptyDir: {}                                          ║
    ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Forgetting entrypoint or misspelling it (pointing to a nonexistent template name) is a top cause of Workflow submission failures. The API server returns: spec.entrypoint "ci-pipeline" not found in templates. In anihpj/jobpost, the entrypoint ci-pipeline must match exactly the steps template name that orchestrates lint → test → build → push.

Argo Workflows Q3 of 200
When you submit a Workflow with argo submit, it goes through a defined lifecycle. Which of the following correctly lists ALL possible terminal (end) states a Workflow can reach?
A) Succeeded, Failed, Error — the three possible final outcomes
B) Succeeded and Failed — only two terminal states exist
C) Running, Succeeded, Failed — Running is a terminal state
D) Succeeded, Failed, Terminated — Terminated replaces Error
✅ Correct Answer: A

Argo Workflows has exactly three terminal states: Succeeded (all templates completed successfully), Failed (one or more templates failed), and Error (the workflow controller itself encountered an error — e.g., invalid YAML reference, missing artifact, or the Workflow was rejected by the admission controller). "Running" and "Pending" are transient, not terminal.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Argo Workflow Lifecycle — Pending → Running → Terminal
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    WORKFLOW LIFECYCLE                            │
  │                                                                  │
  │  argo submit workflow.yaml                                       │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  ┌─────────┐                                                     │
  │  │ PENDING │  ← Admission check: Valid CRD? Mutating webhooks?   │
  │  └────┬────┘                                                     │
  │       │  ✅ CRD valid, admission passed                          │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  ┌─────────┐                                                     │
  │  │ RUNNING │  ← Controller creates pods, monitors progress       │
  │  └────┬────┘                                                     │
  │       │                                                          │
  │  ┌────┴────────────┬──────────────────┐                          │
  │  ▼                 ▼                  ▼                          │
  │ ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────────────┐                 │
  │ │SUCCEEDED │  │  FAILED  │  │     ERROR        │                 │
  │ │  ✅      │  │   ❌     │  │      ⚠️          │                 │
  │ │All steps │  │≥1 step  │  │ Controller-level │                 │
  │ │passed    │  │failed   │  │failure: bad ref, │                 │
  │ │          │  │         │  │missing artifact, │                 │
  │ │          │  │         │  │invalid YAML      │                 │
  │ └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────────────┘                 │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Transient (not terminal):                                       │
  │  ❌ RUNNING — "Running" is NOT a terminal state                  │
  │  ❌ PENDING — Admission phase, not an end state                  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Key exam tip: Error ≠ Failed. Error = Workflow controller       │
  │  issues (bad template ref). Failed = Step execution issues.      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: The distinction between Failed and Error is frequently tested. A failing pytest step → Failed (step ran, returned non-zero). A misspelled template reference like template: run-testz → Error (controller cannot find the template). In anihpj/jobpost CI, a syntax error in flake8 triggers a Failed state; a typo in entrypoint triggers an Error state.

Argo Workflows Q4 of 200
Argo Workflows offers two ways to define execution flow: Steps and DAG. What is the key difference between them regarding parallel execution?
A) Only DAG supports parallelism; Steps always run sequentially
B) Steps use nested lists [[step], [step, step]] for grouping—steps in the same inner list run in parallel; DAG uses dependencies to define explicit run-after relationships
C) DAG requires a separate CRD (DAGTemplate) while Steps runs directly inside the Workflow
D) Steps and DAG are interchangeable — there is no functional difference between them
✅ Correct Answer: B

Steps uses nested YAML lists: [[A], [B, C]] means A runs first, then B and C run in parallel, then the next inner list. DAG uses explicit dependencies per task: "dependencies: [lint]" means "run after lint completes." Both support parallelism — the difference is HOW you express it.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Steps vs DAG — Two Ways to Model Execution Flow
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  STEPS (Nested Lists)              DAG (Explicit Dependencies)   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  entrypoint: ci-steps              entrypoint: ci-dag            │
  │  templates:                        templates:                    │
  │  - name: ci-steps                  - name: ci-dag                │
  │    steps:                            dag:                        │
  │    - - name: lint                      tasks:                    │
  │          template: lint              - name: lint                │
  │                                       template: lint             │
  │    - - name: test-python310          - name: test-310            │
  │          template: test                template: test            │
  │        - name: test-python311          dependencies: [lint]      │
  │          template: test                                          │
  │                                    - name: test-311              │
  │      ┌─────────────────┐              template: test             │
  │      │ test-310  test-311│ ← PARALLEL   dependencies: [lint]     │
  │      └─────────────────┘                                        │
  │                                    ┌──────────────────┐          │
  │    - - name: build                  │ test-310  test-311│ PARALLEL│
  │          template: build            └──────────────────┘          │
  │                                                                │
  │  Parallel rule: steps in SAME       Parallel rule: tasks with  │
  │  inner list run concurrently        SAME dependencies or NO    │
  │                                     dependencies run together  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  💡 When to use which?                                           │
  │  Steps: Linear pipeline with clear phases (lint→test→build)      │
  │  DAG:  Complex graphs with multiple dependency chains            │
  │        (fan-in, fan-out, diamond patterns)                       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Steps is easier to read for linear pipelines but can become confusing with deeply nested lists. DAG is more flexible for complex dependency graphs. For anihpj/jobpost, Steps is ideal: lint runs first, then test-310/test-311/test-312 run in parallel (same inner list), then build runs when all tests pass. A DAG would overcomplicate this simple linear pipeline.

Argo Workflows Q5 of 200
After a Workflow completes, hundreds of completed pods may remain in the cluster consuming API resources. What mechanism does Argo Workflows provide to automatically clean up these pods?
A) The Kubernetes garbage collector automatically deletes pods after the Workflow CRD is removed
B) You must manually run kubectl delete pods -l workflows.argoproj.io/workflow=NAME — there is no automatic cleanup
C) spec.podGC strategy — configurable to delete pods on Workflow Success, Failure, or both using OnWorkflowCompletion or OnWorkflowSuccess
D) spec.ttlStrategy — pods are deleted after a configurable time-to-live regardless of Workflow state
✅ Correct Answer: C

spec.podGC (Pod Garbage Collection) is the Argo Workflows mechanism for cleaning up completed pods. The strategy OnWorkflowCompletion deletes pods when the Workflow reaches any terminal state (Succeeded/Failed/Error). OnWorkflowSuccess only cleans up on success — useful for keeping failed pods for debugging.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Pod GC Strategies — Controlling Pod Cleanup
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  spec:                                                           │
  │    podGC:                                                        │
  │      strategy: OnWorkflowCompletion    ← DELETES pods when done  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  STRATEGY                     BEHAVIOR                      │ │
  │  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤ │
  │  │  OnPodCompletion   (default)  Delete pod when its template  │ │
  │  │                               completes (Success/Fail)      │ │
  │  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤ │
  │  │  OnPodSuccess                 Delete pod only on Success    │ │
  │  │                               Keep failed pods for debug    │ │
  │  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤ │
  │  │  OnWorkflowCompletion         Delete ALL pods only when     │ │
  │  │                               entire Workflow is DONE       │ │
  │  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤ │
  │  │  OnWorkflowSuccess            Delete all pods only on       │ │
  │  │                               Workflow Success              │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─── WORKFLOW EXECUTION ──────────────────────────────────┐     │
  │  │                                                         │     │
  │  │  Pending → Running → lint (pod) → test (3 pods ⚡)       │     │
  │  │                                    ├─test-310            │     │
  │  │                                    ├─test-311            │     │
  │  │                                    └─test-312            │     │
  │  │                                             → build (pod)│     │
  │  │  OnWorkflowCompletion: ALL 5 pods deleted together ✅     │     │
  │  │  OnPodCompletion:      Each pod deleted as it finishes   │     │
  │  │                                                         │     │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘     │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ⚠️  Without podGC, pods accumulate and can overwhelm the       │
  │     Kubernetes API server with etcd storage and LIST calls       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: In production CI pipelines like anihpj/jobpost, you may run 50+ workflows per hour. Without podGC, thousands of completed pods accumulate, increasing etcd size and slowing kubectl get pods. OnWorkflowCompletion is the recommended strategy for CI pipelines. For debugging failed workflows, use OnWorkflowSuccess so failed pods are preserved for log analysis.

Argo Workflows Q6 of 200
How can you pass dynamic input values to an Argo Workflow at submission time, so the same Workflow YAML can be reused with different inputs across multiple runs?
A) Edit the Workflow YAML file each time before running argo submit — this is the only way to change inputs
B) Define spec.arguments.parameters in the Workflow and pass values at submission with argo submit -p key=value
C) Set environment variables like export WF_PARAM=value on the CLI before calling argo submit
D) Reference a ConfigMap in the template's envFrom field — the Workflow reads parameters from Kubernetes
✅ Correct Answer: B

Workflow parameters are defined in spec.arguments.parameters[] and referenced inside templates using the substitution syntax {{workflow.parameters.name}}. At submission time, you pass values with argo submit workflow.yaml -p key=value — no YAML editing needed.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Workflow Parameters — Dynamic Input at Submission Time
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Workflow YAML (same file, reused)                               │
  │                                                                  │
  │  apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1                                │
  │  kind: Workflow                                                  │
  │  metadata:                                                       │
  │    generateName: anihpj-ci-                                      │
  │  spec:                                                           │
  │    ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗  │
  │    ║  arguments:                      ← Define parameters    ║  │
  │    ║    parameters:                        here              ║  │
  │    ║    - name: repo-ref                                     ║  │
  │    ║      value: main                  ← Default value       ║  │
  │    ║    - name: python-version                               ║  │
  │    ║      value: "3.11"                                      ║  │
  │    ║    - name: run-tests                                    ║  │
  │    ║      value: "true"                                      ║  │
  │    ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝  │
  │                                                                  │
  │    entrypoint: ci-pipeline                                       │
  │    templates:                                                    │
  │    - name: ci-pipeline                                           │
  │      steps:                                                      │
  │      - - name: checkout                                          │
  │          template: git-clone                                     │
  │          arguments:                                              │
  │            parameters:                                           │
  │            - name: ref                                           │
  │              value: "{{workflow.parameters.repo-ref}}"           │
  │                                    ↑                             │
  │                    Template references Workflow-level parameter  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ──────────────────── SUBMISSION ────────────────────────────    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  $ argo submit workflow.yaml \                                   │
  │      -p repo-ref=feature/login \                                 │
  │      -p python-version=3.12 \                                    │
  │      -p run-tests=true                                           │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ✅ Same YAML, different inputs every run!                       │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Template-level parameters (for individual templates):           │
  │  {{inputs.parameters.name}}  ← passed via steps arguments       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Parameters are the foundation of reusable Workflows. Without them, you'd need a separate YAML for every branch, Python version, and test configuration. In anihpj/jobpost, the same CI Workflow runs against main, feature/login, and hotfix/db branches — only the repo-ref parameter changes. The {{workflow.parameters.name}} syntax is frequently tested.

Argo Workflows Q7 of 200
Argo Workflows steps run in isolated containers. How do you pass files (like test reports, build artifacts, or coverage data) from one step to another?
A) Use Artifacts — define outputs.artifacts in one template and inputs.artifacts in another, backed by storage like S3, GCS, or a PersistentVolumeClaim
B) Write files to a shared emptyDir volume mounted in both steps — the Workflow controller handles the rest
C) Use kubectl cp between the pods — Argo Workflows provides no native file-passing mechanism
D) Store data in the Workflow CRD's status.outputs field — subsequent steps read from there
✅ Correct Answer: A

Artifacts are Argo Workflows' native mechanism for passing files between steps. A producing step declares outputs.artifacts with a name, path (where the file is inside the container), and destination (S3 bucket, GCS, or PVC). A consuming step declares inputs.artifacts with the same name — Argo handles the transfer transparently.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Artifact Passing — How Steps Share Files
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  STEP A: run-tests (PRODUCER)                                    │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐     │
  │  │  container: python:3.11                                 │     │
  │  │  command: [pytest, --junitxml=/tmp/report.xml]          │     │
  │  │                                                         │     │
  │  │  outputs:                                               │     │
  │  │    artifacts:                                           │     │
  │  │    - name: test-report       ← Name used by consumer    │     │
  │  │      path: /tmp/report.xml   ← File INSIDE container    │     │
  │  │      s3:                     ← Where to store it        │     │
  │  │        key: reports/{{workflow.name}}/report.xml        │     │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘     │
  │                          │                                       │
  │                          │  Argo copies to S3 automatically      │
  │                          ▼                                       │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐     │
  │  │                 S3 / GCS / PVC                           │     │
  │  │            reports/my-wf-abc/report.xml                  │     │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘     │
  │                          │                                       │
  │                          │  Argo downloads from S3               │
  │                          ▼                                       │
  │  STEP B: upload-results (CONSUMER)                               │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐     │
  │  │  inputs:                                                │     │
  │  │    artifacts:                                           │     │
  │  │    - name: test-report       ← Same name as producer    │     │
  │  │      path: /tmp/incoming.xml ← Where to PUT the file    │     │
  │  │                                                         │     │
  │  │  container: amazon/aws-cli                               │     │
  │  │  command: [aws, s3, cp, /tmp/incoming.xml, ...]         │     │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘     │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Supported artifact storage:                                     │
  │  • S3 (AWS)     • GCS (Google)    • Azure Blob                   │
  │  • PVC          • HTTP            • Git (clone repo as artifact) │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Artifacts are essential for CI/CD pipelines where each step produces output consumed by the next. In anihpj/jobpost: the test step produces a JUnit XML report (artifact), the coverage step produces an HTML coverage report (artifact), and the notify step consumes both to post results to Slack. Without artifacts, each step would need to re-run tests or use shared volumes.

Argo Workflows Q8 of 200
You need a cleanup step that runs regardless of whether the Workflow succeeds or fails — for example, deleting a temporary test database. Which Argo Workflows feature guarantees this?
A) Add the cleanup as the last step in your DAG — it always runs because it's the final step
B) Use a Kubernetes preStop hook in every template's container spec
C) Define an Exit Handler template — referenced by spec.onExit, it executes after the main workflow completes regardless of outcome
D) Wrap the entire Workflow in a try-catch block using spec.errorHandling
✅ Correct Answer: C

Exit Handlers (spec.onExit) are templates that ALWAYS execute when the main Workflow finishes — whether it Succeeded, Failed, or Errored. This is the only guaranteed way to run cleanup logic. A final DAG step won't run if an earlier step fails the entire DAG.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Exit Handler — Cleanup That ALWAYS Runs
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  spec:                                                           │
  │    entrypoint: main                                              │
  │    onExit: cleanup-db       ← Runs AFTER main, ALWAYS            │
  │                                                                  │
  │    templates:                                                    │
  │    - name: main                                                  │
  │      steps:                                                      │
  │      - - name: run-tests                                         │
  │          template: execute-tests                                 │
  │                                                                  │
  │    - name: cleanup-db        ← Exit handler template             │
  │      container:                                                  │
  │        image: postgres:15                                        │
  │        command: [psql, -c, "DROP DATABASE test_db"]              │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  SCENARIO                      DOES CLEANUP RUN?            │ │
  │  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤ │
  │  │  All tests pass ✅             ✅ cleanup-db runs            │ │
  │  │  Tests fail ❌                  ✅ cleanup-db runs            │ │
  │  │  Invalid template ref (Error)  ✅ cleanup-db runs            │ │
  │  │  Workflow manually terminated  ✅ cleanup-db runs            │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Exit handler receives workflow status:                          │
  │  {{workflow.status}} = Succeeded / Failed / Error                │
  │  Use this to send different notifications per outcome            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Exit handlers are commonly tested because they solve a critical problem: resource leaks. In anihpj/jobpost CI, every test run creates a temporary PostgreSQL database. Without an exit handler, failed test runs leave orphaned databases consuming cluster resources. The exit handler cleanup-db drops the temp database whether tests pass or fail.

Argo Workflows Q9 of 200
Why would an organization choose Argo Workflows over traditional CI tools like Jenkins or GitHub Actions for running their CI/CD pipelines?
A) Argo Workflows runs each step as an isolated Kubernetes pod with native resource management, scaling, and artifact passing — no external CI server needed
B) Argo Workflows has a built-in web UI that Jenkins and GitHub Actions lack
C) Argo Workflows is faster because it bypasses the Kubernetes scheduler and runs containers directly on the node
D) Argo Workflows replaces the need for Dockerfiles — you define container behavior directly in the Workflow YAML
✅ Correct Answer: A

Argo Workflows is Kubernetes-native: every step runs as a pod with its own resource limits, service account, and isolation. No separate CI server infrastructure is needed — your Kubernetes cluster IS the CI engine. Artifacts, parameters, retry strategies, and parallelism are all first-class YAML declarations.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Jenkins vs Argo Workflows — Architecture Comparison
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  TRADITIONAL CI (Jenkins/GitHub Actions)                         │
  │  ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ Git Push │───▶│ CI Server│───▶│ CI Server manages runners │   │
  │  │          │    │ (VM/EC2) │    │ • Install dependencies    │   │
  │  └──────────┘    └──────────┘    │ • Run tests               │   │
  │                                  │ • Build images            │   │
  │  ❌ CI server is a pet           │ • Push to registry        │   │
  │  ❌ Scaling = bigger VMs         └──────────────────────────┘   │
  │  ❌ Resource contention                                          │
  │                                                                  │
  │  KUBERNETES-NATIVE CI (Argo Workflows)                           │
  │  ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ Git Push │───▶│        Kubernetes Cluster                │   │
  │  │          │    │  ┌─────┐  ┌──────┐  ┌──────┐  ┌──────┐  │   │
  │  └──────────┘    │  │lint │  │test  │  │test  │  │build │  │   │
  │                  │  │pod  │  │310   │  │311   │  │pod   │  │   │
  │  ✅ No CI server │  └─────┘  └──────┘  └──────┘  └──────┘  │   │
  │  ✅ Each step =  │        ↑ Each step is a pod ↑            │   │
  │     isolated pod │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐│   │
  │  ✅ Native K8s   │  │  Argo Workflows Controller           ││   │
  │     scheduling   │  │  • Creates pods per step             ││   │
  │  ✅ Resource     │  │  • Manages DAG/Steps flow            ││   │
  │     limits       │  │  • Handles artifacts, retries        ││   │
  │                  │  └──────────────────────────────────────┘│   │
  │                  └──────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: The CAPA exam tests your understanding of WHY Argo is better for Kubernetes environments. Key advantages: no external CI server to maintain, each step gets its own pod (resource isolation), native Kubernetes scheduling (bin-packing), and seamless integration with ArgoCD for GitOps deployment after CI completes.

Argo Workflows Q10 of 200
You are designing the CI pipeline for anihpj/jobpost (Django + PostgreSQL + Redis). The pipeline must: lint code, run tests on Python 3.10/3.11/3.12 in parallel, build a Docker image only if all tests pass, then push to ECR. Which Argo Workflows pattern achieves this?
A) A single DAG template where all steps (lint, test, build, push) run in parallel with no dependencies
B) A Steps template with 4 sequential inner lists: [[lint], [test], [build], [push]]
C) A Steps template: [[lint], [test-310, test-311, test-312], [build], [push]] — lint first, tests in parallel, build after all tests, push after build
D) Four separate Workflows submitted sequentially with argo submit --wait between each
✅ Correct Answer: C

The Steps template with nested lists is the correct pattern: [[lint], [test-310, test-311, test-312], [build], [push]]. Each inner list runs its steps in parallel — all 3 Python versions test simultaneously. Inner lists execute sequentially — build waits for ALL tests, push waits for build.

📝 Detailed Explanation
anihpj/jobpost CI Pipeline — End-to-End Workflow
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  anihpj/jobpost CI Workflow                                      │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─────────┐                                                     │
  │  │  LINT   │  flake8 + black --check (1 pod)                    │
  │  └────┬────┘                                                     │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐                 │
  │  │           TEST MATRIX (3 pods ⚡)            │                 │
  │  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  │                 │
  │  │  │ Python   │  │ Python   │  │ Python   │  │                 │
  │  │  │  3.10    │  │  3.11    │  │  3.12    │  │                 │
  │  │  │ pytest   │  │ pytest   │  │ pytest   │  │                 │
  │  │  │ --cov    │  │ --cov    │  │ --cov    │  │                 │
  │  │  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  │                 │
  │  └──────────────────────┬──────────────────────┘                 │
  │                         │ ALL must pass                           │
  │                         ▼                                         │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐                 │
  │  │              BUILD (1 pod)                   │                 │
  │  │  docker build -t anihpj/jobpost:$TAG .      │                 │
  │  │  (Multi-stage distroless Dockerfile)         │                 │
  │  └──────────────────────┬──────────────────────┘                 │
  │                         │                                         │
  │                         ▼                                         │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐                 │
  │  │              PUSH (1 pod)                    │                 │
  │  │  aws ecr get-login && docker push            │                 │
  │  │  → 123456789.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com │                 │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────┘                 │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Total: 6 pods (lint + 3 tests + build + push)                   │
  │  Steps YAML: [[lint], [test-310, test-311, test-312],            │
  │               [build], [push]]                                   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Q1 taught Templates, Q2 taught entrypoint, Q3 taught lifecycle, Q4 taught Steps vs DAG, Q5 taught pod cleanup, Q6 taught parameters, Q7 taught artifacts, Q8 taught exit handlers — and Q10 shows how they all work together in a real CI pipeline for anihpj/jobpost. The same pattern applies to any application stack.

1.2 Workflow Templates — Types & UsageQ11-Q18

Argo Workflows Q11 of 200
Argo Workflows supports multiple template types for different execution needs. Which of the following is NOT a valid built-in template type?
A) Container — runs a container with image, command, args
B) Script — runs an inline script inside a container
C) Service — deploys a long-running Kubernetes Service for the workflow duration
D) Suspend — pauses the workflow for a duration or until manually resumed
✅ Correct Answer: C

Argo Workflows has 6 built-in template types: Container, Script, Resource, Suspend, Steps, and DAG. There is no Service template type — if you need a long-running service, you'd use a Resource template to create a Kubernetes Service manifest.

📝 Detailed Explanation
6 Built-in Template Types
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  TEMPLATE TYPE      PURPOSE                                      │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  🐳 Container       Run a container (image, cmd, args, env)      │
  │                     Most common — every CI step uses this        │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  📜 Script           Run an inline script (source: inline string) │
  │                     Python/shell/boto3 scripts in YAML           │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  📦 Resource         Create/Get/Delete K8s resources directly    │
  │                     kubectl apply inside a workflow step         │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  ⏸️  Suspend          Pause workflow (duration or manual resume)  │
  │                     Wait for approval before deploying to prod   │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  🪜 Steps            Orchestrate templates in nested lists       │
  │                     [[A], [B, C]] → A then B∥C then next         │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  🔀 DAG              Orchestrate with explicit dependencies      │
  │                     tasks: [{name:A}, {name:B, deps:[A]}]        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Knowing which template type to use is fundamental. Container for simple steps, Script for inline logic (no Dockerfile needed), Resource for managing Kubernetes objects within a workflow, Suspend for manual approval gates, Steps/DAG for orchestrating multiple templates. The exam expects you to select the right type for a given scenario.

Argo Workflows Q12 of 200
You need to run flake8 linting on your Python code as part of a CI workflow. Which template configuration correctly defines this step?
A) A Script template with command: [flake8] — scripts can run any CLI tool
B) A Container template with image: python:3.11 and command: [flake8, .]
C) A Resource template that creates a Job to run flake8
D) A Steps template with a single inner list containing flake8
✅ Correct Answer: B

A Container template is the correct choice for running a CLI tool like flake8. You specify the container image that has flake8 installed and the command to execute. The container runs to completion, and its exit code determines success/failure.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Container Template Anatomy
  - name: lint-code
    container:                    ← Template type
      image: python:3.11          ← Which image to run
      command: [flake8]           ← Entrypoint override
      args: [".", "--max-line-length=120"]   ← Arguments
      env:                        ← Environment variables
      - name: PYTHONPATH
        value: /app
      resources:                  ← CPU/Memory limits
        requests:
          memory: 512Mi
          cpu: 250m
        limits:
          memory: 1Gi
          cpu: 500m
      volumeMounts:               ← Mount shared volumes
      - name: workdir
        mountPath: /app

  The container runs ONCE, produces exit code 0 (success) or non-zero (fail).
  Argo marks the step Succeeded or Failed based on the exit code.

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Container templates are the most common type. Key fields to know: image (required), command (overrides ENTRYPOINT), args (overrides CMD), env, resources, volumeMounts. In anihpj/jobpost CI, lint, test, build, and push steps all use Container templates with different images.

Argo Workflows Q13 of 200
You need to run a Python script that processes test results and posts them to Slack — but you don't want to build a custom Docker image for this 10-line script. Which template type lets you embed the script directly in the Workflow YAML?
A) Script template — write the script inline in the source field, and it runs inside the specified image
B) Container template with command: [python3, -c, "script code here"]
C) Resource template — create a ConfigMap with the script, then mount it
D) Suspend template — pause the workflow while you manually run the script
✅ Correct Answer: A

The Script template wraps a Container template but adds a source field where you write your script inline. Argo automatically mounts it into the container and executes it. No Dockerfile, no image build — just write the script in YAML.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Script Template — Inline Code, No Docker Build
  - name: notify-slack
    script:                       ← Template type
      image: python:3.11          ← Runtime image
      source: |                   ← INLINE script (no Dockerfile!)
        import requests
        import json
        
        # Read test results
        with open("/tmp/report.xml") as f:
            test_count = f.read().count('testcase')
        
        # Post to Slack
        webhook = "{{workflow.parameters.slack-webhook}}"
        msg = {"text": f"Tests completed: {test_count} test cases"}
        requests.post(webhook, json=msg)
        
        print("✅ Slack notification sent")
      
      resources:                  ← Same as Container template
        requests:
          memory: 256Mi
          cpu: 100m

  💡 Script template = Container template + source: | (inline code)
  Perfect for: glue logic, API calls, data transforms, notifications
  Not for: complex apps (build a Docker image instead)

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Script templates are heavily tested because they're unique to Argo. Key difference from Container: Script adds source: field and runs the code through a wrapper. You can use {{workflow.parameters.name}} inside the script for parameterized logic. In anihpj/jobpost, the Slack notification step uses a Script template to avoid maintaining a separate notification image.

Argo Workflows Q14 of 200
You need your workflow to create a Kubernetes Secret, then a Deployment that references it. Which template type lets you manage Kubernetes resources directly from a workflow step?
A) Container template running bitnami/kubectl image with kubectl apply commands
B) Script template using the Kubernetes Python client library
C) DAG template with a special createResource task type
D) Resource template — specify the action (create/apply/delete) and the manifest directly in the template
✅ Correct Answer: D

The Resource template directly interacts with Kubernetes resources. You specify an action (create, apply, delete, get, patch) and the manifest (the YAML of the resource). Argo executes the action and waits for the resource to reach the desired state.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Resource Template — Manage K8s Objects Natively
  - name: create-db-secret
    resource:                     ← Template type
      action: create              ← create | apply | delete | get | patch
      manifest: |                 ← The Kubernetes resource YAML
        apiVersion: v1
        kind: Secret
        metadata:
          name: db-credentials
        type: Opaque
        stringData:
          username: admin
          password: "{{workflow.parameters.db-password}}"
      
      successCondition: status.phase == Active
      failureCondition: status.phase == Failed

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SUPPORTED ACTIONS                                               │
  │  • create  — kubectl create (fails if exists)                    │
  │  • apply   — kubectl apply (create or update)                    │
  │  • delete  — kubectl delete                                      │
  │  • get     — kubectl get -o json (for status checking)           │
  │  • patch   — kubectl patch (partial update)                      │
  │                                                                  │
  │  💡 Resource template vs Container running kubectl:              │
  │  Resource template is DECLARATIVE — Argo handles retries,        │
  │  waits for resource readiness, and provides structured output    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Resource templates eliminate the need for kubectl in a container. They're safer (no kubectl binary, no kubeconfig management), declarative (Argo handles retries and waiting), and provide structured outputs. In anihpj/jobpost, the CI workflow uses a Resource template to create a test database Secret before running tests, and deletes it via an exit handler.

Argo Workflows Q15 of 200
Your CI pipeline needs a manual approval step before deploying to production. The workflow should wait until a human clicks "approve." Which template type implements this gate?
A) Container template running sleep infinity — it waits indefinitely until manually deleted
B) Suspend template — pauses the workflow for a duration or until manually resumed via argo resume
C) Resource template that creates a Pending approval CRD and polls for approval
D) DAG template with a conditional task that checks an external approval API
✅ Correct Answer: B

The Suspend template pauses workflow execution. You can set a fixed duration (e.g., "24h") or omit it for indefinite suspension — the workflow waits until someone runs argo resume <workflow>. This is the native Argo mechanism for approval gates.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Suspend Template — Manual Approval Gate
  - name: await-approval
    suspend:                      ← Template type
      duration: "24h"             ← Auto-resume after 24h (optional)

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WORKFLOW EXECUTION                                              │
  │                                                                  │
  │  lint ──▶ test ──▶ build ──▶ [⏸️ AWAIT APPROVAL] ──▶ deploy     │
  │                                  │                               │
  │                                  │  argo resume my-wf            │
  │                                  │  (human clicks approve)       │
  │                                  ▼                               │
  │                               deploy-to-prod                     │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Two modes:                                                      │
  │  1. duration: "24h"  → Auto-resumes after 24 hours               │
  │     Use: "Deploy if no one objects within 24h"                   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  2. No duration       → Waits FOREVER for manual resume          │
  │     Use: "Explicit approval required before production deploy"   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  $ argo resume anihpj-deploy-abc123                             │
  │  $ argo terminate anihpj-deploy-abc123   ← Cancel the workflow   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Suspend templates are the Argo-native way to implement change control. Combine with exit handlers: if the suspend times out, the exit handler can send a Slack notification and clean up. In anihpj/jobpost, a Suspend template gates the production deployment — the CI pipeline builds and tests automatically, but deployment to prod requires a human to run argo resume.

Argo Workflows Q16 of 200
You have a test template that multiple workflows need to reuse. Instead of copying the template YAML into every Workflow, what Argo CRD allows you to define a template once and reference it across workflows?
A) ConfigMap — store the template YAML and mount it as a volume
B) Template CRD — the built-in Kubernetes resource for reusable templates
C) Workflow CRD — reference another Workflow's spec.templates directly
D) WorkflowTemplate CRD — namespace-scoped reusable template referenced via templateRef
✅ Correct Answer: D

WorkflowTemplate is a namespace-scoped CRD that holds reusable templates. Workflows reference it using templateRef: {name: my-template, template: test-step}. This is the DRY (Don't Repeat Yourself) mechanism for Argo Workflows.

📝 Detailed Explanation
WorkflowTemplate — Define Once, Reuse Everywhere
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WorkflowTemplate (defined once per namespace)                    │
  │  apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1                                │
  │  kind: WorkflowTemplate                                          │
  │  metadata:                                                       │
  │    name: shared-templates                                        │
  │  spec:                                                           │
  │    templates:                                                    │
  │    - name: python-test       ← Reusable template                 │
  │      inputs:                                                     │
  │        parameters:                                               │
  │        - name: python-version                                    │
  │      container:                                                  │
  │        image: python:{{inputs.parameters.python-version}}        │
  │        command: [pytest, --cov]                                  │
  │                                                                  │
  │    - name: docker-build      ← Another reusable template         │
  │      container:                                                  │
  │        image: docker:24                                          │
  │        command: [docker, build, -t, "{{inputs.parameters.tag}}"] │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ─────────────── WORKFLOWS REFERENCE IT ──────────────────────   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Workflow A (CI for main):       Workflow B (CI for PR):         │
  │  - name: test-311                - name: test-312                │
  │    templateRef:                    templateRef:                  │
  │      name: shared-templates         name: shared-templates       │
  │      template: python-test          template: python-test        │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ✅ One definition → used in 100+ workflows                      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: WorkflowTemplate is essential for platform teams. In anihpj/jobpost, the python-test, docker-build, and ecr-push templates are defined once in a WorkflowTemplate and referenced by CI workflows for every branch. If you need to update the test command, you edit ONE WorkflowTemplate — all workflows pick up the change.

Argo Workflows Q17 of 200
You want a set of CI templates (lint, test, build) available to ALL namespaces in the cluster, not just one namespace. Which CRD provides cluster-wide reusable templates?
A) ClusterWorkflowTemplate — cluster-scoped, accessible from any namespace via templateRef with clusterScope: true
B) WorkflowTemplate in the kube-system namespace — accessible cluster-wide by convention
C) GlobalTemplate CRD — the cluster-scoped equivalent of WorkflowTemplate
D) Copy the WorkflowTemplate to every namespace — there is no cluster-scoped option
✅ Correct Answer: A

ClusterWorkflowTemplate is identical to WorkflowTemplate but cluster-scoped. To reference it from a Workflow, add clusterScope: true to the templateRef: {name: ci-templates, template: lint, clusterScope: true}.

📝 Detailed Explanation
WorkflowTemplate vs ClusterWorkflowTemplate
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WorkflowTemplate (namespace-scoped)                             │
  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐                       │
  │  │  ns: dev │  │ ns: stg  │  │ ns: prod │                       │
  │  │  WFT: ci │  │  WFT: ci │  │  WFT: ci │  ← Copied 3 times!   │
  │  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘                       │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ClusterWorkflowTemplate (cluster-scoped)                        │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐       │
  │  │              ClusterWFT: ci-templates                 │       │
  │  │         (defined once, usable from any namespace)     │       │
  │  └────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┘       │
  │       ┌───────────────┼───────────────┐                         │
  │       ▼               ▼               ▼                         │
  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐                       │
  │  │  ns: dev │  │ ns: stg  │  │ ns: prod │                       │
  │  │refs: CWFT│  │refs: CWFT│  │refs: CWFT│  ← All share 1 def   │
  │  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘                       │
  │                                                                  │
  │  templateRef:                                                    │
  │    name: ci-templates                                            │
  │    template: python-test                                         │
  │    clusterScope: true        ← THIS makes it cluster-scoped      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: ClusterWorkflowTemplate is the platform team's tool for standardizing CI/CD across all teams. In a multi-tenant cluster running anihpj/jobpost, the platform team defines lint/test/build templates once as ClusterWorkflowTemplates, and all dev teams reference them without duplication. The clusterScope: true flag is frequently tested.

Argo Workflows Q18 of 200
A WorkflowTemplate defines a steps template named full-ci that orchestrates lint, test, and build. How does a Workflow invoke this entire orchestrated template?
A) Copy the steps YAML into the Workflow — WorkflowTemplate can only be used for individual container templates, not orchestration templates
B) Set spec.entrypoint to reference the WorkflowTemplate: entrypoint: full-ci and add a workflowTemplateRef at the spec level pointing to the WorkflowTemplate
C) Use templateRef inside the Workflow's spec.templates list, referencing {name: ci-wft, template: full-ci}
D) Submit the WorkflowTemplate directly with argo submit workflowtemplate/ci-wft
✅ Correct Answer: B

To use a WorkflowTemplate as the entrypoint of an entire Workflow, you set spec.workflowTemplateRef at the Workflow level and point spec.entrypoint to the template name inside the WorkflowTemplate. This is different from referencing individual templates via steps-level templateRef (which is for composing templates within a workflow).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Template Composition — Two Ways to Reference Templates
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  METHOD 1: Workflow-level reference (entire workflow = WFT)      │
  │                                                                  │
  │  apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1                                │
  │  kind: Workflow                                                  │
  │  spec:                                                           │
  │    workflowTemplateRef:           ← Reference the whole WFT      │
  │      name: ci-pipeline-templates                                 │
  │    entrypoint: full-ci            ← Template INSIDE that WFT     │
  │    arguments:                                                   │
  │      parameters:                                                │
  │      - name: branch                                              │
  │        value: main                                               │
  │                                                                  │
  │  METHOD 2: Step-level reference (compose templates)              │
  │                                                                  │
  │  spec:                                                           │
  │    entrypoint: my-pipeline                                       │
  │    templates:                                                    │
  │    - name: my-pipeline                                           │
  │      steps:                                                      │
  │      - - name: lint                                              │
  │          templateRef:              ← Reference ONE template      │
  │            name: shared-templates                                │
  │            template: lint-code                                   │
  │        - name: test                                              │
  │          templateRef:                                            │
  │            name: shared-templates                                │
  │            template: run-tests                                   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: The distinction between workflowTemplateRef (entire workflow IS a WFT) and step-level templateRef (composing individual templates from a WFT) is a common exam topic. In anihpj/jobpost, the workflowTemplateRef approach is used so that every branch CI workflow is just a thin wrapper referencing the full CI pipeline template.

1.3 Generating & Consuming ArtifactsQ19-Q24

Argo Workflows Q19 of 200
Which statement best describes what an Artifact is in Argo Workflows?
A) Artifacts are Kubernetes ConfigMaps that store workflow metadata between steps
B) Artifacts are environment variables passed between containers using the workflow controller
C) Artifacts are Docker images built during the workflow and cached for subsequent steps
D) Artifacts are files or directories produced by one step and consumed by another, stored in external storage (S3, GCS, PVC) and managed transparently by Argo
✅ Correct Answer: D

Artifacts are Argo's mechanism for file-level data passing between steps. A producer step writes files to a path and declares them as outputs.artifacts. Argo uploads them to configured storage. A consumer step declares inputs.artifacts with the same name — Argo downloads the files to the specified path before the step runs.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Artifacts — Files That Flow Between Steps
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Step A: GENERATE                           Step B: CONSUME      │
  │  ┌────────────────────────┐              ┌────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ pytest --junitxml=     │              │ junit2html \       │  │
  │  │   /tmp/report.xml      │              │   /tmp/report.xml \│  │
  │  │                        │              │   > report.html    │  │
  │  │ outputs:               │              │                    │  │
  │  │   artifacts:           │              │ inputs:            │  │
  │  │   - name: test-report  │──────────────│   artifacts:       │  │
  │  │     path: /tmp/report  │   ┌──────┐   │   - name: test-rpt │  │
  │  │     s3:                │   │ S3   │   │     path: /tmp/    │  │
  │  │       key: rpt.xml     │   │bucket│   │                    │  │
  │  └────────────────────────┘   └──────┘   └────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Storage backends: S3 | GCS | Azure Blob | PVC | HTTP | Git     │
  │  Default artifact repo: configured in workflow-controller ConfigMap │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Artifacts are the ONLY way to pass files between steps (parameters pass small values). Artifact repositories must be pre-configured. In anihpj/jobpost, test reports, coverage HTML, and build logs are all passed as artifacts between CI steps.

Argo Workflows Q20 of 200
A step runs pytest --cov --cov-report=xml:/tmp/coverage.xml. How do you make the coverage XML file available to the next step?
A) Declare outputs.artifacts with name: coverage, path: /tmp/coverage.xml and a storage destination (S3/GCS/PVC)
B) Write the file path to /tmp/argo/outputs/coverage — Argo auto-detects and uploads it
C) Add outputs.parameters: [{name: coverage-path, value: /tmp/coverage.xml}] — parameters can pass file paths
D) Mount a shared emptyDir volume — all steps in the same workflow share the same filesystem automatically
✅ Correct Answer: A

To produce an artifact, you explicitly declare it in outputs.artifacts with a name (used by the consumer), path (where the file is inside the container), and a storage destination. Argo does NOT auto-detect files — you must declare them.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Output Artifact Declaration
  - name: run-tests-with-coverage
    container:
      image: python:3.11
      command: [pytest]
      args: ["--cov", "--cov-report=xml:/tmp/coverage.xml"]
    outputs:
      artifacts:
      - name: coverage-report           ← Consumer references this name
        path: /tmp/coverage.xml         ← File inside the container
        s3:                             ← Where to store it
          endpoint: s3.amazonaws.com
          bucket: anihpj-artifacts
          key: coverage/{{workflow.name}}/coverage.xml
          accessKeySecret:              ← Credentials via K8s Secret
            name: s3-credentials
            key: accessKey

  ⚠️  Common mistake: Forgetting to declare outputs.artifacts.
      The file exists in the container but Argo won't upload it
      unless you explicitly declare it.

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Explicit artifact declaration is a core concept. The exam tests whether you know to use outputs.artifacts (not parameters) for files. Credentials for S3/GCS are passed via Kubernetes Secrets referenced in the artifact config.

Argo Workflows Q21 of 200
A downstream step needs the coverage report produced by the test step. How does the consuming step access the artifact?
A) Access it via {{artifacts.coverage-report}} in the container command
B) The file is automatically available at the same path in every subsequent step
C) Declare inputs.artifacts with the same name as the producer and specify a path where Argo will place the downloaded file
D) Use argo artifacts get coverage-report in a Script template
✅ Correct Answer: C

To consume an artifact, declare inputs.artifacts with the matching name and a path where Argo should place the downloaded file before the step executes. Argo handles the download transparently from the configured artifact repository.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Input Artifact — Consumer Declaration
  - name: upload-coverage
    inputs:
      artifacts:
      - name: coverage-report           ← MATCHES producer's name
        path: /tmp/incoming/coverage.xml ← Where Argo puts the file
    container:
      image: amazon/aws-cli
      command: [aws, s3, cp]
      args: [/tmp/incoming/coverage.xml, s3://reports/coverage/]

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Artifact Flow:                                                  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Producer                          Consumer                      │
  │  outputs.artifacts:                inputs.artifacts:             │
  │  - name: coverage-report           - name: coverage-report      │
  │    path: /tmp/cov.xml       → S3 →   path: /tmp/incoming/       │
  │                                                                  │
  │  1. Producer writes /tmp/cov.xml                                 │
  │  2. Argo uploads to S3                                           │
  │  3. Argo downloads from S3 to consumer's /tmp/incoming/          │
  │  4. Consumer reads /tmp/incoming/coverage.xml                    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: The name matching between producer outputs and consumer inputs must be EXACT. Mismatched names are a common source of workflow failures. The path in the consumer can be different from the producer — Argo only cares about the name for matching.

Argo Workflows Q22 of 200
You are running Argo Workflows on-premises without cloud storage. Which artifact storage backend can you use that requires no external services?
A) S3-compatible storage — you must run MinIO or equivalent, there is no built-in option
B) PersistentVolumeClaim (PVC) — artifacts are stored on a Kubernetes PVC, no external service needed
C) hostPath volumes — artifacts are saved to the node's local disk
D) emptyDir — artifacts are stored in memory shared between pods on the same node
✅ Correct Answer: B

A PVC (PersistentVolumeClaim) can serve as an artifact repository. It requires no external services — the PVC is backed by your cluster's storage provisioner. This is the simplest on-premises artifact storage option.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Artifact Storage Backends
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  BACKEND         CONFIG                          USE CASE         │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  S3              endpoint + bucket + region      AWS (production) │
  │  GCS             bucket + serviceAccountKey      GCP (production) │
  │  Azure Blob      container + accountKey          Azure (prod)     │
  │  PVC             name: my-pvc                    On-prem / local  │
  │  HTTP            url: https://files.example.com  External server  │
  │  Git             repo + revision                 Clone repos      │
  │                                                                  │
  │  PVC Artifact Config:                                            │
  │  artifacts:                                                      │
  │  - name: test-report                                             │
  │    path: /tmp/report.xml                                         │
  │    pvc:                                                          │
  │      name: artifact-storage   ← Pre-created PVC                  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  💡 PVC artifacts stored at: /artifacts/{namespace}/{wf-name}/   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: The exam tests your knowledge of artifact backends. PVC is the correct answer for on-premises/minikube/kind setups. For anihpj/jobpost running on EKS, S3 is used. For your 6-cluster lab, PVC is the simplest option.

Argo Workflows Q23 of 200
Completed workflows leave artifacts in storage (S3/GCS/PVC). What mechanism cleans up these artifacts to prevent indefinite storage growth?
A) Artifact GC — configure artifactGC in the Workflow spec with a strategy (OnWorkflowCompletion/OnWorkflowDeletion) to delete artifacts when the workflow finishes or is deleted
B) S3 lifecycle policies — Argo has no built-in cleanup; you must configure cloud storage lifecycle rules
C) podGC strategy also applies to artifacts — the same setting cleans up both pods and artifacts
D) Artifacts are automatically deleted 24 hours after the workflow completes
✅ Correct Answer: A

Artifact GC (spec.artifactGC) is a separate strategy from Pod GC. It controls when artifacts stored in the artifact repository are deleted. OnWorkflowCompletion deletes artifacts when the workflow finishes; OnWorkflowDeletion deletes when the Workflow CRD is removed.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Artifact GC Strategies
  spec:
    artifactGC:
      strategy: OnWorkflowCompletion    ← Delete artifacts when done

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  STRATEGY                       BEHAVIOR                          │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  OnWorkflowCompletion           Delete all artifacts when WF      │
  │                                 reaches Success/Fail/Error        │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  OnWorkflowDeletion             Delete artifacts when the WF      │
  │                                 CRD is deleted from Kubernetes     │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Never (default)                Artifacts persist indefinitely    │
  │                                 until manually deleted            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ⚠️  WITHOUT artifactGC, your S3 bucket/PVC fills up over time
      with test reports, build logs, coverage data from every run

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Artifact GC is tested alongside Pod GC — know the difference. Pod GC cleans up pods; Artifact GC cleans up files in storage. They are configured separately. For anihpj/jobpost CI running 50+ workflows/day, Artifact GC with OnWorkflowCompletion prevents S3 storage costs from ballooning.

Argo Workflows Q24 of 200
In the anihpj/jobpost CI pipeline, the test step produces a JUnit XML report. A subsequent report step must convert it to HTML and upload to S3. Which artifact chain achieves this?
A) Test step writes to /tmp/report.xml → Report step reads /tmp/report.xml — same path, no artifact declaration needed
B) Test step uses outputs.parameters with the file content as value → Report step reads the parameter
C) Test step declares outputs.artifacts: [{name: junit, path: /tmp/report.xml}] → Report step declares inputs.artifacts: [{name: junit, path: /tmp/report.xml}] — Argo handles the transfer via S3/PVC
D) Test step pushes to S3 directly using AWS CLI → Report step pulls from S3 — Argo artifacts are bypassed
✅ Correct Answer: C

This is the canonical artifact chain: producer declares outputs.artifacts → Argo uploads to configured storage → consumer declares inputs.artifacts with matching name → Argo downloads to consumer's path → consumer processes the file. The name must match exactly; paths can differ.

📝 Detailed Explanation
anihpj/jobpost Artifact Chain — Test → Report → Upload
  STEP: run-tests                       STEP: generate-report
  ┌──────────────────────┐             ┌──────────────────────┐
  │ pytest --junitxml=   │             │ junit2html \         │
  │   /tmp/report.xml    │    ┌─────┐  │   /tmp/report.xml \  │
  │                      │    │ S3  │  │   > /tmp/report.html │
  │ outputs:             │    │ or  │  │                      │
  │   artifacts:         │───▶│ PVC │──▶ inputs:             │
  │   - name: junit      │    └─────┘  │   artifacts:         │
  │     path: /tmp/rpt   │             │   - name: junit      │
  │                      │             │     path: /tmp/rpt   │
  └──────────────────────┘             └──────────┬───────────┘
                                                  │
                                                  ▼
                                         STEP: upload-to-s3
                                         ┌──────────────────────┐
                                         │ inputs:              │
                                         │   artifacts:         │
                                         │   - name: html-rpt   │
                                         │     path: /tmp/html  │
                                         │ aws s3 cp \          │
                                         │   /tmp/html/rpt.html │
                                         │   s3://reports/      │
                                         └──────────────────────┘

  This chain demonstrates: produce → consume → produce → consume
  Each step adds value to the artifact pipeline

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: This is the real-world pattern tested on the exam. You'll be asked to wire up a multi-step artifact chain. Key points: names must match, paths are independent, storage backend is transparent to the steps, and each step can be both a consumer AND producer of artifacts.
