1.4 DAG — Dependency-Driven ExecutionQ25-Q30

Argo Workflows Q25 of 200
What is a DAG in Argo Workflows and what problem does it solve that Steps cannot easily address?
A) DAG is an alternative name for Steps — they are functionally identical
B) DAG is a template type where each task declares explicit dependencies on other tasks, enabling complex dependency graphs like fan-in/fan-out that would be awkward with nested Steps lists
C) DAG is a separate CRD that runs workflows faster by bypassing the Kubernetes scheduler
D) DAG stands for "Distributed Argo Groups" — it groups templates for resource optimization
✅ Correct Answer: B

A DAG (Directed Acyclic Graph) template defines tasks with explicit dependencies fields. Each task lists which other tasks must complete (and in what state) before it runs. This enables patterns like fan-in (multiple tasks → one merge), fan-out (one → many), and diamond dependencies that would be convoluted with Steps.

📝 Detailed Explanation
DAG — Explicit Dependency Graph
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DAG Example: Multi-language Build Pipeline                      │
  │                                                                  │
  │                  ┌─────────┐                                     │
  │                  │  lint   │                                     │
  │                  └────┬────┘                                     │
  │                       │                                          │
  │          ┌────────────┼────────────┐                             │
  │          ▼            ▼            ▼                             │
  │    ┌─────────┐ ┌─────────┐ ┌─────────┐                           │
  │    │test-py310│ │test-py311│ │test-py312│   ← Fan-out from lint  │
  │    └────┬─────┘ └────┬─────┘ └────┬─────┘                       │
  │         │             │            │                              │
  │         └─────────────┼────────────┘                              │
  │                       ▼                                          │
  │                 ┌─────────┐                                      │
  │                 │  merge  │  ← Fan-in from all 3 tests           │
  │                 └────┬────┘                                      │
  │                      │                                           │
  │                      ▼                                           │
  │                 ┌─────────┐                                      │
  │                 │  build  │                                      │
  │                 └─────────┘                                      │
  │                                                                  │
  │  YAML:                                                           │
  │  dag:                                                            │
  │    tasks:                                                        │
  │    - name: lint                                                  │
  │      template: lint-code                                         │
  │    - name: test-310                                              │
  │      dependencies: [lint]                                        │
  │      template: run-tests                                         │
  │    - name: merge                                                 │
  │      dependencies: [test-310, test-311, test-312]  ← Fan-in      │
  │      template: merge-results                                     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: DAG vs Steps is a core design decision. Steps = sequential phases with optional parallelism within a phase. DAG = arbitrary dependency graph. Use DAG when tasks have complex dependencies that don't fit into clean sequential phases.

Argo Workflows Q26 of 200
When should you choose DAG over Steps for orchestrating your workflow?
A) When tasks have non-sequential dependencies — e.g., task C depends on both A AND B, but A and B are independent. Steps would force A and B into the same inner list even if they're unrelated
B) When you need maximum parallelism — Steps cannot run tasks in parallel
C) When you have fewer than 3 tasks — DAG is simpler for small workflows
D) When you need to pass artifacts — DAG has better artifact support than Steps
✅ Correct Answer: A

DAG shines when tasks have non-sequential dependencies. In Steps, ALL tasks in an inner list run in parallel, and the next inner list waits for ALL of them. DAG lets you say "C depends on A and B" while A and B themselves have no relationship — they just both need to finish before C starts.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Steps vs DAG — When to Use Which
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  STEPS (Good for this):            DAG (Good for this):          │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Phase 1: [lint]                   ┌───┐                         │
  │  Phase 2: [test-py311, test-py312] │ A │──┐                      │
  │  Phase 3: [build]                  └───┘  │    ┌───┐             │
  │  Phase 4: [push]                          ├───▶│ C │             │
  │                                  ┌───┐    │    └───┘             │
  │  ✅ Clean sequential phases      │ B │──┘                       │
  │     with parallel within phase   └───┘                          │
  │                                                                  │
  │  RULE OF THUMB:                                                  │
  │  • Pipeline-like (lint→test→build)     → Steps                   │
  │  • Graph-like (complex dependencies)   → DAG                     │
  │  • Fan-in/fan-out patterns              → DAG                     │
  │  • Diamond dependencies (A→B→D, A→C→D) → DAG                     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: The exam often presents a dependency diagram and asks you to choose Steps or DAG. Key signal: if tasks have dependencies that skip phases (e.g., "D depends on A" where A is 3 phases back), use DAG.

Argo Workflows Q27 of 200
In a DAG, task C should run if task A succeeds OR task B is skipped. How do you express this conditional dependency?
A) dependencies: [A || B] — the double-pipe means OR
B) DAG dependencies are always AND — you cannot express OR conditions
C) Use when: "{{tasks.A.status}} == Succeeded || {{tasks.B.status}} == Skipped" on task C
D) dependencies: ["A.Succeeded || B.Skipped"] — state-qualified dependency with OR operator
✅ Correct Answer: D

DAG dependencies support state-qualified conditions: "taskName.State". States include Succeeded, Failed, Error, Skipped, Omitted, Daemoned. You can combine with || (OR) and && (AND): ["A.Succeeded || B.Skipped", "C.Succeeded"].

📝 Detailed Explanation
DAG Conditional Dependencies
  dag:
    tasks:
    - name: unit-tests
      template: pytest
    - name: integration-tests
      template: integration
    - name: deploy-staging
      dependencies:
        - "unit-tests.Succeeded || unit-tests.Skipped"
        - "integration-tests.Succeeded"
      template: deploy

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DEPENDENCY OPERATORS                                            │
  │  ||  = OR   (at least one condition met)                         │
  │  &&  = AND  (all conditions must be met)                         │
  │                                                                  │
  │  TASK STATES                                                     │
  │  .Succeeded  = Task completed successfully                       │
  │  .Failed     = Task failed                                       │
  │  .Error      = Controller-level error                            │
  │  .Skipped    = Task was skipped (when: condition false)          │
  │  .Omitted    = Task omitted (dep failed AND not .AnySucceeded)   │
  │  .Daemoned   = Daemon task running                               │
  │  .AnySucceeded = True if ANY parent succeeded (for fan-in)       │
  │  .AllFailed    = True if ALL parents failed                      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: State-qualified dependencies are a DAG-only feature. Steps has no equivalent — you can't express "run if A succeeded OR B was skipped" in Steps. This is frequently tested.

Argo Workflows Q28 of 200
In a DAG with tasks A, B, C, and D — if A and B have no dependencies declared, and C depends on A, and D depends on B — how do A, B, C, D execute?
A) A runs first, then B, then C, then D — alphabetical order within a DAG
B) All four run sequentially because DAG tasks without explicit ordering are serialized
C) A and B start immediately in parallel (no dependencies). C waits for A. D waits for B. C could finish before B, or D before A — only explicit dependencies constrain order
D) The workflow fails because DAG requires every task to have at least one dependency
✅ Correct Answer: C

DAG tasks with no dependencies (or empty dependencies: []) start immediately and run in parallel. Only explicitly declared dependencies constrain execution order. A and B start together; C waits for A; D waits for B. C and D may finish in any order.

📝 Detailed Explanation
DAG Parallelism — Only Dependencies Constrain
  Time ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────▶

  A ────────────┐
                │ (C waits for A)
  B ──────┐     └──── C ────
          │                  
          └──── D ────────── (D starts after B, finishes independently)

  • A and B start simultaneously (both have no dependencies)
  • C waits for A (dependency: [A])
  • D waits for B (dependency: [B])
  • C and D have no relationship — C could finish before D or vice versa
  • Maximum parallelism: 2 tasks at start (A, B), then up to 2 (C, D)

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Understanding that tasks with no dependencies run in parallel is critical. Many candidates assume DAG tasks run sequentially by default. The exam tests your ability to read a dependency graph and predict execution order and parallelism.

Argo Workflows Q29 of 200
You want a DAG task to run only if a previous step's output parameter equals "success". Which field controls conditional execution?
A) when: field on the task — accepts a boolean expression like "{{tasks.build.outputs.result}} == success". If false, the task is Skipped
B) condition: field — similar to Kubernetes Pod conditions
C) Conditional execution is only available in Steps, not DAG
D) dependencies: with a condition suffix like ["build?result=success"]
✅ Correct Answer: A

The when: field on a DAG task evaluates a boolean expression. If true, the task runs normally. If false, the task is marked Skipped. This is identical to the when: field in Steps — both template types support conditional execution.

📝 Detailed Explanation
DAG Conditional Execution with when:
  dag:
    tasks:
    - name: run-tests
      template: pytest
    - name: check-result
      template: analyze
    - name: deploy-prod
      dependencies: [run-tests, check-result]
      when: "{{tasks.run-tests.outputs.result}} == success && {{tasks.check-result.outputs.result}} == pass"
      template: deploy

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  when: EXPRESSIONS                                               │
  │                                                                  │
  │  "{{tasks.X.status}} == Succeeded"                               │
  │  "{{tasks.X.outputs.parameters.result}} == pass"                │
  │  "{{workflow.parameters.env}} == production"                    │
  │  "{{tasks.A.status}} != Failed && {{tasks.B.status}} != Error" │
  │                                                                  │
  │  When false: task status → Skipped (not Failed!)                 │
  │  Tasks depending on a Skipped task: use .Skipped || .Succeeded   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: when: is the primary conditional mechanism in both Steps and DAG. It's frequently combined with dependencies to create complex conditional pipelines. In anihpj/jobpost, the production deployment task has when: "{{workflow.parameters.env}} == production" — it only runs for the production environment.

Argo Workflows Q30 of 200
You need to process 100 customer records: split them into 10 batches, process each batch in parallel, then merge results. Which DAG pattern achieves this?
A) Sequential processing — DAG cannot do fan-out/fan-in, only Steps can
B) Fan-out/fan-in: one split task → 10 parallel process tasks (all depending on split) → one merge task (depending on ALL 10 process tasks via .AnySucceeded)
C) 10 separate DAGs submitted in parallel with argo submit — Argo has no built-in fan-out
D) A single DAG task with withItems: loop — DAG supports loops natively
✅ Correct Answer: B

The fan-out/fan-in pattern is DAG's strength. Use withSequence or withItems to dynamically generate parallel tasks from a list, and .AnySucceeded in the merge task's dependencies so it runs when ALL parallel tasks have completed.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Fan-Out/Fan-In Pattern with DAG
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                        split-records                             │
  │                   (output: list of 10 batches)                   │
  └────────────┬──────────────┬──────────────┬──────────────────────┘
               │              │              │
     ┌─────────▼──┐  ┌────────▼──┐  ┌───────▼───┐
     │process-batch│  │process-batch│  │process-batch│  ... ×10
     │    #1      │  │    #2      │  │    #3     │
     └─────────┬──┘  └────────┬──┘  └───────┬───┘
               │              │              │
               └──────────────┼──────────────┘
                              │
                    ┌─────────▼──┐
                    │merge-results│
                    │   (fan-in) │
                    └────────────┘

  dag:
    tasks:
    - name: split
      template: split-records
    - name: process
      template: process-batch
      dependencies: [split]
      withParam: "{{tasks.split.outputs.parameters.batches}}"  ← Dynamic fan-out
    - name: merge
      dependencies: [process]   ← .AnySucceeded by default
      template: merge-results

  💡 withParam + dependencies = dynamic fan-out/fan-in

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Fan-out/fan-in is the most tested DAG pattern. Know that withParam dynamically creates tasks from a list, and the merge task's dependencies: [process] waits for ALL dynamic tasks (Argo handles the expansion). In anihpj/jobpost, the nightly report generator fans out across all job categories, processes each in parallel, then merges into a single CSV.

1.5 Workflow Spec Deep Dive & PatternsQ31-Q36

Argo Workflows Q31 of 200
A Workflow spec has entrypoint, templates, arguments, and volumes. Which field is responsible for passing small configuration values (strings, numbers, booleans) between the Workflow level and individual templates?
A) volumes — mount ConfigMaps with configuration values
B) env — set environment variables in every template container
C) arguments.parameters — define typed parameters at the Workflow level, referenced as {{workflow.parameters.name}} in templates
D) annotations — store metadata that templates read via the Kubernetes API
✅ Correct Answer: C

arguments.parameters at the Workflow spec level define values accessible to all templates via {{workflow.parameters.name}}. Parameters are for small values (strings, numbers). For files and large data, use Artifacts.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Parameters vs Artifacts — When to Use Which
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PARAMETERS                          ARTIFACTS                   │
  │  • Strings, numbers, booleans        • Files, directories        │
  │  • Passed inline in YAML or CLI      • Stored in S3/GCS/PVC      │
  │  • {{workflow.parameters.name}}      • inputs.artifacts + name   │
  │  • Good for: branch name, env,       • Good for: test reports,   │
  │    toggle flags, version numbers       coverage data, logs       │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Example:                                                        │
  │  spec:                                                           │
  │    arguments:                                                    │
  │      parameters:              ← Workflow-level (global)          │
  │      - name: env                                                  │
  │        value: staging                                            │
  │      - name: run-migrations                                      │
  │        value: "true"                                             │
  │    entrypoint: main                                              │
  │    templates:                                                    │
  │    - name: main                                                  │
  │      steps:                                                      │
  │      - - name: deploy                                            │
  │          template: deploy-app                                    │
  │          arguments:                                              │
  │            parameters:         ← Step-level (passed to template) │
  │            - name: target-env                                     │
  │              value: "{{workflow.parameters.env}}"                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Parameters vs Artifacts is a fundamental distinction. Parameters = small values (use for config). Artifacts = files (use for data). Passing a file path as a parameter does NOT pass the file — you must use artifacts for actual file transfer.

Argo Workflows Q32 of 200
A test step intermittently fails due to network flakes. You want it to automatically retry up to 3 times with a 10-second backoff between attempts. How do you configure this?
A) Wrap the workflow submit in a shell loop: for i in 1 2 3; do argo submit ... && break; done
B) Set backoffLimit: 3 on the template — same as Kubernetes Job retries
C) Use a DAG with 3 identical tasks — if one fails, the next runs via dependency chaining
D) Add retryStrategy to the template: {limit: 3, retryPolicy: Always, backoff: {duration: 10s}}
✅ Correct Answer: D

retryStrategy is a template-level setting that controls automatic retries. Key fields: limit (max attempts), retryPolicy (Always/OnFailure/OnError), and backoff (duration between retries with optional factor and maxDuration).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Retry Strategy Configuration
  - name: flaky-test
    retryStrategy:
      limit: 3                     ← Max 3 retries (4 attempts total)
      retryPolicy: Always           ← Retry on any failure
      backoff:
        duration: "10s"             ← Wait 10s between attempts
        factor: 2                   ← Exponential: 10s, 20s, 40s
        maxDuration: "5m"           ← Cap backoff at 5 minutes

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  retryPolicy OPTIONS                                             │
  │  • Always          — Retry on any failure or error               │
  │  • OnFailure       — Retry only on step failure (not Error)     │
  │  • OnError         — Retry only on controller errors            │
  │  • OnTransientError — Retry on transient infra errors only      │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Execution timeline (limit: 3, duration: 10s):                   │
  │  Attempt 1 → FAIL → [wait 10s] → Attempt 2 → FAIL → [wait 10s]  │
  │  → Attempt 3 → FAIL → [wait 10s] → Attempt 4 → (final result)   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Retry strategy is essential for production workflows. In anihpj/jobpost CI, the integration test step uses retryStrategy: {limit: 2, retryPolicy: OnFailure} because integration tests occasionally fail due to external API timeouts — retry eliminates false positives without masking real bugs.

Argo Workflows Q33 of 200
A workflow sometimes hangs indefinitely due to a stalled step. How do you enforce a maximum total runtime for the entire workflow?
A) Set spec.activeDeadlineSeconds — the workflow is terminated (status: Failed) if it exceeds this duration from start time
B) Set spec.ttlSecondsAfterFinished — the workflow CRD is deleted after the timeout
C) Use a Suspend template with a duration — the workflow pauses and then fails
D) Set timeout on each individual template — there is no workflow-level timeout
✅ Correct Answer: A

activeDeadlineSeconds is a workflow-level hard timeout. If the workflow hasn't completed within the specified seconds, it's forcefully terminated with status Failed. Individual templates can also have timeout: "2h" for per-step time limits.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Timeouts — Workflow-Level vs Template-Level
  spec:
    activeDeadlineSeconds: 3600     ← Workflow: max 1 hour TOTAL

    templates:
    - name: slow-step
      timeout: "30m"                ← Template: this step max 30 min
      container:
        image: python:3.11
        command: [python, process.py]

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  TIMEOUT HIERARCHY                                               │
  │                                                                  │
  │  activeDeadlineSeconds (Workflow)  ← Hard cap on TOTAL time      │
  │  └─ timeout (Template)              ← Per-step cap               │
  │                                                                  │
  │  If timeout expires: step is terminated, workflow continues      │
  │  If activeDeadlineSeconds expires: ENTIRE workflow is killed     │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ⚠️  activeDeadlineSeconds starts counting from workflow         │
  │     submission, NOT from when the first pod starts running       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Timeouts prevent runaway workflows from consuming cluster resources indefinitely. Always set activeDeadlineSeconds in production workflows. In anihpj/jobpost CI, the nightly report workflow has activeDeadlineSeconds: 7200 (2 hours) — if it takes longer, something is wrong and it should fail rather than block the next night's run.

Argo Workflows Q34 of 200
You need a nightly ETL workflow that: queries PostgreSQL, transforms data with Python pandas, writes CSV to S3. Which template type orchestrates the sequence: DB query → transform → upload?
A) DAG — ETL pipelines always use DAG for data lineage tracking
B) Steps — a clean sequential pipeline: [[extract], [transform], [load]], each as a Container or Script template
C) A single Container template running a shell script that does all three — simpler is better
D) Resource template — create Kubernetes Jobs for each phase
✅ Correct Answer: B

For a simple sequential ETL pipeline, Steps is the clearest choice: [[extract], [transform], [load]]. Each inner list runs one phase. If a phase needs parallelism (e.g., transform 5 partitions), add multiple templates to that inner list. DAG would be overkill — no complex dependencies exist.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Steps ETL Pattern — Simple and Clean
  entrypoint: nightly-etl
  templates:
  - name: nightly-etl
    steps:
    - - name: extract
        template: query-db           ← PostgreSQL → CSV
    - - name: transform
        template: pandas-transform   ← Python pandas data cleaning
    - - name: load
        template: upload-s3          ← aws s3 cp → reports bucket

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Each step uses artifacts to pass data:                          │
  │                                                                  │
  │  extract ──{raw.csv}──▶ transform ──{clean.csv}──▶ load          │
  │                                                                  │
  │  • extract: outputs.artifacts [{name: raw-data, path: /tmp/raw}] │
  │  • transform: inputs.artifacts [{name: raw-data}] → process →    │
  │               outputs.artifacts [{name: clean-data}]             │
  │  • load: inputs.artifacts [{name: clean-data}] → upload to S3    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Choosing Steps vs DAG is a common scenario question. ETL = sequential phases = Steps. The anihpj/jobpost nightly report workflow uses exactly this pattern: query job postings from PostgreSQL, transform with pandas, upload CSV to S3 for the analytics dashboard.

Argo Workflows Q35 of 200
A data processing workflow needs to run the same transformation on 100 different CSV files. Which Argo feature dynamically creates parallel tasks from a list?
A) withSequence — generates tasks from a numeric range like start: 1, end: 100
B) Manually write 100 task definitions in the DAG — Argo has no dynamic task generation
C) withParam — accepts a JSON list and creates one task per item, passing the item value to the template as {{item}}
D) withItems — a hardcoded YAML list of values, one task per item
✅ Correct Answer: C

withParam dynamically generates tasks from a parameter value (typically a JSON list produced by a previous step). Each item in the list spawns a parallel task. withSequence is for numeric ranges. withItems is for hardcoded lists. withParam is the most flexible — the list comes from workflow state.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Dynamic Task Generation — withParam vs withItems vs withSequence
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  withItems (hardcoded list):                                     │
  │  withItems:                                                      │
  │  - file-a.csv                                                    │
  │  - file-b.csv                   ← Static list in YAML            │
  │  - file-c.csv                                                    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  withParam (dynamic list, from step output):                     │
  │  - name: list-files                                             │
  │    template: list-csv-files     ← Outputs JSON list              │
  │  - name: process-file                                           │
  │    dependencies: [list-files]                                    │
  │    withParam: "{{tasks.list-files.outputs.parameters.csvs}}"     │
  │    template: transform-csv                                       │
  │                                                                  │
  │  withSequence (numeric range):                                   │
  │  withSequence:                                                   │
  │    start: "1"                                                    │
  │    end: "100"                   ← Generates 100 tasks            │
  │    format: "batch-%d"           ← Item values: batch-1..batch-100│
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: withParam is the most commonly tested dynamic generation method. Know the difference: withItems = hardcoded, withParam = dynamic (from step output), withSequence = numeric range. In anihpj/jobpost, the nightly report uses withParam to process job categories dynamically — if a new category is added, no YAML change needed.

Argo Workflows Q36 of 200
The anihpj/jobpost nightly report workflow must: query PostgreSQL for new job postings → compute statistics with pandas → generate a CSV → email it to the admin. How should this be structured?
A) A single Container template running a monolithic Python script that does everything — simplest, fewest moving parts
B) A DAG with each step as an independent task with dependencies — complex dependencies require DAG
C) Four separate Workflows submitted sequentially — one per phase
D) A Steps template: [[query-db], [compute-stats], [generate-csv], [email-report]] with artifacts passing data between each step, and an exit handler to clean up temp files
✅ Correct Answer: D

A Steps template with sequential phases and artifact chaining is the cleanest design for a linear ETL pipeline. Each phase is isolated (debuggable, retryable), data flows through artifacts, and an exit handler ensures cleanup regardless of outcome.

📝 Detailed Explanation
anihpj/jobpost Nightly Report — Complete Workflow
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  anihpj Nightly Report Workflow (runs at 2 AM daily)             │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐   │
  │  │ query-db │───▶│compute   │───▶│generate  │───▶│email     │   │
  │  │          │    │stats     │    │csv       │    │report    │   │
  │  │ SELECT.. │    │ pandas   │    │ to_csv() │    │ smtplib  │   │
  │  └────┬─────┘    └────┬─────┘    └────┬─────┘    └──────────┘   │
  │       │               │               │                          │
  │       ▼               ▼               ▼                          │
  │  {raw.json}  ←─── {stats.pkl} ←─── {report.csv}                  │
  │  (artifact)       (artifact)       (artifact)                    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  onExit: cleanup-temp                                            │
  │  • Deletes temporary artifacts from S3                           │
  │  • Drops temp tables in PostgreSQL                               │
  │  • Logs completion to audit table                                │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Schedule: CronWorkflow — "0 2 * * *" (2 AM daily)              │
  │  Timeout: activeDeadlineSeconds: 3600 (1 hour max)               │
  │  Retry: retryStrategy {limit: 2, retryPolicy: OnFailure}         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Q36 ties together Q31-Q35: parameters (DB credentials), retry strategy (network flakes), timeouts (1 hour max), data processing patterns (ETL), and dynamic generation (if reporting across categories). This is the capstone that shows how all Workflow Spec concepts combine into a production-ready pipeline.

🚢 Category 2: Argo CD (34%)Q37-Q70

🟡 Intermediate: GitOps Continuous Delivery with Argo CD

This category is about declarative GitOps deployment using Argo CD. Think of Argo CD as the controller that makes your cluster match your Git repo — when you push to Git, Argo CD detects the change, compares it with the live cluster state, and syncs to bring reality in line with your declared desired state. For anihpj/jobpost, Argo CD watches the k8s/ directory in the repo and automatically deploys the Django app, PostgreSQL, and Redis to your clusters.

Q37-Q70 34 Questions 🟡→🔴 Intermediate→Advanced

2.1 Argo CD Fundamentals — Architecture & Core ConceptsQ37-Q44

Argo CD Q37 of 200
What is Argo CD and what core problem does it solve in a Kubernetes environment?
A) Argo CD is a CI server that builds and tests container images before deploying them
B) Argo CD is a monitoring tool that tracks application health and sends alerts on failures
C) Argo CD is a secrets management tool that injects credentials into pods at runtime
D) Argo CD is a GitOps continuous delivery tool — it watches a Git repository and continuously reconciles the live cluster state with the desired state declared in Git
✅ Correct Answer: D

Argo CD implements the GitOps pull model: instead of a CI server pushing changes to the cluster, Argo CD runs inside the cluster and pulls the desired state from a Git repository. It continuously compares the live state with the desired state and reconciles any drift — automatically or manually.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Argo CD — GitOps Pull Model
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PUSH MODEL (Traditional CI/CD)    PULL MODEL (Argo CD GitOps)   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────┐    ┌─────────┐          ┌──────┐                      │
  │  │  CI  │───▶│ kubectl │          │  CI  │───▶ Git Push          │
  │  │Server│    │  apply  │          │Server│                      │
  │  └──────┘    └────┬────┘          └──────┘                      │
  │                   │                          ┌──────────┐       │
  │                   ▼                          │ Argo CD  │       │
  │              ┌─────────┐                     │ (inside  │       │
  │              │ Cluster │                     │ cluster) │       │
  │              └─────────┘                     └────┬─────┘       │
  │                                                   │             │
  │  ❌ CI has cluster creds                          ▼             │
  │  ❌ No drift detection                       ┌─────────┐       │
  │  ❌ Manual rollbacks                         │ Cluster │       │
  │                                              └─────────┘       │
  │                                              ✅ No external creds│
  │                                              ✅ Auto drift fix  │
  │                                              ✅ Git = audit log │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Argo CD is a CNCF Graduated project. The exam tests your understanding of the GitOps pull model vs traditional push CI/CD. Key advantage: the cluster pulls from Git, so no external system needs cluster credentials. For anihpj/jobpost, Argo CD watches the k8s/ directory and deploys all components across your 6-cluster lab.

Argo CD Q38 of 200
Which components make up the core Argo CD architecture?
A) API Server (gRPC/REST), Repository Server (Git/Helm), Application Controller (reconciliation), Redis (cache), Dex (SSO)
B) API Server, Scheduler, Controller Manager, etcd — same as Kubernetes control plane
C) Web UI, Git Webhook Receiver, Sync Engine, PostgreSQL database
D) ArgoCD Agent (per cluster), Central Controller, Git Poller, MongoDB
✅ Correct Answer: A

Argo CD has 5 core components: API Server (exposes the API and Web UI), Repository Server (clones Git repos, renders Helm/Kustomize), Application Controller (the reconciliation loop — compares desired vs live state), Redis (caches repo content and cluster state), and Dex (optional SSO/OIDC integration).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Argo CD Architecture — 5 Core Components
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                      ARGO CD ARCHITECTURE                        │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────────────┐     ┌──────────────────┐ │
  │  │   Git    │────▶│  Repo Server     │────▶│     Redis        │ │
  │  │  Repo    │     │  (clones,render) │     │     (cache)      │ │
  │  └──────────┘     └──────────────────┘     └──────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────────────┐     ┌──────────────────┐ │
  │  │  Web UI  │────▶│   API Server     │────▶│    Dex (SSO)     │ │
  │  │  /  CLI  │     │   (gRPC+REST)    │     │   (optional)     │ │
  │  └──────────┘     └────────┬─────────┘     └──────────────────┘ │
  │                            │                                     │
  │                            ▼                                     │
  │                   ┌──────────────────┐                           │
  │                   │   Application    │                           │
  │                   │   Controller     │──▶ kubectl apply/diff     │
  │                   │   (reconcile)    │                           │
  │                   └────────┬─────────┘                           │
  │                            │                                     │
  │                            ▼                                     │
  │                   ┌──────────────────┐                           │
  │                   │  Target Cluster  │                           │
  │                   │  (same or remote)│                           │
  │                   └──────────────────┘                           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: You need to know each component's role. The Application Controller is the heart — it runs the reconciliation loop every 3 minutes (default). Redis is critical for performance — without it, every sync would re-clone the repo. Dex enables enterprise SSO (Okta, GitHub, Google).

Argo CD Q39 of 200
Which three CRDs form the core of Argo CD's resource model?
A) Deployment, Service, Ingress — standard Kubernetes resources
B) Application (what+where to deploy), AppProject (logical grouping with RBAC), ApplicationSet (template-driven app generation)
C) SyncPolicy, HealthCheck, ResourceOverride — ArgoCD-specific configuration CRDs
D) GitRepo, TargetCluster, SyncJob — the GitOps pipeline resources
✅ Correct Answer: B

The three core CRDs are: Application (defines what to deploy and where), AppProject (groups Applications with RBAC and source/destination restrictions), and ApplicationSet (generates multiple Applications from a template — e.g., one per cluster or branch).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Argo CD CRD Hierarchy
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  AppProject: "production"                                        │
  │  │  sourceRepos: [github.com/anihpj/*]                           │
  │  │  destinations: [{namespace: prod-*, server: https://...}]    │
  │  │  roles: [admin, developer, viewer]                            │
  │  │                                                               │
  │  ├── Application: "jobpost-api"                                  │
  │  │   source: {repo: ..., path: k8s/overlays/prod}                │
  │  │   destination: {server: ..., namespace: prod-api}             │
  │  │   syncPolicy: {automated: {prune: true, selfHeal: true}}     │
  │  │                                                               │
  │  ├── Application: "jobpost-db"                                   │
  │  │   source: {chart: postgresql, repo: bitnami-charts}           │
  │  │   destination: {server: ..., namespace: prod-db}              │
  │  │                                                               │
  │  ApplicationSet: "jobpost-clusters"                              │
  │    generators: [{list: {elements: [{cluster: us-east}, ...]}}]  │
  │    template: (generates one Application per cluster)             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: These 3 CRDs are the foundation. Application = single deployment unit. AppProject = security/access boundary. ApplicationSet = scale (deploy to N clusters automatically). For anihpj/jobpost, an ApplicationSet generates per-cluster Applications across your 6-cluster lab.

Argo CD Q40 of 200
Your application uses a Helm chart. Another team uses raw YAML. A third uses Kustomize overlays. How does Argo CD handle these different source formats?
A) Argo CD only supports raw YAML — Helm and Kustomize must be pre-rendered before committing to Git
B) Argo CD converts everything to Helm internally — Kustomize and raw YAML are auto-converted
C) Argo CD natively supports multiple source types via the source field: Helm, Kustomize, Directory (raw YAML), Plugin (custom), and Jsonnet
D) Argo CD treats all sources as raw YAML — it's up to the user to run helm template or kustomize build before committing
✅ Correct Answer: C

Argo CD's Repository Server detects the source type and renders it accordingly. For Helm, it runs helm template. For Kustomize, it runs kustomize build. For raw YAML directories, it applies all .yaml files. This happens transparently — you just declare the source type (or let Argo auto-detect).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Argo CD Source Types
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SOURCE TYPE        CONFIG                        ARGO DOES       │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Helm               chart: postgresql             helm template   │
  │                     repo: https://charts.bitnami.com             │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Kustomize          path: k8s/overlays/prod       kustomize build│
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Directory          path: k8s/raw-yaml            kubectl apply  │
  │                     (recursive YAML discovery)                   │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Plugin (CMP v2)    Custom init/ generate commands               │
  │                     (e.g., jsonnet, cue, cdk8s)                  │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Jsonnet            path: jsonnet-app/            jsonnet eval   │
  │                     (native support)                              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Multi-source support is a key Argo CD feature. You'll be tested on configuring source types correctly. For anihpj/jobpost, the Django app uses Kustomize overlays, PostgreSQL uses a Helm chart, and Redis uses raw YAML — all managed by one Argo CD instance.

Argo CD Q41 of 200
You have 6 Kubernetes clusters (dev, staging, prod-us, prod-eu, prod-asia, mgmt). How does Argo CD deploy applications to multiple clusters?
A) Install a separate Argo CD instance on each cluster — one Argo CD manages only its own cluster
B) One Argo CD instance can manage multiple clusters — each Application specifies a destination.server (cluster API URL) and destination.namespace
C) Argo CD uses a federation protocol — clusters automatically sync with each other
D) Use ApplicationSet with a cluster generator to deploy per cluster, but a single Argo CD can only manage 3 clusters
✅ Correct Answer: B

Argo CD supports multi-cluster management from a single control plane. Each Application specifies destination.server (the Kubernetes API server URL) and destination.namespace. Argo CD connects to each cluster using credentials stored in Secrets. For your 6-cluster lab, one Argo CD instance on the management cluster can deploy to all 6.

Argo CD Q43 of 200

Argo CD Q44 of 200
What is the Argo CD reconciliation loop and how often does it run by default?
A) Real-time — Argo CD uses webhooks to detect Git changes and syncs immediately on every push
B) Every 3 minutes by default — the Application Controller compares the desired state (Git) with the live state (cluster) and triggers sync if drift is detected (when auto-sync is enabled)
C) Every 15 seconds — Argo CD is designed for sub-minute reconciliation
D) Reconciliation is manual only — Argo CD only checks when you click SYNC or run argocd app sync
✅ Correct Answer: B

The reconciliation loop runs every 3 minutes by default (configurable via timeout.reconciliation). It compares the desired state from Git with the live state in the cluster. If drift is detected and auto-sync is enabled, Argo CD corrects it automatically. Webhooks can trigger faster reconciliation but the polling loop is the fallback.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Reconciliation Loop — Every 3 Minutes
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  RECONCILIATION LOOP (every 3 min by default)                    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─────────┐     ┌──────────────┐     ┌──────────────┐          │
  │  │  Git    │────▶│ 1. Clone/Pull│────▶│ 2. Render    │          │
  │  │  Repo   │     │    latest    │     │    YAML      │          │
  │  └─────────┘     └──────────────┘     └──────┬───────┘          │
  │                                              │                   │
  │                    ┌─────────────────────────┘                   │
  │                    ▼                                             │
  │              ┌──────────────┐                                    │
  │              │ 3. Compare   │◀──── Desired State                 │
  │              │    Diff      │                                    │
  │              └──────┬───────┘                                    │
  │                     │                                            │
  │                     ▼                                            │
  │              ┌──────────────┐     ┌──────────────┐              │
  │              │ 4. Sync?     │─Yes─▶│ 5. Apply    │              │
  │              │    Drift?    │     │    Changes   │              │
  │              └──────────────┘     └──────────────┘              │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Faster syncs: Configure webhooks from GitHub/GitLab             │
  │  Timeout: timeout.reconciliation: 180s (default, configurable)   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: The 3-minute reconciliation interval is a frequently tested fact. Know that webhooks provide faster response (<5 seconds) but reconciliation is the safety net. For anihpj/jobpost, webhooks from GitHub trigger near-instant syncs, but the 3-minute loop ensures sync even if a webhook is missed.

2.2 Synchronize Applications — Manual & AutomatedQ45-Q52

Argo CD Q45 of 200
An Application is OutOfSync after a Git push. How do you manually trigger a sync to deploy the changes?
A) Delete and recreate the Application — Argo CD has no manual sync trigger
B) Run kubectl apply -f application.yaml again to force the controller to re-process
C) Run argocd app sync <app-name> via CLI or click the SYNC button in the Web UI
D) Wait for the 3-minute reconciliation loop — there is no way to trigger an immediate sync
✅ Correct Answer: C

Manual sync is triggered via argocd app sync <name> or the SYNC button in the Web UI. In the Web UI, you can also select specific resources to sync (e.g., sync only the Deployment but not the ConfigMap) and choose sync options (Prune, Dry Run, Apply Only, Force).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Manual Sync — CLI vs Web UI
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CLI:                                                            │
  │  $ argocd app sync jobpost-api                  ← Full sync      │
  │  $ argocd app sync jobpost-api --resource Deployment:jobpost-api │
  │                                                ← Selective sync  │
  │  $ argocd app sync jobpost-api --dry-run        ← Preview only   │
  │  $ argocd app sync jobpost-api --prune          ← Delete extras  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  WEB UI:                                                         │
  │  Application detail → SYNC button → options:                     │
  │  ☑ PRUNE       (delete resources not in Git)                     │
  │  ☑ DRY RUN     (preview without applying)                        │
  │  ☑ APPLY ONLY  (don't prune, just apply changes)                 │
  │  ☑ FORCE       (delete and recreate, e.g., for immutables)       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Manual sync is the most basic operation. You'll need to know both CLI and Web UI methods. Sync options (Prune, Dry Run, Force) are frequently tested. For anihpj/jobpost, developers manually sync staging after verifying changes in the Web UI diff view.

Argo CD Q46 of 200
You want Argo CD to automatically sync an Application whenever Git changes AND automatically delete resources removed from Git. Which syncPolicy configuration achieves this?
A) syncPolicy: {automated: {prune: true, selfHeal: true}} — auto-syncs on Git changes AND self-heals manual edits AND prunes removed resources
B) syncPolicy: {automated: {enabled: true}} — the simplest auto-sync configuration
C) syncPolicy: {automated: {prune: true}} — prune alone enables auto-sync
D) syncPolicy: {sync: automatic, prune: yes, heal: yes}
✅ Correct Answer: A

syncPolicy.automated with both prune: true and selfHeal: true provides full automation: auto-sync on Git changes, auto-revert manual edits, and auto-delete resources removed from Git. Without prune, removed resources persist. Without selfHeal, manual edits are not reverted until the next Git change.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Automated Sync Policy — prune + selfHeal
  spec:
    syncPolicy:
      automated:
        prune: true          ← Delete resources removed from Git
        selfHeal: true       ← Revert manual changes in cluster
        allowEmpty: false    ← Don't sync if Git is empty

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SCENARIO                          WITHOUT        WITH           │
  │                                    prune+selfHeal prune+selfHeal │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Push new Deployment to Git        Manual sync    Auto-sync ✅   │
  │  Delete Service from Git           Stale Service  Pruned ✅      │
  │  Someone kubectl edits replica=5   Drift stays    Self-heal ✅   │
  │  Pod crashes (no replicas change)  No action      No action ✅  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: prune + selfHeal is the most tested syncPolicy configuration. Know the difference: prune handles resource deletion, selfHeal handles manual edits. For production anihpj/jobpost, this configuration ensures the cluster ALWAYS matches Git — no drift, no stale resources.

Argo CD Q47 of 200
An Application shows status "Unknown." What does this mean?
A) Argo CD has crashed and can no longer determine application state
B) The Git repository is unreachable — Argo CD cannot fetch the desired state
C) The Application has never been synced — Unknown means "no history available"
D) Argo CD cannot determine the sync status — typically because the Application manifest has errors, the repo is unreachable, or the target cluster is offline
✅ Correct Answer: D

Unknown means Argo CD's controller cannot determine whether the live state matches the desired state. Common causes: the Git repository is unreachable, the Application YAML has invalid syntax, the target cluster is unreachable, or the Application Controller is experiencing errors.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Sync Status — Synced vs OutOfSync vs Unknown
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  STATUS        MEANING                          ACTION           │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  🟢 Synced     Live == Desired                  None             │
  │                (cluster matches Git)                              │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  🟡 OutOfSync  Live != Desired                  Sync needed      │
  │                (cluster differs from Git)                        │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  ⚪ Unknown    Can't determine status            Debug!           │
  │                (repo down? bad YAML? cluster offline?)           │
  │                                                                  │
  │  DEBUG Unknown:                                                  │
  │  $ argocd app get jobpost-api --hard-refresh                     │
  │  $ argocd app logs jobpost-api       ← Controller logs           │
  │  $ kubectl get app jobpost-api -o yaml  ← Check conditions       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Sync status interpretation is fundamental. Unknown ≠ OutOfSync. Unknown usually means the controller itself can't complete the comparison. Check repo connectivity and Application YAML validity first when debugging Unknown status.

Argo CD Q48 of 200
Your application needs DB migrations to run BEFORE the new Deployment rolls out, and a smoke test AFTER. Which sync mechanism controls this ordering?
A) syncPolicy.syncOrder — a numeric ordering of sync phases
B) Sync Phases: PreSync → Sync → PostSync → SyncFail. DB migration runs in PreSync, deployment in Sync, smoke test in PostSync
C) Argo CD syncs all resources simultaneously — ordering requires separate Applications
D) initContainers in the Deployment spec — Argo CD doesn't control ordering
✅ Correct Answer: B

Argo CD sync has 4 phases: PreSync (runs before sync — DB migrations, schema updates), Sync (applies the actual resources), PostSync (runs after successful sync — smoke tests, notifications), and SyncFail (runs if sync fails — rollback, alerts).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Sync Phases — Before, During, After
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SYNC OPERATION                                                  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────┐                                                    │
  │  │ PreSync  │  🗄️  DB migrations, schema updates, pre-warming    │
  │  │  Hooks   │     Runs BEFORE any Sync resources                 │
  │  └────┬─────┘                                                    │
  │       │ Success                                                  │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  ┌──────────┐                                                    │
  │  │  Sync    │  🚀 Apply Deployment, Service, ConfigMap, etc.     │
  │  │  Wave 1  │     sync-wave: "1" annotations                     │
  │  │  Wave 2  │     sync-wave: "2" (waits for Wave 1 healthy)     │
  │  │  Wave N  │     ...                                            │
  │  └────┬─────┘                                                    │
  │       │ Success                                                  │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  ┌──────────┐                                                    │
  │  │ PostSync │  ✅ Smoke tests, cache warming, Slack notification │
  │  │  Hooks   │     Runs only after ALL Sync resources healthy     │
  │  └──────────┘                                                    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  On Failure: SyncFail hooks run (rollback, alert on-call)        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Sync phases and hooks are critical for production deployments. PreSync for DB migrations ensures the schema matches before pods start. PostSync for smoke tests verifies the deployment works. For anihpj/jobpost, python manage.py migrate runs as a PreSync hook before the Django pods roll out.

Argo CD Q49 of 200
You need ConfigMap + Secret to deploy first, then Deployment, then HPA. How do you control resource ordering WITHIN the Sync phase?
A) Annotate resources with argocd.argoproj.io/sync-wave: "N" — Argo CD syncs waves in order (-infinity → 0 → 1 → 2 → ... → +infinity), each wave waits for the previous to be healthy
B) List resources in order in the Git repo — Argo CD syncs in alphabetical filename order
C) Use separate Applications with dependencies — there is no in-sync ordering
D) Resources sync in Kubernetes dependency order automatically — Argo CD inherits K8s ordering
✅ Correct Answer: A

Sync waves control resource ordering within the Sync phase via the annotation argocd.argoproj.io/sync-wave: "N". Wave -1 runs first, then 0 (default), 1, 2, etc. Resources in the same wave sync in parallel. Each wave waits for ALL resources in the previous wave to be Healthy before proceeding.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Sync Waves — Ordered Deployment Within Sync Phase
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WAVE -1:  Namespace, CRDs, ClusterRole                          │
  │            (infrastructure prerequisites)                        │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
  │  WAVE 0:   Secrets, ConfigMaps, PVCs, ServiceAccounts            │
  │            (configuration + storage — no pods yet)               │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
  │  WAVE 1:   Deployments, StatefulSets, DaemonSets                 │
  │            (workloads — configs exist, secrets exist)            │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
  │  WAVE 2:   Services, Ingresses, HPAs                             │
  │            (networking + scaling — pods are Running)             │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Annotation:                                                     │
  │  metadata:                                                       │
  │    annotations:                                                  │
  │      argocd.argoproj.io/sync-wave: "2"                           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Sync waves are heavily tested. Without them, a Deployment might start before its ConfigMap exists — causing CrashLoopBackOff. For anihpj/jobpost: Secrets/ConfigMaps (wave 0) → PostgreSQL StatefulSet (wave 1) → Django Deployment (wave 2) → Service/Ingress (wave 3).

Argo CD Q50 of 200
You need to run a database migration job BEFORE the application Deployment rolls out. Which mechanism guarantees the migration completes (and succeeds) before the Deployment is applied?
A) Sync wave: annotate the migration Job with wave 0 and Deployment with wave 1
B) Init container in the Deployment — runs migration before the main container starts
C) PreSync hook — annotate the migration resource with argocd.argoproj.io/hook: PreSync. Argo CD runs ALL PreSync hooks and waits for success BEFORE entering the Sync phase
D) CronJob scheduled 1 minute before the sync — timing-based coordination
✅ Correct Answer: C

Resource hooks (not sync waves) are the correct mechanism for things that must happen BEFORE or AFTER the main sync. Sync waves only order resources WITHIN the Sync phase. Hooks run OUTSIDE the Sync phase — PreSync runs first, PostSync runs after.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Resource Hooks vs Sync Waves
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  HOOKS (run OUTSIDE Sync phase)                                  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  PreSync:   "Run this BEFORE syncing anything"                   │
  │    argocd.argoproj.io/hook: PreSync                              │
  │    Use: DB migrations, schema checks, pre-deploy validation      │
  │                                                                  │
  │  PostSync:  "Run this AFTER everything is healthy"               │
  │    argocd.argoproj.io/hook: PostSync                             │
  │    Use: Smoke tests, cache warming, Slack notifications          │
  │                                                                  │
  │  SyncFail:  "Run this if sync fails"                             │
  │    argocd.argoproj.io/hook: SyncFail                             │
  │    Use: Rollback, alert on-call                                  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  HOOKS vs WAVES:                                                 │
  │  • Hooks = run OUTSIDE sync, before/after all resources          │
  │  • Waves = order resources WITHIN the sync phase                 │
  │  • PreSync hook for DB migration runs BEFORE wave -1 resources   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Hooks vs Waves is a critical distinction. Hooks are for lifecycle events (before/after sync). Waves are for ordering within sync. For anihpj/jobpost, python manage.py migrate runs as a PreSync hook Job — the Django Deployment won't start until migrations succeed. This prevents "table doesn't exist" errors.

Argo CD Q51 of 200
Only a single Deployment in your Application is OutOfSync — the other 15 resources are Synced. How do you sync ONLY the out-of-sync resource without touching the others?
A) argocd app sync --resource Deployment:my-app or use ApplyOutOfSyncOnly sync option — syncs only resources with drift
B) Delete and recreate the Application — Argo CD always syncs everything together
C) Manually run kubectl apply on just the Deployment — bypass Argo CD for selective updates
D) Set syncPolicy.automated.selfHeal: false temporarily — this restricts sync to the changed resource
✅ Correct Answer: A

Argo CD supports selective sync via --resource flag: argocd app sync my-app --resource Deployment:my-deploy. The Web UI also allows checking specific resources in the sync dialog. The ApplyOutOfSyncOnly sync option automatically syncs only resources with drift.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Selective Sync — Target Specific Resources
  CLI:
  $ argocd app sync jobpost-api \
      --resource Deployment:jobpost-api      ← Sync only Deployment
  $ argocd app sync jobpost-api \
      --resource apps:Deployment:jobpost-api \  ← Fully qualified
      --resource ConfigMap:jobpost-config

  SYNC OPTIONS:
  ☑ ApplyOutOfSyncOnly  ← Only sync resources with drift
  ☑ Prune               ← Delete resources not in Git
  ☑ Replace             ← Delete + recreate (for immutables)

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  FULL SYNC vs SELECTIVE SYNC                                     │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Application: 16 resources (15 Synced, 1 OutOfSync Deployment)   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Full sync:    All 16 resources re-applied (slow, unnecessary)   │
  │  Selective:    Only the 1 Deployment is updated (fast, safe)     │
  │  ApplyOutOfSyncOnly: Same as selective but automatic             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Selective sync is essential for large applications. Syncing 100 resources to update 1 ConfigMap is wasteful and risky. For anihpj/jobpost, if only the Django Deployment image tag changes, you sync just that Deployment — PostgreSQL and Redis remain untouched.

Argo CD Q52 of 200
Someone manually ran kubectl scale deployment/jobpost-api --replicas=10. With selfHeal enabled, what happens?
A) Nothing — manual changes are respected and persisted until the next Git push
B) Argo CD sends an alert but does not revert — selfHeal only applies to deleted resources
C) The manual change is merged with the Git state — Argo CD combines both into the final spec
D) Within 3 minutes (next reconciliation), Argo CD detects drift and reverts replicas back to the value declared in Git — selfHeal corrects manual edits automatically
✅ Correct Answer: D

Self-healing is the mechanism that detects and reverts manual changes to resources managed by Argo CD. Within the 3-minute reconciliation interval (or faster with webhooks), Argo CD detects the drift (desired=3 replicas, live=10 replicas) and reverts to the Git-declared state.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Self-Healing — Drift Detection & Auto-Revert
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  TIME 0:00                                                       │
  │  Git: replicas: 3       Cluster: replicas: 3      Synced ✅      │
  │                                                                  │
  │  TIME 0:30 — Someone runs: kubectl scale deploy/jobpost --rep=10 │
  │  Git: replicas: 3       Cluster: replicas: 10     OutOfSync ⚠️   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  TIME 3:00 — Reconciliation loop runs                            │
  │  Argo CD detects drift: desired=3 ≠ live=10                      │
  │  selfHeal: true → Argo CD applies replicas: 3                    │
  │  Git: replicas: 3       Cluster: replicas: 3      Synced ✅      │
  │                                                                  │
  │  selfHeal: true   ← Reverts manual edits                         │
  │  selfHeal: false  ← Manual edits persist until next Git change   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  💡 This is why "Don't kubectl edit Argo CD-managed resources"   │
  │     is a golden rule — Argo CD will revert your changes.         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Self-healing is a core GitOps principle. The exam tests your understanding that Argo CD enforces Git as the single source of truth — manual changes are temporary at best. For anihpj/jobpost production, selfHeal is ALWAYS enabled to prevent configuration drift from accidental manual edits.

2.3 Application CRD — Management & LifecycleQ53-Q60

Argo CD Q53 of 200
An Application CRD defines what to deploy, where, and how. Which fields specify the source repository, target cluster, and sync behavior?
A) spec.from, spec.to, spec.behavior
B) spec.source (git repo + path), spec.destination (cluster + namespace), spec.syncPolicy (automated/manual + prune/selfHeal)
C) spec.git, spec.cluster, spec.policy
D) spec.template.source, spec.template.destination, spec.template.sync
✅ Correct Answer: B

The Application CRD has three core sections: spec.source (what to deploy — repo URL, path, targetRevision), spec.destination (where — server URL, namespace), and spec.syncPolicy (how — automated/manual, prune, selfHeal).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Application CRD — The 3 Core Sections
  apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
  kind: Application
  metadata:
    name: jobpost-api
    namespace: argocd
  spec:
    project: production              ← AppProject membership
    ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
    ║  source:                          ← WHAT to deploy           ║
    ║    repoURL: github.com/anihpj/jobpost                       ║
    ║    targetRevision: main                                      ║
    ║    path: k8s/overlays/prod                                   ║
    ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝
    ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
    ║  destination:                     ← WHERE to deploy          ║
    ║    server: https://kubernetes.default.svc                    ║
    ║    namespace: prod-api                                       ║
    ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝
    ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
    ║  syncPolicy:                      ← HOW to sync              ║
    ║    automated:                                                ║
    ║      prune: true                                             ║
    ║      selfHeal: true                                          ║
    ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Every Application you create on the exam will use these three fields. source → destination mapping is the most common configuration. For anihpj/jobpost, each environment (dev/staging/prod) has its own Application pointing to the same repo but different paths and namespaces.

Argo CD Q54 of 200
An Application shows "Degraded" health status. What does this indicate?
A) One or more resources managed by this Application are not healthy — e.g., a Deployment's pods are CrashLoopBackOff or a StatefulSet is missing replicas
B) The Git repository is unreachable — Argo CD cannot fetch the latest desired state
C) The Application has been deleted but resources still exist in the cluster
D) The Application sync failed — Degraded is a sync status, not a health status
✅ Correct Answer: A

Degraded means the Application is Synced (Git matches cluster) but one or more resources are unhealthy. This is different from OutOfSync (Git ≠ cluster). Common causes: CrashLoopBackOff pods, failed readiness probes, or missing PVCs.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Health Status — 5 Possible States
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  STATUS       MEANING                         ACTION             │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  🟢 Healthy    All resources healthy           None              │
  │               (pods Running, probes ok)                         │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  🟡 Progressing Resources are being created    Wait              │
  │               (pods starting, PV binding)                       │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  🔴 Degraded   ≥1 resource unhealthy           Debug pods!       │
  │               (CrashLoopBackOff, ImagePullBackOff)              │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  ⚫ Suspended  App is suspended                 Resume or delete  │
  │               (no reconciliation runs)                           │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  ⚪ Missing    Resource not found in cluster    Sync needed      │
  │               (was deleted manually or not yet created)          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Health ≠ Sync status. An app can be Synced but Degraded (YAML matches, but pods crash). An app can be OutOfSync but Healthy (old version runs fine). Always check BOTH statuses. For anihpj/jobpost, Degraded usually means the new image has a bug causing CrashLoopBackOff.

Argo CD Q55 of 200
A deployment to production broke the application. You need to immediately revert to the previously working version. Which Argo CD feature handles this?
A) kubectl rollout undo — Argo CD delegates rollback to Kubernetes native mechanisms
B) Delete and recreate the Application pointing to the previous Git commit — there is no built-in rollback
C) argocd app rollback <app> <revision> — Argo CD maintains a revision history and can revert to any previous deployment state with a single command
D) Use argocd app sync --revision HEAD~1 — sync to the parent of the current commit
✅ Correct Answer: C

Argo CD tracks every sync as a revision. argocd app history shows the revision list. argocd app rollback <app> <ID> reverts to any previous revision in seconds — by pointing the Application back to the Git commit that produced that revision.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Rollback — Instant Revert to Any Revision
  $ argocd app history jobpost-api
  ID  DATE                           REVISION
  10  2026-06-12 14:30:00            abc123  ← BROKEN (current)
  9   2026-06-12 14:00:00            def456  ← WORKING
  8   2026-06-12 13:00:00            ghi789

  $ argocd app rollback jobpost-api 9
  Application jobpost-api rolled back to revision 9
  → Argo CD syncs to commit def456
  → Application back to working state in seconds

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ROLLBACK vs kubectl rollout undo                                │
  │  kubectl rollout undo = reverts Deployment spec only             │
  │  argocd rollback = reverts ALL resources (Deployment, ConfigMap, │
  │                    Service, Ingress, etc.) to that Git commit     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Rollback is a critical exam topic. It's faster than git revert + push + wait for sync. For anihpj/jobpost production, rollback to the last known-good revision takes under 10 seconds — essential for minimizing downtime during incidents.

Argo CD Q56 of 200
You pushed a change to Git but Argo CD still shows Synced with the old state. What's the difference between Normal Refresh and Hard Refresh?
A) Normal Refresh re-syncs; Hard Refresh deletes and recreates the Application
B) There is no difference — Refresh always re-clones the repo
C) Normal Refresh updates the UI; Hard Refresh restarts the Application Controller
D) Normal Refresh compares cached manifest with live state. Hard Refresh clears the cache, re-clones the Git repo, re-renders manifests, then compares — useful when the cache is stale
✅ Correct Answer: D

Normal Refresh uses the cached manifest from Redis and re-computes the diff. Hard Refresh (--hard-refresh) invalidates the cache, forces a fresh Git clone, and re-renders all manifests. Use Hard Refresh when Argo CD seems "stuck" showing stale state.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Refresh — Normal vs Hard
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  NORMAL REFRESH (argocd app get --refresh)                       │
  │  ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐                 │
  │  │ Redis    │────▶│ Compare  │────▶│ Update   │                 │
  │  │ (cached) │     │ cached   │     │ UI       │                 │
  │  └──────────┘     └──────────┘     └──────────┘                 │
  │                                                                  │
  │  HARD REFRESH (argocd app get --hard-refresh)                    │
  │  ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐                 │
  │  │ Git Repo │────▶│ Re-clone │────▶│ Re-render│                 │
  │  │ (fresh)  │     │ + cache  │     │ + compare│                 │
  │  └──────────┘     └──────────┘     └──────────┘                 │
  │                                                                  │
  │  💡 When to use Hard Refresh:                                    │
  │  • Git push was 5 min ago but ArgoCD shows "Synced"              │
  │  • Webhook failed to fire                                        │
  │  • Cache became corrupted                                        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Refresh vs Hard Refresh is a key debugging concept. If an app appears stuck, always try Hard Refresh before more drastic measures. For anihpj/jobpost, developers use argocd app get jobpost-api --hard-refresh when they're certain they pushed but Argo CD hasn't detected it.

Argo CD Q57 of 200
What happens when you run argocd app delete jobpost-api? How can you ensure the Application's managed resources are also cleaned up?
A) Resources are always deleted with the Application — that's the default behavior
B) The Application CRD is removed but resources may become orphans. To cascade-delete resources, use --cascade flag or set a finalizer that blocks deletion until resources are cleaned up
C) Resources are never deleted — you must manually kubectl delete each one
D) Argo CD automatically prunes resources when syncPolicy.prune is enabled on the Application
✅ Correct Answer: B

By default, argocd app delete only removes the Application CRD — managed resources become orphans. Use --cascade to delete resources too. Finalizers can prevent accidental deletion until resources are accounted for. syncPolicy.prune applies during sync, not during deletion.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Application Deletion — Cascade & Finalizers
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ argocd app delete jobpost-api                                 │
  │  • Application CRD removed ✅                                    │
  │  • Deployment, Service, ConfigMap → ORPHANED ⚠️                  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  $ argocd app delete jobpost-api --cascade                       │
  │  • Application CRD removed ✅                                    │
  │  • Deployment, Service, ConfigMap → DELETED ✅                   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  FINALIZERS (prevent accidental deletion):                       │
  │  metadata:                                                       │
  │    finalizers:                                                   │
  │    - resources-finalizer.argocd.argoproj.io                     │
  │                                                                  │
  │  With finalizer: App deletion is BLOCKED until resources are     │
  │  cleaned up (or finalizer is manually removed)                   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Understanding what happens on Application deletion prevents resource leaks. In anihpj/jobpost, deleting the Application without cascade leaves orphaned Deployments consuming cluster resources. Always use --cascade for cleanup, or rely on finalizers for safety.

Argo CD Q58 of 200
You want to restrict the "dev" team to only deploy from the "dev" branch to the "dev-*" namespaces. Which Argo CD resource enforces these boundaries?
A) AppProject — defines sourceRepos (allowed Git repos), destinations (allowed clusters+namespaces), clusterResourceAllowList, and roles for RBAC
B) NetworkPolicy — restricts which namespaces can communicate with the Argo CD API
C) Application.spec.project field — setting it to "dev" automatically restricts source and destination
D) Kubernetes RBAC Role + RoleBinding on the dev namespace
✅ Correct Answer: A

AppProject is the security boundary in Argo CD. It defines what sources an Application can use (sourceRepos), where it can deploy (destinations), and what cluster-scoped resources it can create. Combined with RBAC roles, AppProject provides fine-grained access control.

📝 Detailed Explanation
AppProject — Security Boundaries
  apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
  kind: AppProject
  metadata:
    name: dev-team
  spec:
    sourceRepos:                    ← Only these repos
    - https://github.com/anihpj/jobpost
    destinations:                   ← Only these clusters + namespaces
    - namespace: dev-*
      server: https://kubernetes.default.svc
    roles:                          ← RBAC roles for this project
    - name: developer
      policies:
      - p, proj:dev-team:developer, applications, sync, dev-team/*, allow

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  AppProject: dev-team                                            │
  │  │                                                               │
  │  ├── Application: jobpost-dev    (allowed: repo ✓, ns dev-api ✓) │
  │  ├── Application: jobpost-stg    (BLOCKED: ns stg-api not in ns-*)│
  │  └── Application: external-app   (BLOCKED: different repo)       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: AppProject is the primary security mechanism. The exam tests setting up project boundaries and troubleshooting "Permission Denied" errors caused by AppProject restrictions. For anihpj/jobpost, separate AppProjects for dev/staging/prod prevent accidental cross-environment deployments.

Argo CD Q59 of 200
You need to deploy the same application to 6 clusters (your lab). Instead of creating 6 identical Application YAMLs, how do you generate them automatically?
A) Use kubectl apply -f app.yaml in a for loop — create one Application per cluster manually
B) Use Helm to template the Application YAML with cluster-specific values
