C) ApplicationSet — uses generators (List, Cluster, Git, Matrix) to produce multiple Applications from a single template, one per cluster/branch/environment
D) Use the "App of Apps" pattern — a parent Application that references child Applications
✅ Correct Answer: C

ApplicationSet is the scale mechanism for Argo CD. It uses generators (List, Cluster, Git, Matrix, Merge, Pull Request, SCM Provider) to produce multiple Applications from a single template. Add a new cluster to the generator list → a new Application is created automatically.

📝 Detailed Explanation
ApplicationSet — Template × Generator = N Applications
  ApplicationSet: jobpost-all-clusters
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  generators:                           template:                 │
  │  - list:                               project: production      │
  │      elements:                         source:                   │
  │      - cluster: kind-cl1                repoURL: github.com/...  │
  │        env: dev                         path: k8s/overlays/      │
  │      - cluster: kubeadm                       {{env}}            │
  │        env: staging                   destination:              │
  │      - cluster: kind-cl2                server: {{cluster}}      │
  │        env: prod-us                    namespace: {{env}}-api    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Produces 6 Applications:                                        │
  │  • jobpost-kind-cl1  (dev)         • jobpost-kubeadm (staging)   │
  │  • jobpost-kind-cl2  (prod-us)     • jobpost-kind-cl3 (prod-eu)  │
  │  • jobpost-minikube  (prod-asia)   • jobpost-mgmt    (mgmt)      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: ApplicationSet is heavily tested. Know the generator types: List (static), Cluster (auto-discovers clusters), Git (directories in a repo), Matrix (combines two generators). For your 6-cluster lab, a Cluster generator auto-discovers all clusters and creates an Application per cluster.

Argo CD Q60 of 200
You create a parent Application that deploys other Applications (not Deployments). What is this recursive GitOps pattern called and why use it?
A) Nested Deployments — Argo CD deploys Deployments that create more Deployments
B) ApplicationSet with recursive generator — ApplicationSets can generate other ApplicationSets
C) GitOps recursion — a Kubernetes anti-pattern that should be avoided
D) App of Apps — a parent Application whose Git repository contains child Application YAMLs. Argo CD deploys the parent, which triggers deployment of all children. Enables one-click cluster bootstrapping
✅ Correct Answer: D

The App of Apps pattern uses a parent Application whose repo contains YAML files for child Applications. When the parent syncs, Argo CD creates all child Applications, which then deploy their own resources. This is the standard pattern for bootstrapping a cluster with all its applications from a single Git repo.

📝 Detailed Explanation
App of Apps — Recursive GitOps Bootstrapping
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  bootstrap/                              ← Parent App repo       │
  │  ├── app-database.yaml                   (child Application)     │
  │  ├── app-api.yaml                        (child Application)     │
  │  ├── app-redis.yaml                      (child Application)     │
  │  └── app-monitoring.yaml                 (child Application)     │
  │                                                                  │
  │  $ argocd app create bootstrap \                                │
  │      --repo github.com/anihpj/bootstrap \                       │
  │      --path . --dest-server ... --dest-ns argocd                 │
  │                                                                  │
  │  bootstrap Application syncs → creates 4 child Applications      │
  │  Each child Application syncs → deploys its actual resources     │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Result: One command bootstraps an ENTIRE cluster!               │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: App of Apps is the standard cluster bootstrapping pattern. Know that the parent Application's repo contains ONLY Application YAMLs (not Deployments). For anihpj/jobpost, the bootstrap app deploys database, API, Redis, and monitoring — one command to deploy the entire stack to any cluster.

2.4 Helm & Kustomize IntegrationQ61-Q65

Argo CD Q61 of 200
Your team uses a Helm chart for PostgreSQL. How does Argo CD deploy it without running helm install manually?
A) Argo CD runs helm install internally and tracks releases via Helm's native storage
B) Argo CD runs helm template to render the chart into raw YAML, then applies it as regular Kubernetes resources — no Helm Tiller or release storage needed
C) You must pre-render the chart with helm template > manifest.yaml and commit the output to Git
D) Argo CD uses Helm's SDK to manage releases, upgrades, and rollbacks natively
✅ Correct Answer: B

Argo CD uses helm template to render Helm charts into plain Kubernetes YAML, then applies them. No Helm release storage, no Tiller. You configure Helm values via spec.source.helm.values or valueFiles in the Application spec.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Argo CD + Helm — Template, Don't Install
  spec:
    source:
      repoURL: https://charts.bitnami.com/bitnami
      chart: postgresql
      targetRevision: 12.1.0
      helm:
        values: |
          auth:
            username: jobpost
            database: jobpost_db
          primary:
            persistence:
              size: 10Gi

  Argo CD does: helm template postgresql . -f values.yaml → kubectl apply
  Argo CD does NOT: helm install (no release tracking, no Tiller)

  💡 This means: No Helm release state stored anywhere.
      Rollback = argocd app rollback (based on Git commits, not Helm releases)

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Argo CD's Helm integration is "template-then-apply", not "helm install". This is critical for understanding rollback behavior. For anihpj/jobpost, PostgreSQL is deployed via the Bitnami Helm chart with custom values for database name, credentials, and storage size.

Argo CD Q62 of 200
You need different PostgreSQL passwords for dev and prod using the same Helm chart. How do you pass environment-specific values?
A) Use valueFiles to reference values-dev.yaml and values-prod.yaml in different Application specs, or use helm.values inline with {{workflow.parameters}}
B) Create separate Helm charts for each environment — one chart per environment
C) Use --set flags in the Application's syncPolicy — Argo CD passes them during sync
D) Helm values are global — environment-specific configuration requires Kustomize, not Helm
✅ Correct Answer: A

Argo CD supports valueFiles (paths to values YAML files in the repo) and inline values in the Application spec. Different Applications can reference different values files: values-dev.yaml for dev, values-prod.yaml for prod — same chart, different configuration.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Helm Values — Per-Environment Configuration
  # Application: postgres-dev
  source:
    chart: postgresql
    helm:
      valueFiles:
      - values-dev.yaml       ← auth.password: dev-pass
      values: |
        primary:
          persistence:
            size: 5Gi          ← Small disk for dev

  # Application: postgres-prod
  source:
    chart: postgresql
    helm:
      valueFiles:
      - values-prod.yaml      ← auth.password: prod-secret
      values: |
        primary:
          persistence:
            size: 100Gi        ← Large disk for production

  ✅ Same chart, same repo, different values = different deployments

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Helm value management is frequently tested. Know both methods: valueFiles (separate YAML files in repo) and inline values (directly in Application spec). For anihpj/jobpost, PostgreSQL uses valueFiles per environment for cleaner separation of config from Application definition.

Argo CD Q63 of 200
Your team uses Kustomize overlays for environment-specific configurations. How does Argo CD handle Kustomize-based applications?
A) You must run kustomize build and commit the output — Argo CD cannot run Kustomize natively
B) Argo CD treats Kustomize directories as raw YAML — it applies kustomization.yaml as a regular ConfigMap
C) Argo CD auto-detects Kustomize (presence of kustomization.yaml) and runs kustomize build to render the final manifests before applying — fully native support
D) Kustomize requires a Plugin (CMP) — it's not natively supported
✅ Correct Answer: C

Argo CD has native Kustomize support. When it detects a kustomization.yaml in the source path, it automatically runs kustomize build to render the final YAML before applying. No pre-rendering needed — just point the Application at the overlay directory.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kustomize Overlays in Argo CD
  k8s/
  ├── base/
  │   ├── deployment.yaml       ← Base Django deployment
  │   ├── service.yaml
  │   └── kustomization.yaml    ← resources: [deployment, service]
  │
  └── overlays/
      ├── dev/
      │   ├── kustomization.yaml ← patches: [replicas: 1, image: :dev]
      │   └── replicas-patch.yaml
      ├── staging/
      │   └── kustomization.yaml ← patches: [replicas: 2, image: :staging]
      └── prod/
          └── kustomization.yaml ← patches: [replicas: 5, image: :latest]

  Application spec:
    source:
      path: k8s/overlays/prod       ← Argo CD runs: kustomize build .
      targetRevision: main

  Argo CD auto-detects kustomization.yaml → kustomize build → apply

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Kustomize is the most common native Kubernetes configuration tool. Argo CD's seamless Kustomize support is a major selling point. For anihpj/jobpost, the Django app uses Kustomize overlays — dev, staging, and prod differ only in replica count and image tag.

Argo CD Q64 of 200
With Kustomize, the base defines common configuration and overlays customize per environment. How many Argo CD Applications are needed for dev, staging, and prod?
A) One Application pointing to the base directory — Argo CD applies all overlays automatically
B) Zero — Kustomize handles multi-environment internally, no Argo CD Applications needed
C) One Application per overlay — three Applications: dev (path: overlays/dev), staging (path: overlays/staging), prod (path: overlays/prod)
D) Same as C, or use an ApplicationSet with a Git directory generator to auto-create one Application per overlay directory
✅ Correct Answer: D

Each environment needs its own Application (different path, potentially different destination). You can create them manually (3 separate Application YAMLs) or use an ApplicationSet with a Git directory generator to auto-create one Application per overlay directory — add a new overlay, a new Application appears automatically.

📝 Detailed Explanation
ApplicationSet Git Generator — One App Per Overlay
  ApplicationSet: jobpost-all-envs
    generators:
    - git:
        repoURL: github.com/anihpj/jobpost
        directories:
        - path: k8s/overlays/*
    template:
      name: jobpost-{{path.basename}}
      source:
        path: k8s/overlays/{{path.basename}}
      destination:
        namespace: {{path.basename}}-api

  Auto-creates:
  • jobpost-dev     (path: k8s/overlays/dev)
  • jobpost-staging (path: k8s/overlays/staging)
  • jobpost-prod    (path: k8s/overlays/prod)

  💡 Add overlays/canary/ → new jobpost-canary Application appears!

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: ApplicationSet + Kustomize = auto-scaling GitOps. This is the recommended pattern for multi-environment deployments. For anihpj/jobpost, an ApplicationSet with a Git directory generator creates one Application per overlay, deployed to the corresponding namespace on your 6-cluster lab.

Argo CD Q65 of 200
Argo CD 2.6+ supports multiple sources in a single Application. When would you combine Helm AND Kustomize in one Application?
A) When you want to deploy a Helm chart (e.g., PostgreSQL) and then apply Kustomize patches on top — Argo CD renders the Helm chart first, then runs Kustomize on the rendered output
B) Multiple sources are not supported — you must use separate Applications for Helm and Kustomize
C) When you need two different Git repositories — one for Helm values and one for Kustomize overlays
D) Multiple sources only apply to Directory-type sources — Helm and Kustomize cannot be combined
✅ Correct Answer: A

Argo CD 2.6+ supports multiple sources: the first source provides the base manifests (e.g., Helm chart), and subsequent sources apply modifications (e.g., Kustomize patches) on top. This enables "deploy a Helm chart, then customize it with Kustomize" in a single Application.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Multiple Sources — Helm + Kustomize Pipeline
  spec:
    sources:
    - repoURL: https://charts.bitnami.com       ← Source 1: Helm
      chart: postgresql
      targetRevision: 12.1.0
      helm:
        valueFiles:
        - values.yaml
    - repoURL: github.com/anihpj/jobpost         ← Source 2: Kustomize
      path: k8s/patches/postgres
      targetRevision: main

  Pipeline:
  Source 1 → helm template → raw YAML
           → Source 2 → kustomize build on rendered YAML → final YAML
           → kubectl apply

  💡 Use case: Deploy standard PostgreSQL chart, then apply
     company-specific security labels and resource limits via Kustomize,
     all in ONE Application.

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Multiple sources is a newer feature (2.6+) and increasingly tested. For anihpj/jobpost, PostgreSQL is deployed via the Bitnami Helm chart (source 1), then Kustomize applies company-standard labels and security contexts (source 2) — one Application, two sources.

2.5 Reconciliation Patterns — Production StrategiesQ66-Q70

Argo CD Q66 of 200
For production, you want auto-sync with self-healing. But you also want to PREVENT syncs during business hours (9 AM-5 PM). Which feature restricts when syncs can occur?
A) syncPolicy.automated.schedule — cron-based scheduling of sync operations
B) Sync Windows — define syncWindows in AppProject to ALLOW or DENY syncs during specific time windows. Deny windows block syncs; Allow windows enable them
C) syncPolicy.automated.maintenanceWindow — time-based sync restriction
D) Manual sync only — you cannot restrict auto-sync timing in Argo CD
✅ Correct Answer: B

Sync Windows are defined at the AppProject level. A deny window blocks all syncs (manual and automated) during specified times. An allow window permits syncs only during specified times. Multiple windows can be combined for complex schedules.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Sync Windows — Time-Based Sync Control
  spec:
    syncWindows:
    - kind: deny
      schedule: "0 9 * * 1-5"           ← Mon-Fri 9 AM
      duration: 8h                      ← Block for 8 hours (9 AM-5 PM)
      applications:
      - jobpost-prod-*                  ← Only affects prod
      manualSync: false                 ← Even manual syncs blocked

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  9 AM ─────────────────────────────────────── 5 PM               │
  │  ⛔ SYNC BLOCKED (business hours)                                 │
  │  • Auto-syncs are queued, execute at 5 PM                        │
  │  • Manual syncs rejected with "Sync window denied" error         │
  │  • Emergency: add annotation to bypass:                          │
  │    argocd.argoproj.io/sync-options: Force=true                   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  5 PM ─────────────────────────────────────── 9 AM               │
  │  ✅ SYNC ALLOWED (off-hours maintenance window)                   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Sync Windows are production-critical. They prevent changes during business hours when users are active. For anihpj/jobpost production, syncs are blocked 9 AM-5 PM weekdays — changes deploy during the 5 PM-9 AM window when traffic is lower.

Argo CD Q67 of 200
For a compliance-regulated environment, every deployment must be reviewed in a Git PR before reaching production. Which sync strategy enforces this?
A) Manual sync — auto-sync disabled. Changes merge to main, Argo CD shows OutOfSync, and a human approves by clicking SYNC or running argocd app sync. Git PR review + manual sync = compliance gate
B) Auto-sync with sync windows — restrict auto-sync to PR review times
C) Use a PreSync hook that checks for PR approval status before syncing
D) SyncPolicy with requireApproval: true — built-in approval workflow
✅ Correct Answer: A

For compliance, disable auto-sync. The flow: developer creates PR → PR reviewed and merged → Argo CD detects change → shows OutOfSync → authorized person runs argocd app sync. The Git PR is the review gate; the manual sync is the deployment gate.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Compliance GitOps — PR Review + Manual Sync
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  1. Developer: git push → create PR                              │
  │  2. Reviewer: approves PR → merge to main                        │
  │  3. Argo CD: detects new commit → Application shows OutOfSync    │
  │  4. Release Manager: reviews diff in Argo CD Web UI              │
  │  5. Release Manager: clicks SYNC (or argocd app sync)            │
  │  6. Argo CD: deploys (with sync waves, hooks, etc.)              │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Compliance checklist:                                            │
  │  ✅ Git PR review (who approved? git log shows)                   │
  │  ✅ Argo CD diff preview (what will change?)                     │
  │  ✅ Manual sync gate (who deployed? argocd history shows)        │
  │  ✅ Full audit trail: Git log + Argo CD history                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Manual sync for compliance is a key pattern. The exam tests the difference between automated (speed) and manual (control) sync strategies. For anihpj/jobpost production, manual sync ensures no unreviewed code reaches users.

Argo CD Q68 of 200
You want dev to auto-sync every commit, staging to auto-sync during off-hours only, and prod to require manual approval. How do you implement this tiered strategy?
A) All three environments must use the same sync strategy — Argo CD cannot have different policies per environment
B) Use syncPolicy.tier field with values "dev", "staging", "prod"
C) Each environment has its own Application with a different syncPolicy: dev=auto+selfHeal, staging=auto+syncWindow, prod=manual. Separate AppProjects can enforce different Sync Windows
D) Use ApplicationSet with tier-based template — one ApplicationSet handles all tiers
✅ Correct Answer: C

Each environment is a separate Application (or ApplicationSet-generated) with its own syncPolicy. Dev uses automated: {prune: true, selfHeal: true}. Staging uses automated with Sync Windows. Prod has no automated section (manual sync). AppProjects can enforce these policies.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Tiered Sync Strategy — Dev → Staging → Prod
  DEV:                STAGING:               PROD:
  auto-sync: true     auto-sync: true        auto-sync: (absent)
  selfHeal: true      selfHeal: true         manual sync only
  prune: true         prune: true            syncWindows: deny 9-5
                      syncWindows:
                        deny 9-5 weekdays

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ENV        SYNC STRATEGY     REASON                             │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  dev        Auto (instant)    Devs need fast feedback            │
  │  staging    Auto (off-hours)  Test env, don't disrupt QA         │
  │  prod       Manual            Compliance, change control         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Tiered sync strategies are the real-world pattern. The exam expects you to configure different syncPolicies per environment. For anihpj/jobpost, this ensures speed in dev, stability in staging, and safety in production.

Argo CD Q69 of 200
You need to deploy a complex stack: Namespace → CRDs → Secrets → Database → API → Frontend → Monitoring. How do App of Apps + sync waves achieve this ordered deployment?
A) App of Apps handles ordering automatically — child apps deploy in creation order
B) Use separate Argo CD instances — one per layer of the stack
C) Sync waves alone control everything — one Application with wave annotations on all resources
D) App of Apps creates child Applications in order (bootstrap → db → api → frontend). Each child Application uses sync waves internally for its own resources. The combination achieves multi-tier ordered deployment
✅ Correct Answer: D

For complex stacks, combine App of Apps (tier ordering) with sync waves (resource ordering within each tier). The bootstrap app creates child apps sequentially; each child app uses sync waves internally for its resources.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Progressive Sync — App of Apps + Sync Waves
  BOOTSTRAP APP (App of Apps)
  ├── Wave 0: infra-app (Namespace, CRDs)          ← sync-wave: -1
  │   └── Creates: Namespace, CRDs
  ├── Wave 1: db-app (Secrets, PostgreSQL)          ← sync-wave: 0
  │   └── Internal waves: Secret(0) → PVC(0) → StatefulSet(1)
  ├── Wave 2: api-app (Django, Service)             ← sync-wave: 0
  │   └── Internal waves: ConfigMap(0) → Deployment(1) → Service(2)
  ├── Wave 3: frontend-app (React, Ingress)         ← sync-wave: 0
  │   └── Internal waves: Deployment(0) → Service(1) → Ingress(2)
  └── Wave 4: monitoring-app (Prometheus, Grafana)  ← sync-wave: 0

  Each app waits for previous app to be Healthy before syncing
  Within each app, sync waves order resource creation

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Combining App of Apps with sync waves is the recommended pattern for complex deployments. For anihpj/jobpost, this ensures PostgreSQL is running and accepting connections before the Django API attempts to connect — preventing CrashLoopBackOff on startup.

Argo CD Q70 of 200
For anihpj/jobpost across your 6-cluster lab, which sync strategy should each environment use for optimal developer experience and production safety?
A) All clusters use auto-sync with selfHeal — consistency is more important than per-environment customization
B) Dev (kind-cl1): auto-sync + selfHeal. Staging (kubeadm): auto-sync + selfHeal + sync window (off-hours). Prod clusters: manual sync with PR review. This balances speed, testing, and safety
C) All clusters use manual sync — safety first, developers can wait for syncs
D) Dev and staging share one Argo CD instance; production clusters each have their own
✅ Correct Answer: B

The recommended strategy for anihpj/jobpost: dev = fully automated (instant feedback), staging = automated with time windows (test safely), prod = manual (change control). This matches industry best practices and the CAPA exam expectations for tiered GitOps strategies.

📝 Detailed Explanation
anihpj/jobpost — 6-Cluster Sync Strategy
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CLUSTER        SYNC STRATEGY         APPPROJECT                 │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  kind-cl1 (dev)     Auto-sync + selfHeal   dev-team              │
  │                     Instant on push                              │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  kubeadm (staging)  Auto-sync + sync window  staging-team        │
  │                     Mon-Fri 5PM-9AM only                         │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  kind-cl2 (prod-us) Manual sync only       production            │
  │  kind-cl3 (prod-eu) Manual sync only       production            │
  │  minikube (prod-as) Manual sync only       production            │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  mgmt (mgmt)        Manual sync            platform              │
  │                                                                  │
  │  💡 This mirrors real-world enterprise GitOps patterns.          │
  │     Your 6-cluster lab is a complete multi-environment           │
  │     GitOps simulation.                                           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

This caps off Argo CD foundations: Q37-Q70 covered the core Argo CD syllabus — architecture, Applications, sync strategies, Helm/Kustomize, reconciliation patterns, and production readiness. Continue to Q137-Q170 for advanced topics: multi-cluster management, security/RBAC, advanced sync patterns, and enterprise production practices.

📈 Category 3: Argo Rollouts (18%)Q71-Q88

🟡 Intermediate: Progressive Delivery with Argo Rollouts

This category is about deploying safely with zero downtime using Argo Rollouts. Think of Argo Rollouts as a smart Deployment controller — it replaces Kubernetes Deployment and adds Blue/Green and Canary strategies with automated metric analysis. For anihpj/jobpost, Rollouts ensures new versions roll out gradually to 10% → 25% → 50% → 100% of traffic, with automatic rollback if error rates spike.

Q71-Q88 18 Questions 🟡 Intermediate

3.1 Argo Rollouts FundamentalsQ71-Q76

Argo Rollouts Q71 of 200
What is Argo Rollouts and how does it relate to a standard Kubernetes Deployment?
A) Argo Rollouts is an add-on that monitors Deployments for errors and sends alerts
B) Argo Rollouts replaces the need for Kubernetes altogether — it manages containers directly
C) Argo Rollouts is a Kubernetes controller and CRD that replaces Deployment — it has the same pod template spec PLUS a strategy field for Blue/Green and Canary deployments with automated analysis
D) Argo Rollouts is the CI component — it builds images, then Deployment deploys them
✅ Correct Answer: C

Argo Rollouts is a drop-in replacement for Deployment. Its CRD has the same spec.template (pod spec), spec.replicas, and spec.selector as Deployment, PLUS a spec.strategy field that enables Blue/Green and Canary deployment patterns with automated metric-driven promotion or rollback.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Rollout = Deployment + Progressive Delivery Strategy
  Deployment:                          Rollout:
  apiVersion: apps/v1                  apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
  kind: Deployment                     kind: Rollout
  spec:                                spec:
    replicas: 3                          replicas: 3
    selector: ...                        selector: ...
    template:                            template:
      spec:                                spec:
        containers: ...                      containers: ...
                                        strategy:          ← NEW!
                                          canary:
                                            steps:
                                            - setWeight: 10
                                            - pause: {duration: 5m}
                                            - setWeight: 50
                                            - analysis: ...

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Rollout is a superset of Deployment. You convert a Deployment to a Rollout by changing kind: Deployment to kind: Rollout and apiVersion — the pod spec is identical. The Rollout controller manages ReplicaSets just like Deployments do, but with strategy awareness.

Argo Rollouts Q72 of 200
What fields does a Rollout CRD have that a standard Deployment does NOT?
A) strategy — defines Blue/Green or Canary with steps. analysis — references AnalysisTemplate for metric evaluation. restartAt — triggers a controlled restart
B) maxSurge and maxUnavailable — Deployment doesn't have these
C) progressDeadlineSeconds — Deployment has no progress deadline
D) paused field — Deployment cannot be paused mid-rollout
✅ Correct Answer: A

The Rollout CRD adds: strategy (canary or blueGreen with steps), analysis (metric-driven validation), and restartAt (trigger a controlled restart of all pods). These fields enable progressive delivery patterns impossible with standard Deployments.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Rollout CRD — The Extra Fields
  spec:
    replicas: 3
    template: {...}              ← Same as Deployment

    strategy:                    ← NEW: Blue/Green or Canary
      canary:
        steps:
        - setWeight: 10          ← 10% traffic to new version
        - pause: {duration: 5m}  ← Wait 5 minutes
        - setWeight: 50          ← 50% traffic
        - analysis:              ← Run metric analysis
            templates:
            - templateName: error-rate-check

    revisionHistoryLimit: 10     ← Same as Deployment
    paused: false                ← Same as Deployment

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Know the extra Rollout fields. strategy is required. analysis is optional but powerful. restartAt is a convenient way to rolling-restart pods. For anihpj/jobpost, the Django API Rollout uses Canary strategy to gradually shift traffic and roll back automatically if error rates exceed 1%.

Argo Rollouts Q73 of 200
What command-line tool is used to interact with Argo Rollouts, and what are its primary commands?
A) argocd rollout — same CLI as Argo CD with a rollout subcommand
B) kubectl-argo-rollouts — a kubectl plugin with commands: get, status, promote, abort, retry, undo, dashboard
C) argo rollouts — part of the argo CLI suite
D) kubectl rollout — the built-in kubectl command works with Argo Rollouts
✅ Correct Answer: B

kubectl-argo-rollouts is a kubectl plugin installed separately. Key commands: kubectl argo rollouts get <name> (view status), promote (skip pause, advance to next step), abort (stop rollout, rollback), retry (retry failed analysis), undo (revert to previous stable version), and dashboard (launch web UI).

📝 Detailed Explanation
kubectl-argo-rollouts CLI
  $ kubectl argo rollouts get jobpost-api
  Name:            jobpost-api
  Status:          ✔ Healthy
  Strategy:        Canary
  Step:            2/4  (setWeight: 25)
  Ready:           3/3

  $ kubectl argo rollouts promote jobpost-api     ← Skip pause, advance
  $ kubectl argo rollouts abort jobpost-api       ← Rollback now
  $ kubectl argo rollouts undo jobpost-api        ← Revert to stable
  $ kubectl argo rollouts dashboard               ← Launch Web UI
  $ kubectl argo rollouts status jobpost-api -w   ← Watch live

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: CLI proficiency with kubectl-argo-rollouts is tested. Key commands: promote (manual promotion), abort (emergency rollback), undo (revert). For anihpj/jobpost, kubectl argo rollouts promote jobpost-api is used to advance the canary from 10% to 50% after manual verification.

Argo Rollouts Q74 of 200
What are the possible status phases a Rollout can be in?
A) Pending, Running, Succeeded, Failed — same as Workflow lifecycle
B) Synced, OutOfSync, Unknown — same as Argo CD sync status
C) Available, Unavailable, Progressing — same as Deployment conditions
D) Progressing (update in progress), Degraded (analysis failed), Paused (waiting for manual promote or duration), Healthy (all good), Completed (update fully rolled out)
✅ Correct Answer: D

Rollout has 5 status phases: Progressing (canary/blueGreen in progress), Degraded (analysis failed, may auto-rollback), Paused (waiting at a pause step), Healthy (stable, no update in progress), and Completed (update fully promoted and stable).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Rollout Status Phases
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Healthy ──▶ (image update) ──▶ Progressing ──▶ Paused          │
  │                                              │ (pause step)     │
  │                                              ▼                   │
  │  Completed ◀── Progressing ◀── promote        Paused            │
  │  (all done)                                    │                 │
  │                                                │ (analysis fail) │
  │                                                ▼                 │
  │  Healthy ◀──────── rollback ──────── Degraded                   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  STATUS       MEANING                         ACTION             │
  │  Progressing  Canary/BlueGreen in progress    Watch              │
  │  Paused       At a pause step                 Promote or wait    │
  │  Degraded     Analysis failed                 Rollback or retry  │
  │  Healthy      Stable, no update in progress   None               │
  │  Completed    Update fully rolled out         None               │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Rollout status interpretation is critical. Degraded ≠ Failed — Degraded means analysis failed and rollback may be automatic. For anihpj/jobpost, if a canary shows Degraded (error rate > 1%), Argo Rollouts auto-rolls back to the previous stable version.

Argo Rollouts Q75 of 200
What is Blue/Green deployment in Argo Rollouts and how does traffic switching work?
A) Two ReplicaSets run simultaneously: Blue (current/active) and Green (new version). A Kubernetes Service selector switches instantly from Blue to Green after Green is fully ready — providing instant rollback (switch back to Blue)
B) Traffic gradually shifts from Blue to Green — 10% → 50% → 100% over time
C) Blue and Green pods run side-by-side in the same ReplicaSet with different container images
D) Blue/Green is just a renaming of Kubernetes RollingUpdate — same behavior
✅ Correct Answer: A

Blue/Green runs two complete environments: Blue (active, serving traffic) and Green (new version, not receiving traffic until the switch). When Green is fully ready and healthy, the Service selector switches instantly. Rollback is instant — switch the selector back to Blue.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Blue/Green — Two Environments, Instant Switch
  BEFORE SWITCH:                      AFTER SWITCH:
  ┌──────────────────────┐           ┌──────────────────────┐
  │  Service: jobpost-api│           │  Service: jobpost-api│
  │  selector: version:  │           │  selector: version:  │
  │    blue              │           │    green             │
  └──────┬───────────────┘           └──────┬───────────────┘
         │                                  │
    ┌────▼────┐  ┌────────┐           ┌────────┐  ┌────▼────┐
    │  BLUE   │  │ GREEN  │           │  BLUE  │  │  GREEN  │
    │ v1.0.0  │  │ v1.1.0 │           │ v1.0.0 │  │ v1.1.0  │
    │ ACTIVE  │  │ IDLE   │           │ IDLE   │  │ ACTIVE  │
    └─────────┘  └────────┘           └────────┘  └─────────┘

  Rollback: Switch selector back to blue → instant revert

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Blue/Green = instant rollback (just switch the Service selector). Canary = gradual rollback (reverse the weight). Blue/Green requires 2× resources (both environments running). For anihpj/jobpost, Blue/Green is used for database schema changes where gradual traffic shifting would cause errors with mixed schema versions.

Argo Rollouts Q76 of 200
What is Canary deployment and how do canary steps control the rollout progression?
A) Canary deploys one pod at a time — replacing pods until all are updated
B) Canary deploys to a separate namespace first, then migrates traffic
C) Canary gradually shifts traffic percentage via setWeight steps: e.g., 10% → pause 5 min → 25% → pause → 100%. Each step can include analysis that auto-promotes or rolls back based on metrics
D) Canary is identical to Kubernetes RollingUpdate — it replaces pods one by one
✅ Correct Answer: C

Canary deployment uses setWeight steps to gradually shift traffic: 10% to new version, pause to observe, 25%, pause, 50%, analyze metrics, 100%. Each step can include pause (duration or manual) and analysis (metric checks). If analysis fails, the rollout auto-rolls back.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Canary Steps — Gradual Traffic Shifting
  strategy:
    canary:
      steps:
      - setWeight: 10             ← 10% of traffic to new pods
      - pause: {duration: 5m}     ← Wait 5 min, observe metrics
      - setWeight: 25             ← 25% traffic
      - pause: {duration: 10m}    ← Wait 10 min
      - setWeight: 50             ← 50% traffic
      - analysis:                 ← Run Prometheus query
          templates:
          - templateName: error-rate-check
      - setWeight: 100            ← All traffic to new version

  Traffic:  [██████████░░░░░░░░░░] 10%
            [████████████████░░░░] 25%
            [████████████████████] 100%
  
  If analysis fails at 50% → auto-rollback to 0% (previous stable version)

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Canary steps are the most tested Rollout feature. Know: setWeight (traffic %), pause (duration or no duration for manual promote), analysis (automatic metric check). For anihpj/jobpost, the API uses canary: 10% → 5min pause → 50% → Prometheus check → 100%.

3.2 Progressive Rollout StrategiesQ77-Q82

Argo Rollouts Q77 of 200
Blue/Green deployment uses activeService and previewService. What is the purpose of previewService?
A) previewService handles production traffic while activeService is for internal testing
B) previewService is deprecated — only activeService is needed for Blue/Green
C) previewService is an alias for activeService — they serve the same purpose
D) activeService points to the live (Blue) environment. previewService points to the new (Green) environment so testers can verify it BEFORE switching production traffic
✅ Correct Answer: D

previewService provides a separate access point to Green before switching. QA hits preview.jobpost-api to verify the new version while users continue on Blue. After verification, promote switches the active selector.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Blue/Green with Preview Service — Test Before You Switch
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  BLUE/GREEN DEPLOYMENT WITH PREVIEW SERVICE                      │
  │                                                                  │
  │  strategy:                                                       │
  │    blueGreen:                                                    │
  │      activeService: jobpost-api       ← Users hit this           │
  │      previewService: jobpost-preview  ← QA hits this             │
  │      autoPromotionEnabled: false      ← Manual promote required  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  BEFORE PROMOTE:                                            │ │
  │  │                                                             │ │
  │  │       Users ──────▶ jobpost-api (active) ──▶ Blue  (v1.0)  │ │
  │  │         QA ──────▶ jobpost-preview     ──▶ Green (v1.1)     │ │
  │  │                                                             │ │
  │  │       Blue = LIVE (serving real traffic)                    │ │
  │  │       Green = IDLE (only QA testing)                        │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  AFTER PROMOTE: (QA approves → argo rollouts promote)       │ │
  │  │                                                             │ │
  │  │       Users ──────▶ jobpost-api (active) ──▶ Green (v1.1)  │ │
  │  │                                                             │ │
  │  │       Green = LIVE (now serving real traffic!)              │ │
  │  │       Blue  = IDLE (ready for instant rollback)            │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                  │
  │  💡 ROLLBACK: Switch selector back to Blue → INSTANT             │
  │     No pods restart, no traffic interruption                     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: previewService enables testing before the switch. Without it, Blue/Green is a blind switch. For anihpj/jobpost, QA verifies Green via previewService before production promotion.

Argo Rollouts Q78 of 200
How does Argo Rollouts route traffic in a Canary deployment?
A) Via Service Mesh (Istio) or Ingress (NGINX, ALB) — Rollout updates VirtualService weights or ingress annotations to shift traffic between stable and canary ReplicaSets
B) Argo Rollouts has a built-in proxy that handles traffic routing
C) Traffic routing is manual — you update DNS records or load balancer configs separately
D) Only Kubernetes Service selectors are used — Rollout updates selectors directly
✅ Correct Answer: A

Argo Rollouts integrates with Istio, NGINX, ALB, SMI, and Linkerd for traffic shaping. As canary steps progress, the controller updates these resources to shift traffic percentages — e.g., Istio VirtualService weight from 10→50→100.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Traffic Routing Providers — Service Mesh & Ingress
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  TRAFFIC ROUTING OPTIONS FOR CANARY DEPLOYMENTS                  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  PROVIDER      │  CONFIGURATION                             │ │
  │  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤ │
  │  │  Istio         │  VirtualService:                           │ │
  │  │                │    http:                                   │ │
  │  │                │    - route:                                │ │
  │  │                │      - destination: stable  weight: 90     │ │
  │  │                │      - destination: canary  weight: 10     │ │
  │  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤ │
  │  │  NGINX Ingress │  annotations:                              │ │
  │  │                │    nginx.ingress.kubernetes.io/canary: true│ │
  │  │                │    canary-weight: "10"                      │ │
  │  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤ │
  │  │  AWS ALB       │  alb.ingress.kubernetes.io/actions.weight  │ │
  │  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤ │
  │  │  SMI / Linkerd │  TrafficSplit resource                     │ │
  │  │                │    backends: [{service: stable, weight:90}]│ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─────── TRAFFIC FLOW ──────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │                                                            │   │
  │  │  Users ──▶ Ingress/ServiceMesh ──┬── 90% ──▶ Stable pods  │   │
  │  │                                  │                          │   │
  │  │                                  └── 10% ──▶ Canary pods  │   │
  │  │                                                            │   │
  │  │  Rollout controller updates weights as canary progresses:  │   │
  │  │  10% → 25% → 50% → 100% (via Istio/NGINX/ALB)             │   │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Traffic routing is essential for canary. For anihpj/jobpost on EKS, ALB Ingress handles traffic shifting automatically as the Rollout progresses.

Argo Rollouts Q79 of 200
A canary is at 50% with an indefinite pause. How do you advance to 100%?
A) Edit the Rollout YAML to remove the pause — no promote command exists
B) kubectl argo rollouts promote <name> — skips the pause and advances
C) Wait for the pause to expire — indefinite pauses auto-expire after 1 hour
D) argocd app sync — Argo CD handles Rollout promotion
✅ Correct Answer: B

Promotion advances the rollout past a pause step. With pause: {} (no duration), promotion is the ONLY way to advance. With pause: {duration: 10m}, promotion skips the remaining wait. Promotion can be manual (CLI/dashboard) or automatic (after successful analysis).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Promotion — Manual & Automatic Gate Control
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CANARY STEPS WITH PROMOTION GATES                               │
  │                                                                  │
  │  strategy:                                                       │
  │    canary:                                                       │
  │      steps:                                                      │
  │      - setWeight: 10          ← 10% traffic to canary           │
  │      - pause: {}              ← INDEFINITE: MUST promote         │
  │      - setWeight: 50          ← 50% traffic (after promote)     │
  │      - pause: {duration: 10m} ← 10 min OR promote now           │
  │      - setWeight: 100         ← 100% (after duration/promote)   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  PAUSE TYPES:                                               │ │
  │  │                                                             │ │
  │  │  pause: {}                     = Manual promotion REQUIRED  │ │
  │  │    → $ kubectl argo rollouts promote jobpost-api           │ │
  │  │                                                             │ │
  │  │  pause: {duration: 10m}        = Auto-advance after 10 min  │ │
  │  │    → Promote NOW skips remaining wait time                  │ │
  │  │                                                             │ │
  │  │  analysis: {templates: [...]}  = Auto-promote if pass      │ │
  │  │    → Auto-rollback if fail                                  │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─── EXECUTION FLOW ────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │                                                            │   │
  │  │  10% traffic                                                │   │
  │  │     │                                                       │   │
  │  │     ▼  (human runs: kubectl argo rollouts promote)         │   │
  │  │  50% traffic                                                │   │
  │  │     │                                                       │   │
  │  │     ▼  (auto after 10min, or human promotes early)         │   │
  │  │  100% traffic → COMPLETE                                    │   │
  │  │                                                            │   │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Promotion is the manual approval gate. For anihpj/jobpost production, canary uses indefinite pause at 10% — SRE promotes after verifying metrics.

Argo Rollouts Q80 of 200
What is an AnalysisTemplate and how does it integrate with a Rollout?
A) AnalysisTemplate is a Grafana dashboard for rollout metrics
B) AnalysisTemplate is a manual checklist for SREs before promoting
C) AnalysisTemplate defines metric queries with success/failure conditions. A Rollout references it in canary steps — if analysis fails, the rollout auto-rolls back
D) AnalysisTemplate is a Helm chart for deploying monitoring tools
✅ Correct Answer: C

AnalysisTemplate defines metric queries (Prometheus, Datadog, Job) with successCondition and failureLimit. A Rollout's canary step references it. Argo creates an AnalysisRun that executes queries and determines pass/fail — failed analysis triggers auto-rollback.

📝 Detailed Explanation
AnalysisTemplate — Automated Metric Validation
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  AnalysisTemplate: error-check                                   │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  metrics:                                                   │ │
  │  │  - name: error-rate                                         │ │
  │  │    provider:                                                │ │
  │  │      prometheus:                                            │ │
  │  │        query: rate(errors[5m]) / rate(total[5m])           │ │
  │  │    successCondition: result < 0.01    ← Error < 1%          │ │
  │  │    failureLimit: 3                    ← 3 strikes → fail    │ │
  │  │    interval: 30s                      ← Check every 30s     │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─── ROLLOUT INTEGRATION ───────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │                                                            │   │
  │  │  canary steps:                                             │   │
  │  │  - setWeight: 50                                           │   │
  │  │  - analysis:            ← References AnalysisTemplate      │   │
  │  │      templates:                                            │   │
  │  │      - templateName: error-check                           │   │
  │  │                                                            │   │
  │  │  RESULT:  error 0.5% ✅ → PASS → continue to 100%         │   │
  │  │           error 2.1% ❌ (3×) → FAIL → auto-rollback       │   │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: AnalysisTemplate automates deployment safety. For anihpj/jobpost, error-rate > 1% triggers auto-rollback within 90 seconds.

Argo Rollouts Q81 of 200
Which metrics providers can AnalysisTemplate query?
A) Only Prometheus — Argo Rollouts exclusively supports Prometheus
B) Prometheus and Datadog only
C) Prometheus, Datadog, and NewRelic
D) Prometheus, Datadog, NewRelic, Wavefront, Web, Job, CloudWatch, Graphite, InfluxDB — 9+ providers natively supported
✅ Correct Answer: D

Argo Rollouts supports 9+ providers. Job is unique — it runs a K8s Job whose exit code is the metric result (0=pass, non-zero=fail). Web makes HTTP calls to any API. Prometheus is the most common for metric queries.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Metrics Providers — 9+ Native Integrations
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ANALYSIS METRICS PROVIDERS                                      │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  PROVIDER      │  QUERY TYPE              │  USE CASE       │ │
  │  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤ │
  │  │  prometheus    │  PromQL: rate(...)[5m]   │  Error rate,    │ │
  │  │                │                          │  latency, CPU   │ │
  │  │  datadog       │  Datadog Metrics API     │  APM metrics    │ │
  │  │  newrelic      │  NRQL queries            │  Apdex, throughput│
  │  │  job           │  K8s Job exit code       │  Smoke tests    │ │
  │  │  web           │  HTTP GET → JSON parse   │  Custom APIs    │ │
  │  │  cloudwatch    │  AWS CloudWatch API      │  AWS metrics    │ │
  │  │  wavefront     │  Wavefront Query Lang    │  Enterprise     │ │
  │  │  graphite      │  Graphite functions      │  Time-series    │ │
  │  │  influxdb      │  InfluxQL                │  Analytics      │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                  │
  │  💡 Prometheus = most common. Job = run ANY container as test.   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Know Prometheus + Job. The Job provider is exam-relevant — it enables custom test logic beyond metrics. For anihpj/jobpost, Prometheus checks error rate; a Job smoke test validates API functionality before promotion.

Argo Rollouts Q82 of 200
For anihpj/jobpost, canary must ensure error rate < 1% AND p99 < 500ms before promotion. How is this configured?
A) Use HPA with custom metrics — scaling replaces analysis
B) Monitor Grafana and manually promote if metrics look good
C) Use Argo CD health checks for application validation
D) AnalysisTemplate with two Prometheus metrics: error-rate (< 1%) and p99-latency (< 500ms). Both must pass or auto-rollback
✅ Correct Answer: D

Multi-metric AnalysisTemplate validates all conditions. Each metric has its own successCondition and failureLimit. The AnalysisRun evaluates all metrics in parallel — if ANY fail and exceed their failureLimit, the rollout is Degraded and auto-rolls back.

📝 Detailed Explanation
anihpj Multi-Metric Production Analysis
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  AnalysisTemplate: jobpost-prod-check                            │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  metrics:                                                   │ │
  │  │  - name: error-rate                                         │ │
  │  │    successCondition: result < 0.01    ← ERROR < 1%          │ │
  │  │    failureLimit: 2                    ← 2 strikes = rollback│ │
  │  │    provider:                                                │ │
  │  │      prometheus:                                            │ │
  │  │        query: rate(errors{svc="api"}[5m])/rate(total[5m])  │ │
  │  │                                                             │ │
  │  │  - name: p99-latency                                        │ │
  │  │    successCondition: result < 0.5    ← P99 < 500ms          │ │
  │  │    failureLimit: 2                                           │ │
  │  │    provider:                                                │ │
  │  │      prometheus:                                            │ │
  │  │        query: histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(latency[5m]))  │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─── EXECUTION ─────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │                                                            │   │
  │  │  Both metrics evaluated in PARALLEL every 30s              │   │
  │  │                                                            │   │
  │  │  error-rate ✅ (0.5%)  +  p99 ✅ (320ms)  = PASS → 100%   │   │
  │  │  error-rate ❌ (2.1%)  OR  p99 ❌ (800ms)  = FAIL         │   │
  │  │                                                            │   │
  │  │  FAIL after 2 measurements → Rollout Degraded             │   │
  │  │                            → Canary scaled to 0            │   │
  │  │                            → All traffic back to stable    │   │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Multi-metric analysis is production best practice. For anihpj/jobpost, this ensures both reliability (error rate) and performance (latency) are validated before full deployment. Argo Rollouts + Argo CD + Argo Workflows = complete GitOps progressive delivery.

3.3 Analysis Templates — Deep DiveQ83-Q88

Argo Rollouts Q83 of 200
What is an AnalysisRun and how does it relate to AnalysisTemplate?
A) AnalysisRun is a CLI command — same as kubectl argo rollouts analyze
B) AnalysisTemplate is the definition (what to measure). AnalysisRun is a specific execution instance (when + results). One Template generates many Runs
C) AnalysisRun replaces AnalysisTemplate — Template is deprecated
D) AnalysisRun and AnalysisTemplate are the same CRD with different names
✅ Correct Answer: B

AnalysisTemplate = recipe. AnalysisRun = specific execution. Each canary deployment creates a new AnalysisRun referencing the same AnalysisTemplate. This follows the Kubernetes pattern: Template defines it, Run executes it.

📝 Detailed Explanation
AnalysisTemplate vs AnalysisRun — Definition vs Instance
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  TEMPLATE (Defined ONCE)              RUN (Created per canary)    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  AnalysisTemplate: error-check        AnalysisRun: error-check-142│
  │  ┌────────────────────────┐           ┌────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ metrics:               │           │ Started: 14:30 UTC      │ │
  │  │ - name: error-rate     │──spawns──▶│ Status:  Successful     │ │
  │  │   successCondition:... │           │ Results: 0.005% ✅      │ │
  │  │   failureLimit: 3      │           └────────────────────────┘ │
  │  └────────────────────────┘                                      │
  │           │                         AnalysisRun: error-check-143 │
  │           │                         ┌────────────────────────┐  │
  │           └────────────spawns──────▶│ Started: 15:00 UTC      │  │
  │                                     │ Status:  Failed         │  │
  │                                     │ Results: 2.3% ❌        │  │
  │                                     └────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─── VIEW HISTORY ──────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  $ kubectl get analysisrun                                 │   │
  │  │  NAME                STATUS      AGE                       │   │
  │  │  error-check-142     Successful  2h                        │   │
  │  │  error-check-143     Failed      1h                        │   │
  │  │  error-check-144     Running     5m                        │   │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Template vs Run is a common K8s pattern. For anihpj/jobpost, every canary deployment creates a new AnalysisRun — view history with kubectl get analysisrun.

Argo Rollouts Q84 of 200
How do you parameterize an AnalysisTemplate so it works for multiple services with different thresholds?
A) Analysis arguments — Rollout passes args: [{name: svc, value: api}]; Template references {{args.svc}} in queries. One template serves all services
B) Create separate AnalysisTemplates per service — no parameterization exists
C) Use environment variables in the AnalysisRun pod spec
D) ConfigMap mounted into the AnalysisRun — external configuration
✅ Correct Answer: A

Analysis arguments enable template reuse. The Rollout passes dynamic values; the template references them. This means one generic-error-check template validates API, frontend, and workers — each with different thresholds.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Analysis Arguments — Parameterized Templates
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ROLLOUT PASSES ARGUMENTS:                                       │
  │                                                                  │
  │  strategy:                                                       │
  │    canary:                                                       │
  │      steps:                                                      │
  │      - setWeight: 50                                             │
  │      - analysis:                                                 │
  │          args:                    ← DYNAMIC values               │
  │          - name: service-name                                    │
  │            value: jobpost-api                                    │
  │          - name: error-threshold                                 │
  │            value: "0.01"                                         │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
  │                                                                  │
  │  TEMPLATE RECEIVES ARGUMENTS:                                    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  AnalysisTemplate: generic-error-check                           │
  │    metrics:                                                      │
  │    - name: error-rate                                            │
  │      successCondition: result < {{args.error-threshold}}        │
  │      provider:                                                   │
  │        prometheus:                                               │
  │          query: |                                                │
  │            rate(errors{                                                  │
  │              service="{{args.service-name}}"                           │
  │            }[5m]) / rate(total[5m])                              │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─── REUSABILITY ───────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │                                                            │   │
  │  │  One Template → Multiple Services:                         │   │
  │  │  • jobpost-api:   error-threshold=0.01 (strict)            │   │
  │  │  • jobpost-front: error-threshold=0.05 (lenient)           │   │
  │  │  • jobpost-worker: error-threshold=0.10 (background job)   │   │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Arguments make templates reusable — a core design pattern. For anihpj/jobpost, one template validates all microservices.

Argo Rollouts Q85 of 200
What happens when an AnalysisRun exceeds its failureLimit?
A) The Rollout continues but logs a warning — analysis is advisory
B) The Rollout pauses indefinitely until a human intervenes
C) The Rollout becomes Degraded and auto-rolls back — canary pods scaled to 0, all traffic returns to the stable ReplicaSet
D) The AnalysisRun retries forever — it never permanently fails
✅ Correct Answer: C

When failureLimit is reached, the AnalysisRun fails. The Rollout transitions to Degraded and automatically scales down canary pods. All traffic returns to the previous stable version. This is the core safety mechanism of Argo Rollouts.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Analysis Failure → Automatic Rollback
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  failureLimit: 2, interval: 30s                                  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─── TIMELINE ──────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │                                                            │   │
  │  │  T+0s:   Measurement 1: error rate 0.5%  ✅ (below 1%)    │   │
  │  │  T+30s:  Measurement 2: error rate 1.8%  ❌ (strike 1/2)  │   │
  │  │  T+60s:  Measurement 3: error rate 2.1%  ❌ (strike 2/2)  │   │
  │  │                                                            │   │
  │  │  → failureLimit REACHED → AnalysisRun = Failed            │   │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─── AUTO-ROLLBACK SEQUENCE ─────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │                                                            │   │
  │  │  1. AnalysisRun status → Failed                            │   │
  │  │  2. Rollout status → Degraded                               │   │
  │  │  3. Canary ReplicaSet → scaled to 0 pods                   │   │
  │  │  4. Stable ReplicaSet → serves 100% of traffic             │   │
  │  │  5. Total time: ~90 seconds from first bad measurement     │   │
  │  │                                                            │   │
  │  │  Users: Never saw the bad version (canary only reached 50%)│   │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Auto-rollback is the killer feature. For anihpj/jobpost, a bad release is automatically reverted before most users are affected.

Argo Rollouts Q86 of 200
You need to run a smoke test (API calls, DB queries) as part of canary analysis. Which provider runs arbitrary container logic?
A) web provider — makes HTTP requests only
B) script provider — inline scripts like Argo Workflows
C) Use a PreSync hook — Argo CD handles testing
D) Job provider — runs a K8s Job. Exit 0 = pass, non-zero = fail. Can do API calls, DB queries, complex assertions — anything containerizable
✅ Correct Answer: D

The Job provider runs a Kubernetes Job. Its exit code is the metric result. This enables arbitrary test logic in any language — Python, Go, shell scripts. The job can access the canary service, run integration tests, and validate business logic.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Job Provider — Run Any Container as a Metric Test
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  AnalysisTemplate: smoke-test                                    │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  metrics:                                                   │ │
  │  │  - name: api-smoke                                          │ │
  │  │    provider:                                                │ │
  │  │      job:                                                   │ │
  │  │        spec:                                                │ │
  │  │          template:                                          │ │
  │  │            spec:                                            │ │
  │  │              containers:                                    │ │
  │  │              - name: test                                   │ │
  │  │                image: anihpj/smoke:v1                       │ │
  │  │                command: [pytest, tests/smoke/]              │ │
  │  │                env:                                         │ │
  │  │                - name: API_URL                              │ │
  │  │                  value: http://jobpost-canary:8000           │ │
  │  │              restartPolicy: Never                           │ │
  │  │    failureLimit: 1                                          │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─── JOB LIFECYCLE ─────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │                                                            │   │
  │  │  Container starts → runs pytest tests/smoke/               │   │
  │  │                                                            │   │
  │  │  exit 0 = ALL TESTS PASSED → metric = SUCCESS ✅           │   │
  │  │  exit 1 = SOME TESTS FAILED → metric = FAILURE  ❌         │   │
  │  │                                                            │   │
  │  │  💡 Job provider = any logic in any language               │   │
  │  │     Python, Go, shell, curl — if it exits 0 or 1           │   │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: The Job provider is unique to Argo Rollouts. For anihpj/jobpost, smoke tests validate API health, DB connectivity, and login flow before promotion.

Argo Rollouts Q87 of 200
What configuration controls how many consecutive failed measurements cause an AnalysisRun to fail?
A) maxFailures — the maximum number of failures before the Run fails
B) failureLimit — after this many consecutive failed measurements, the AnalysisRun is marked Failed. maxFailures is the total allowed (including non-consecutive). Using failureLimit: 2 means "fail after 2 consecutive bad measurements"
C) retryStrategy.limit — same as Workflow retry logic
D) AnalysisRuns never fail — they retry until metrics pass
✅ Correct Answer: B

failureLimit controls consecutive failures. maxFailures is the total across the entire run. Set failureLimit: 3 to allow 3 consecutive bad measurements before failing — a single good measurement resets the consecutive count.

📝 Detailed Explanation
failureLimit vs maxFailures — Consecutive vs Total
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  failureLimit: 3     ← Fail after 3 CONSECUTIVE bad measurements │
  │  maxFailures: 5      ← Fail after 5 TOTAL bad measurements       │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─── MEASUREMENT SEQUENCE ──────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │                                                            │   │
  │  │  T+0s:   ✅  (consecutive: 0, total failures: 0)           │   │
  │  │  T+30s:  ❌  (consecutive: 1, total failures: 1)           │   │
  │  │  T+60s:  ❌  (consecutive: 2, total failures: 2)           │   │
  │  │  T+90s:  ✅  (consecutive: 0 ← RESET!, total: 2)           │   │
  │  │  T+120s: ❌  (consecutive: 1, total failures: 3)           │   │
  │  │  T+150s: ❌  (consecutive: 2, total failures: 4)           │   │
  │  │  T+180s: ❌  (consecutive: 3, total failures: 5)           │   │
  │  │                                                            │   │
  │  │  → BOTH failureLimit (3 consecutive) AND maxFailures (5)   │   │
  │  │    reached → AnalysisRun = Failed                          │   │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  💡 failureLimit catches PERSISTENT issues (sustained spike)     │
  │     maxFailures catches INTERMITTENT issues (flaky behavior)     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: failureLimit vs maxFailures is a key distinction. Consecutive failures catch persistent issues; maxFailures catches intermittent issues. For anihpj/jobpost, failureLimit: 2 catches sustained error rate spikes while ignoring transient blips.

Argo Rollouts Q88 of 200
For anihpj/jobpost production, the canary analysis must validate error rate < 1% via Prometheus AND p99 latency < 500ms. How is this multi-condition analysis configured?
A) Two separate AnalysisTemplates running in sequence — one for errors, one for latency
B) Use Argo CD health checks combined with Prometheus alerts
C) Multi-metric AnalysisTemplate: one template with two metrics — error-rate (successCondition: result < 0.01) and p99-latency (successCondition: result < 0.5). Both must pass or the rollout auto-rolls back
D) Run Prometheus query externally and manually promote if both pass
✅ Correct Answer: C

A single AnalysisTemplate can define multiple metrics. Each metric has its own provider, query, successCondition, and failureLimit. The AnalysisRun evaluates ALL metrics in parallel. If ANY metric fails (and exceeds its failureLimit), the AnalysisRun fails and the Rollout auto-rolls back.

📝 Detailed Explanation
anihpj/jobpost Multi-Metric Production Analysis
  AnalysisTemplate: jobpost-prod-check
    metrics:
    - name: error-rate
      successCondition: result < 0.01
      failureLimit: 2
      provider:
        prometheus:
          query: rate(errors{service="jobpost-api"}[5m])/rate(total[5m])
    - name: p99-latency
      successCondition: result < 0.5
      failureLimit: 2
      provider:
        prometheus:
          query: histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(latency_bucket[5m]))

  Both must pass → promote to 100%
  Either fails (2 consecutive bad measurements) → auto-rollback

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Multi-metric analysis is production best practice. For anihpj/jobpost, this ensures both reliability AND performance are validated before full deployment. Combined with Argo CD's GitOps reconciliation, Argo Rollouts provides a complete progressive delivery platform with automated safety gates.

⚡ Category 4: Argo Events (12%)Q89-Q100

🟡 Intermediate: Event-Driven Automation with Argo Events

This category is about triggering workflows from external events using Argo Events. Think of Argo Events as the nervous system of your GitOps platform — it listens for events (GitHub push, S3 upload, SQS message, webhook) and triggers actions (run Workflow, call HTTP, send Slack). For anihpj/jobpost, Argo Events triggers the CI Workflow when code is pushed to GitHub, and triggers deployment verification after Argo CD syncs.

Q89-Q100 12 Questions 🟡 Intermediate

4.1 Argo Events FundamentalsQ89-Q94

Argo Events Q89 of 200
What is Argo Events and what problem does it solve in the Argo ecosystem?
A) Argo Events is a monitoring tool that sends alerts when workflows fail
B) Argo Events is an event-driven automation framework — it listens to external events (GitHub, S3, SQS, webhooks) and triggers actions (create Workflow, call HTTP, send message). It connects the outside world to Argo Workflows/Argo CD
C) Argo Events is a message queue that replaces Kafka/RabbitMQ for event streaming
D) Argo Events is a logging aggregator for Argo Workflows and Argo CD
✅ Correct Answer: B

Argo Events connects external events to internal actions. EventSources listen for events (GitHub webhook, S3 bucket notification, SQS queue message). Sensors filter and transform events. Triggers execute actions (submit Workflow, create Pod, HTTP call). Together they create event-driven CI/CD pipelines.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Argo Events Architecture — External World → Internal Actions
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  EXTERNAL WORLD                    ARGO EVENTS                   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────┐                 ┌─────────────────────┐            │
  │  │ GitHub   │──push event──▶  │                     │            │
  │  │ Push     │                 │  ┌───────────────┐   │            │
  │  └──────────┘                 │  │  EventSource  │   │            │
  │                               │  │  (listens)    │   │            │
  │  ┌──────────┐                 │  └───────┬───────┘   │            │
  │  │ S3       │──upload──────▶  │          │           │            │
  │  │ Bucket   │                 │          ▼           │            │
  │  └──────────┘                 │  ┌───────────────┐   │            │
  │                               │  │   EventBus    │   │  TRIGGERS  │
  │  ┌──────────┐                 │  │   (NATS)      │──▶├──────────┐ │
  │  │ Webhook  │──POST────────▶  │  └───────┬───────┘   ││Argo WF   │ │
  │  └──────────┘                 │          │           ││(CI run)  │ │
  │                               │          ▼           │└──────────┘ │
  │  ┌──────────┐                 │  ┌───────────────┐   │             │
  │  │ Calendar │──cron────────▶  │  │    Sensor     │──▶├──────────┐ │
  │  │ (2 AM)   │                 │  │  (filter,     │   ││HTTP call │ │
  │  └──────────┘                 │  │   transform)  │   │└──────────┘ │
  │                               │  └───────────────┘   │             │
  │                               └─────────────────────┘             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Argo Events completes the Argo suite. Workflows = CI, CD = deployment, Rollouts = progressive delivery, Events = trigger. For anihpj/jobpost, a GitHub push → Argo Events → creates CI Workflow → builds image → Argo CD syncs → deploys to cluster.

Argo Events Q90 of 200
What are the four core components of Argo Events and their roles?
A) Source, Filter, Action, Log — basic event processing pipeline
B) Listener, Router, Processor, Responder — network event handling
C) EventSource (listen), EventBus (transport), Sensor (filter + trigger), Trigger (execute action)
D) Publisher, Broker, Subscriber, Handler — pub/sub messaging pattern
✅ Correct Answer: C

The 4 components: EventSource listens to external event producers (GitHub, S3, SQS, webhook). EventBus transports events (NATS Streaming/JetStream). Sensor subscribes to the bus, filters events, and invokes triggers. Trigger executes the action (create Workflow, HTTP call, Slack message).

📝 Detailed Explanation
4 Core Components — Event Flow from Source to Action
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  COMPONENT       ROLE                   REAL-WORLD EXAMPLE       │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  EventSource     Listens to external    GitHub webhook,          │
  │                  event producers         S3 bucket notification  │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  EventBus        Transports events      NATS Streaming/JetStream│
  │                  (persistent pub/sub)    with at-least-once      │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Sensor          Subscribes to bus,     "If push to main AND     │
  │                  filters events,         test data uploaded,     │
  │                  fires triggers          THEN run CI workflow"   │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Trigger         Executes the action    Submit Argo Workflow,    │
  │                  (what happens)           HTTP call, Slack msg   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  ┌─── FLOW ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  GitHub Push → EventSource → EventBus(NATS) → Sensor →       │
  │  → (filter: main branch?) → Trigger → Argo Workflow submit   │
  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: These 4 components are the core of every Argo Events question. For anihpj/jobpost: GitHub EventSource → NATS EventBus → CI Sensor (filter: push to main) → Argo Workflow Trigger (run CI pipeline).

Argo Events Q91 of 200
Which EventSource types are available for listening to external events?
A) GitHub, S3, SQS, Kafka, Webhook, Calendar, Resource, AMQP, MQTT, Stripe, Slack — 20+ event source types for various external systems
B) Only Webhook and GitHub — Argo Events has limited event source support
C) GitHub, GitLab, Bitbucket — only Git provider event sources
D) Any HTTP endpoint can be an event source — there are no specific types
✅ Correct Answer: A

Argo Events supports 20+ EventSource types: GitHub/GitLab/Bitbucket (webhooks), S3/GCS (bucket notifications), SQS/Kafka/AMQP (message queues), Webhook (generic HTTP), Calendar (cron), Resource (K8s resource changes).

📝 Detailed Explanation
EventSource Types — 20+ External Event Producers
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  EVENT SOURCE         │  TRIGGERS WHEN...                        │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  GitHub/GitLab/Bitbucket│  Push, PR, release, issue comment     │
  │  S3 / GCS              │  Object created, deleted, updated       │
  │  SQS / Kafka / AMQP    │  Message arrives in queue/topic         │
  │  Webhook               │  HTTP POST received at endpoint         │
  │  Calendar              │  Cron schedule fires (e.g., "0 2 * * *")│
  │  Resource              │  K8s resource created/updated/deleted   │
