Argo CD Q142 of 200
How can you declaratively register clusters with Argo CD instead of using the CLI?
A) Add entries to argocd-cm ConfigMap — declarative cluster list
B) Create a Cluster CRD — the only declarative method
C) Create a Secret with label argocd.argoproj.io/secret-type: cluster containing the cluster's server URL and credentials. Argo CD auto-discovers and registers it
D) Declarative registration requires the CLI — Secrets don't work
✅ Correct Answer: C

Clusters can be registered declaratively via Secrets with the label argocd.argoproj.io/secret-type: cluster. The Secret contains server (API URL), config (TLS), and name. Argo CD watches for these Secrets and auto-registers the clusters.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Declarative Cluster Secret
  apiVersion: v1
  kind: Secret
  metadata:
    name: cluster-kind-cl1
    namespace: argocd
    labels:
      argocd.argoproj.io/secret-type: cluster
  stringData:
    name: dev-cluster
    server: https://kind-cl1-control-plane:6443
    config: |
      {"tlsClientConfig":{"caData":""}}
      {"bearerToken":""}

  → Argo CD auto-discovers and registers the cluster

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Declarative cluster registration enables GitOps for Argo CD itself. Store cluster Secrets in Git and let Argo CD manage its own cluster list.

Argo CD Q143 of 200
What is Argo CD's RBAC model and where is it configured?
A) Kubernetes RBAC only — Argo CD inherits K8s Roles and RoleBindings
B) policy.csv in argocd-rbac-cm ConfigMap. CSV format: p, subject, resource, action, object, allow/deny. Supports project-scoped permissions and SSO group mapping
C) Argo CD Web UI — RBAC is configured through the admin dashboard
D) Each Application has its own RBAC — permissions are Application-level, not centralized
✅ Correct Answer: B

Argo CD uses policy.csv in the argocd-rbac-cm ConfigMap. Format: p, <subject>, <resource>, <action>, <object>, <effect>. Supports role-based access with project scoping and SSO group integration via Dex.

📝 Detailed Explanation
RBAC Policy Configuration
  argocd-rbac-cm ConfigMap:
    policy.csv: |
      p, role:admin, applications, *, */*, allow
      p, role:developer, applications, sync, dev-team/*, allow
      p, role:developer, applications, get, dev-team/*, allow
      p, role:readonly, applications, get, */*, allow
      
      g, dev-team, role:developer        ← Group → Role mapping
      g, ops-team, role:admin

  Permission: Who (subject) can Do What (action) on Which (object)

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: RBAC policy.csv is the access control mechanism. For anihpj/jobpost, developers can sync dev but not prod; ops can manage all environments.

Argo CD Q144 of 200
How does Argo CD integrate with enterprise SSO providers like Okta, GitHub, or Google?
A) Argo CD has built-in user management — no external SSO needed
B) Use Kubernetes OIDC — Argo CD inherits cluster-level authentication
C) Argo CD only supports local users — SSO requires a reverse proxy in front
D) Dex — Argo CD's bundled OIDC provider. Configure argocd-cm with Dex connector settings for GitHub, Okta, Google, LDAP, SAML. Dex authenticates users and returns OIDC tokens to Argo CD
✅ Correct Answer: D

Dex is Argo CD's SSO component. It acts as an OIDC provider, federating to upstream identity providers. Configure connectors in argocd-cm for GitHub (OAuth app), Okta (SAML/OIDC), Google (OAuth), LDAP, or any OIDC-compliant provider.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Dex SSO Integration
  User → Argo CD UI → Redirect to Dex → Dex → Okta/GitHub/Google
                                      ↓
  Okta authenticates user → Dex issues OIDC token → Argo CD
  → Argo CD maps group membership → RBAC policy.csv → permissions

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Dex SSO is the enterprise authentication standard. For anihpj/jobpost, GitHub OAuth via Dex allows developers to log in with their GitHub accounts — group mappings control access to dev/staging/prod environments.

2.7 Security & RBAC — Access Control & AuthenticationQ145-Q152

Argo CD Q145 of 200
How do you restrict a developer to only sync Applications in the "dev-team" AppProject and prevent them from touching production?
A) AppProject defines sourceRepos + destinations boundaries. RBAC policy.csv scopes permissions to proj:dev-team:developer. Combined: developer can only deploy from allowed repos to dev namespaces
B) Kubernetes RBAC on the target namespace — Argo CD inherits K8s permissions
C) Application-level annotations — each Application specifies allowed users
D) NetworkPolicy restricting access to production API servers
✅ Correct Answer: A

AppProject + RBAC = defense in depth. AppProject restricts WHERE (source repos, target clusters+namespaces). RBAC restricts WHO (which subjects/roles) and WHAT (which actions). Together they create granular, enforceable boundaries.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Security Layers — AppProject + RBAC
  AppProject: dev-team
    sourceRepos: [github.com/anihpj/*]
    destinations:
    - namespace: dev-*
      server: https://kubernetes.default.svc

  RBAC policy.csv:
    p, proj:dev-team:developer, applications, sync, dev-team/*, allow
    p, proj:dev-team:developer, applications, get, dev-team/*, allow
    g, dev-team-oidc-group, proj:dev-team:developer

  Result: Developer can sync dev-* apps from allowed repos ONLY
          Cannot touch staging or production Applications at all

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: AppProject + RBAC is the security model. For anihpj/jobpost, devs can sync only dev, ops can manage staging, and only release managers can touch production.

Argo CD Q146 of 200
How do you manage Kubernetes Secrets in Git without exposing sensitive values in plaintext?
A) Add secrets to .gitignore — Secrets are managed outside of Git
B) Base64 encode secrets — encoding makes them "secure enough" for Git
C) SealedSecrets (Bitnami) or ExternalSecrets — encrypt Secrets for safe Git storage. SealedSecrets requires cluster-side decryption; ExternalSecrets syncs from AWS/GCP/Azure secret stores
D) Argo CD has built-in secret encryption — no external tool needed
✅ Correct Answer: C

SealedSecrets encrypts Secrets with a cluster-specific key — the encrypted result is safe to commit to Git. Only the target cluster can decrypt. ExternalSecrets syncs from external providers (AWS Secrets Manager, GCP Secret Manager, Azure Key Vault) into Kubernetes Secrets.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Secret Management in GitOps
  SealedSecrets:
    Secret YAML → kubeseal (encrypts) → SealedSecret CRD (safe for Git)
    → Argo CD syncs → SealedSecret controller → decrypts → Secret

  ExternalSecrets (AWS):
    ExternalSecret CRD → references AWS Secrets Manager secret
    → ExternalSecrets controller → fetches from AWS → creates K8s Secret

  For anihpj/jobpost: DB credentials stored in AWS Secrets Manager
  → ExternalSecrets syncs to cluster → Argo CD deploys pods that use the Secret

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Secret management in GitOps is essential. For anihpj/jobpost, DB credentials are in AWS Secrets Manager, synced via ExternalSecrets — never stored in plaintext Git.

Argo CD Q147 of 200

        ...

  → No credentials in Application YAML at all

  $ argocd app history jobpost-api

  14  2026-06-12 14:00    jane.doe   def456    Synced
  13  2026-06-11 09:00    admin      ghi789    Failed

  Layer 1: Git log       → git log (what code changed?)

Argo CD Q149 of 200
What are Config Management Plugins (CMP v2) and when would you use them?

B) CMP v2 replaces Helm and Kustomize — the next-generation templating engine
C) CMP v2 is for custom health checks — it extends Argo CD's health assessment
D) CMP v2 plugins are deprecated — use plain YAML or Helm instead
✅ Correct Answer: A

CMP v2 (Config Management Plugin) extends Argo CD's manifest generation. It runs as a sidecar container alongside the repo-server. The plugin defines init (setup command) and generate (manifest generation command). Use for tools without native Argo CD support.

📝 Detailed Explanation
CMP v2 Sidecar Pattern
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Repo Server Pod                         │
  │  ┌────────────────┐  ┌────────────────┐  │
  │  │  repo-server   │  │  CMP plugin    │  │
  │  │  (main)        │  │  (sidecar)     │  │
  │  │                │  │  init: pip ... │  │
  │  │  Clones repo → │  │  generate:      │  │
  │  │  calls plugin  │  │  jsonnet eval  │  │
  │  └────────────────┘  └────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Plugin config:
    discover.find.command: [find, ., -name, '*.jsonnet']
    generate.command: [jsonnet, eval]

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: CMP v2 is for extensibility beyond Helm/Kustomize. For anihpj/jobpost, if the team uses Jsonnet or CUE instead of Kustomize, a CMP v2 plugin enables native Argo CD integration.

Argo CD Q151 of 200
How do sync waves control resource ordering within a single Application?
A) Resources sync alphabetically — Argo CD doesn't support ordering
B) Annotate each resource with argocd.argoproj.io/sync-wave: "N" (integer, low to high). Wave -1 runs first, then 0, 1, 2... Resources within the same wave sync concurrently. This orders Namespace→CRDs→Deployment
C) List resources in order in the Application spec — manifest ordering controls sync
D) Sync waves only apply to Helm releases — not supported for raw manifests
✅ Correct Answer: B

Sync waves use integer annotations: lower numbers sync first. Wave -1 for prerequisites (Namespace, CRDs), wave 0 for base resources, wave 1 for application deployments. Resources in the same wave sync concurrently.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Sync Wave Ordering
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WAVE  -1 : Namespace, CRDs (prerequisites first)                │
  │  WAVE   0 : Secrets, ConfigMaps, PVC                             │
  │  WAVE   1 : StatefulSet (PostgreSQL)                             │
  │  WAVE   2 : Deployments, Services (Django API)                   │
  │  WAVE   3 : Ingress, NetworkPolicy                               │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Annotation: argocd.argoproj.io/sync-wave: "2"

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Sync waves are distinct from hooks. Waves order resources WITHIN the Sync phase. Hooks run OUTSIDE sync phases (PreSync/PostSync). For anihpj/jobpost, waves ensure PostgreSQL starts before Django.

Argo CD Q152 of 200

  Bootstrap Application (app-of-apps):

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: App of Apps is the standard bootstrap pattern, frequently tested. For anihpj/jobpost, one bootstrap Application creates all 6 cluster-specific child Applications — install Argo CD, sync bootstrap, entire platform is live.

2.8 Advanced Sync & Health PatternsQ153-Q160

Argo CD Q153 of 200
Argo CD's built-in health checks cover standard resources (Deployment, StatefulSet). How do you add custom health checks for your application?
A) Argo CD health checks are fixed — custom checks require a separate monitoring tool
B) Lua-based custom health checks in argocd-cm ConfigMap under resource.customizations. Write Lua scripts that inspect resource status and return Healthy/Progressing/Degraded
C) Add health annotations to your Kubernetes resources — Argo CD reads them
D) Use Argo Rollouts AnalysisTemplate — health checks belong to Rollouts, not CD
✅ Correct Answer: B

Custom health checks use Lua scripts in the argocd-cm ConfigMap. You define a Lua function that receives the resource object and returns a health status code. This enables health assessment for CRDs and custom resources.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Custom Health Check — Lua Script Example
  argocd-cm ConfigMap:
    resource.customizations: |
      extensions/Job:
        health.lua: |
          hs = {}
          if obj.status ~= nil then
            if obj.status.succeeded == 1 then
              hs.status = "Healthy"
              return hs
            end
          end
          hs.status = "Progressing"
          return hs

  For anihpj/jobpost: Custom health check for Django migration Jobs
  — Healthy when Job succeeded, Progressing while running

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Custom health checks are essential for CRDs and custom resources. For anihpj/jobpost, a Lua health check validates Django migration Jobs and custom analytics CronWorkflows.

Argo CD Q154 of 200
A Deployment's replicas field is managed by HPA, but Argo CD shows it as OutOfSync because Git says 3 and HPA scaled to 5. How do you prevent this false OutOfSync?
A) Disable auto-sync — the only way to handle HPA-managed fields
B) Set replicas to null in Git — Argo CD ignores null fields
C) ignoreDifferences in the Application spec: {group: apps, kind: Deployment, jsonPointers: [/spec/replicas]}. Argo CD skips comparing specified fields
D) Remove the HPA — GitOps and HPA are incompatible
✅ Correct Answer: C

ignoreDifferences tells Argo CD to skip specific fields during diff comparison. This solves the HPA conflict: Git declares the base replica count, HPA scales dynamically, and Argo CD ignores the replicas field in its drift detection.

📝 Detailed Explanation
ignoreDifferences — Skip HPA-Managed Fields
  spec:
    ignoreDifferences:
    - group: apps
      kind: Deployment
      jsonPointers:
      - /spec/replicas              ← Ignore HPA changes
    - group: apps
      kind: Deployment
      jqPathExpressions:
      - .spec.template.spec.containers[].resources  ← JQ syntax

  For anihpj/jobpost: HPA scales Django pods 3→10 under load.
  ignoreDifferences prevents Argo CD from reverting HPA scaling.

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: ignoreDifferences is essential for HPA, VPA, mutating webhooks, and operators that modify resources post-deployment. For anihpj/jobpost, it prevents Argo CD from fighting the HPA on replica counts.

Argo CD Q155 of 200
A Deployment is stuck because a field is immutable. What sync option forces deletion and recreation?
A) Replace — argocd app sync --force --replace deletes and recreates the resource instead of patching. Used when immutability prevents standard updates
B) Prune — deletes resources not in Git, not related to immutability
C) ApplyOutOfSyncOnly — only updates out-of-sync resources
D) ServerSideApply — uses K8s server-side apply instead of client-side
✅ Correct Answer: A

Replace sync option deletes and recreates the resource. This is necessary when fields are immutable (e.g., StatefulSet volumeClaimTemplates, Job spec fields). Standard patching fails; Replace handles it.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Sync Options Overview
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  OPTION              BEHAVIOR                                    │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  ApplyOutOfSyncOnly  Only sync resources with drift              │
  │  Prune               Delete resources removed from Git           │
  │  Replace             Delete + recreate (for immutable fields)    │
  │  Force               Force delete (orphan dependents)            │
  │  ServerSideApply     Use K8s server-side apply (field managers)  │
  │  RespectIgnoreDiff   Honor ignoreDifferences during sync         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Replace is tested for immutable field scenarios. For anihpj/jobpost, if PostgreSQL StatefulSet volume size changes, Replace is needed because volumeClaimTemplates are immutable.

Argo CD Q156 of 200
How do you set up progressive sync where Namespace deploys first, then Secrets, then StatefulSet, then Deployment?
A) List resources in order in Git — Argo CD syncs alphabetically
B) Separate Applications with dependencies — one per resource type
C) PreSync hooks for each resource — hooks control ordering within an Application
D) App of Apps for tier ordering (Namespace app → DB app → API app) + sync waves within each Application for resource ordering (Secret wave 0 → StatefulSet wave 1 → Service wave 2)
✅ Correct Answer: D

Combine App of Apps (tier ordering across applications) with sync waves (resource ordering within an application). The bootstrap app creates child apps in order; each child uses sync wave annotations for its own resources.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Progressive Sync — App of Apps + Sync Waves
  BOOTSTRAP APP (App of Apps)
  ├── Wave 1: infra-app → Namespace, CRDs, RBAC
  ├── Wave 2: secrets-app → Secrets, ConfigMaps (sync-wave: -1)
  ├── Wave 3: db-app → PVC(0) → StatefulSet(1) → Service(2)
  └── Wave 4: api-app → ConfigMap(0) → Deployment(1) → Service(2)

  Each child app waits for previous to be Healthy
  Within each app, sync waves order resource creation

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Progressive sync is the production pattern for complex stacks. For anihpj/jobpost, this ensures PostgreSQL is running before Django starts — preventing CrashLoopBackOff.

Argo CD Q157 of 200
How can Argo CD perform a Blue/Green deployment using two separate Applications?

Blue/Green with Argo CD
  Application: jobpost-blue         Application: jobpost-green

  └── Service: jobpost-active      └── Service: jobpost-preview
      selector: version=blue           selector: version=green

  Switch: Update jobpost-active selector from blue→green
  Rollback: Switch back blue→blue (instant!)

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Argo CD + Argo Rollouts are complementary. CD handles the GitOps aspect; Rollouts handles progressive delivery. For simple Blue/Green, CD alone can manage it with two Applications.

Argo CD Q160 of 200
How do you backup and restore Argo CD's state for disaster recovery?
A) Backup the etcd database — Argo CD state is stored there
B) No backup needed — Git is the backup. Just recreate Applications from Git
C) Snapshot the argocd namespace with Velero — full namespace backup
D) argocd-util export exports all Argo CD data (Applications, Projects, settings) as YAML. argocd-util import restores. Combine with Velero for Secrets and cluster credentials
✅ Correct Answer: D

argocd-util export exports Applications, AppProjects, and settings. argocd-util import restores them. Combine with Velero for Secrets (cluster credentials, repo credentials). Applications themselves redeploy from Git — only Argo CD's own configuration needs backup.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Argo CD Backup Strategy
  argocd-util export > backup.yaml      ← Applications, Projects, settings
  velero backup include argocd          ← Secrets, cluster credentials
  
  RESTORE:
  argocd-util import backup.yaml        ← Restore Applications + Projects
  velero restore                        ← Restore Secrets + credentials
  → Argo CD resumes reconciliation → apps sync from Git

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Backup strategy is essential for production. For anihpj/jobpost, weekly argocd-util export + daily Velero snapshots ensure Argo CD can be restored within minutes of a disaster.

2.9 Advanced Application PatternsQ161-Q166

Argo CD Q161 of 200
Argo CD 2.6+ supports multiple sources in a single Application. How do you combine a Helm chart with Kustomize patches in one Application?
A) spec.sources (array): first source defines the Helm chart (rendered first), subsequent sources apply modifications (Kustomize patches on rendered output). The pipeline: Helm template → Kustomize build → apply
B) Multiple sources require separate Applications — one for Helm, one for Kustomize
C) Use a CMP plugin — native Helm+Kustomize combination isn't supported
D) Render Helm locally and commit output — then apply Kustomize in a second Application
✅ Correct Answer: A

Argo CD 2.6+ multiple sources chains rendering: Source 1 produces base manifests (Helm template), Source 2+ applies modifications (Kustomize patches). The final combined output is applied to the cluster — all in one Application.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Multiple Sources Pipeline
  spec:
    sources:
    - repoURL: https://charts.bitnami.com
      chart: postgresql              ← Source 1: Helm chart
      helm:
        values: ...
    - repoURL: github.com/anihpj/jobpost
      path: k8s/patches/postgres    ← Source 2: Kustomize overlay

  Pipeline:
  Source 1 → helm template → raw YAML
  → Source 2 → kustomize build on rendered YAML → final YAML → apply

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Multiple sources is a key 2.6+ feature. For anihpj/jobpost, deploy PostgreSQL Helm chart + apply company security labels via Kustomize — one Application.

Argo CD Q162 of 200
PreSync and PostSync hooks control deployment lifecycle. How do you run database migrations BEFORE the application deploys and smoke tests AFTER?
A) Use sync waves — PreSync resources with wave -1, PostSync with wave 999
B) PreSync/PostSync are Argo Events concepts — use Events for lifecycle hooks
C) Annotate the migration Job with argocd.argoproj.io/hook: PreSync and the smoke test Job with hook: PostSync. PreSync runs first; PostSync runs after all resources are healthy
D) Run migrations as init containers — hooks are not needed
✅ Correct Answer: C

PreSync hooks run BEFORE any sync wave resources. PostSync hooks run AFTER all resources are healthy. Migrations (PreSync) ensure schema compatibility before pods start. Smoke tests (PostSync) validate the deployment works end-to-end.

📝 Detailed Explanation
PreSync/PostSync Hook Lifecycle
  PreSync:  python manage.py migrate      ← Schema update BEFORE deploy
  ─────────────────────────────────────
  Sync:     Wave 0: Secrets, ConfigMaps
            Wave 1: StatefulSet (PostgreSQL)
            Wave 2: Deployment (Django API)
  ─────────────────────────────────────
  PostSync: pytest tests/smoke/           ← Validate AFTER deploy

  Annotations:
    argocd.argoproj.io/hook: PreSync       (or PostSync / SyncFail)
    argocd.argoproj.io/hook-delete-policy: HookSucceeded

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Hooks vs Waves is a critical distinction. Hooks run OUTSIDE sync phases. Waves order resources WITHIN the Sync phase. For anihpj/jobpost, migrations (PreSync) + smoke tests (PostSync) ensure safe deployments.

Argo CD Q163 of 200
How do sync windows prevent production deployments during business hours while allowing emergency fixes?
A) Sync windows cannot be bypassed — deployments are strictly blocked
B) Disable sync windows temporarily via the Web UI — admin override
C) Use manual sync only during business hours — no windows needed
D) Deny window blocks syncs 9 AM–5 PM. Emergency bypass: add annotation argocd.argoproj.io/sync-options: Force=true to override the window for critical fixes
✅ Correct Answer: D

Sync windows in AppProject block syncs during specified times. The Force=true annotation bypasses the window for emergencies. This is logged and auditable — you can see who forced a sync during a blocked window.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Sync Windows with Emergency Override
  AppProject syncWindows:
  - kind: deny
    schedule: "0 9 * * 1-5"      ← Mon-Fri 9 AM
    duration: 8h                  ← Block for 8 hours
    manualSync: true              ← Allow manual syncs

  Emergency:
    kubectl annotate app jobpost-prod \
      argocd.argoproj.io/sync-options=Force=true
    → Sync allowed despite window → annotation removed after sync

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Sync windows with Force override balance safety with flexibility. For anihpj/jobpost production, syncs are blocked 9-5, but critical hotfixes can bypass via Force annotation.

Argo CD Q164 of 200
How do you scale Argo CD to manage 1000+ applications across 50 clusters?
A) Run multiple Argo CD instances — one per cluster. Single-instance scaling is limited
B) Controller sharding: assign Applications to specific controller replicas using cluster labels or Application labels. Redis tuning: increase Redis memory. Repo-server scaling: horizontal replicas with affinity
C) Argo CD auto-scales — no configuration needed for 1000+ apps
D) Use Argo CD ApplicationSet instead of Applications — ApplicationSets are more efficient
✅ Correct Answer: B

Controller sharding distributes Application reconciliation across multiple controller replicas. Each shard handles a subset of Applications based on labels or cluster assignments. Redis caching reduces Git operations. Repo-server replicas handle parallel manifest generation.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Argo CD Scalability
  Controller Sharding:
    Shard 1: Applications in clusters [kind-cl1, kubeadm]
    Shard 2: Applications in clusters [kind-cl2, kind-cl3]
    Shard 3: Applications in clusters [minikube, mgmt]

  Redis: Increase memory, enable persistence for large cache
  Repo-server: 3 replicas for parallel manifest generation
  API Server: 2 replicas behind load balancer for HA

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Scalability patterns are relevant for production. Your 6-cluster lab with ~20 Applications demonstrates small-scale patterns; sharding prepares for enterprise-scale 1000+ app deployments.

Argo CD Q166 of 200
For high availability, how many replicas should each Argo CD component run?
A) One replica per component — Argo CD is designed for single-replica operation
B) Only the API server needs HA — other components are stateless
C) API Server: 2+ replicas (active-active). Repo Server: 2+ replicas (active-active). Application Controller: 2+ replicas (active-passive with leader election). Redis: 3 replicas (sentinel for HA)
D) All components are stateless — no HA configuration needed
✅ Correct Answer: C

HA Argo CD: API Server (2+, active-active, load balanced), Repo Server (2+, active-active), Application Controller (2+, leader election with lease), Redis (3 with Sentinel). The controller uses leader election — only the leader reconciles; standby takes over if leader fails.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Argo CD High Availability
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  COMPONENT          REPLICAS   MODE            FAILURE HANDLING  │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  API Server          2+       Active-Active    Load balancer     │
  │  Repo Server         2+       Active-Active    Any replica       │
  │  App Controller      2+       Leader election  Standby promotes  │
  │  Redis               3        Sentinel         Auto-failover     │
  │  Dex (SSO)           2+       Active-Active    Load balancer     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: HA configuration is production-essential. For anihpj/jobpost managing 6 clusters, HA Argo CD ensures GitOps continuity even during component failures.

2.10 Production Best PracticesQ167-Q170

Argo CD Q167 of 200
How do you structure Git repos for a multi-environment GitOps strategy (dev, staging, prod)?
A) Single repo, multiple overlays: k8s/base/ (common manifests) + k8s/overlays/dev/, staging/, prod/ using Kustomize. Argo CD Applications point to their respective overlay. Git branches deploy automatically on push to env-specific branch
B) One repo per environment — complete isolation. Merge manifests between repos for promotion
C) Single environment with labels — use namespace labels to differentiate dev/prod
D) Use ApplicationSets to auto-generate apps per environment — one generator per cluster
✅ Correct Answer: A

Kustomize overlays: Base defines common config, overlays customize per environment (replicas, resources, ingress hostnames). Argo CD Applications target overlays/{env}. Promotion = copying overlay to prod path in Git.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Multi-Environment Repo Structure
  k8s/
  ├── base/                        ← Common: Deployment, Service, ConfigMap
  │   ├── deployment.yaml
  │   ├── service.yaml
  │   └── kustomization.yaml
  ├── overlays/
  │   ├── dev/
  │   │   ├── kustomization.yaml   ← replicas: 1, resources: low
  │   │   └── ingress-patch.yaml
  │   ├── staging/
  │   │   └── kustomization.yaml   ← replicas: 2, resources: medium
  │   └── prod/
  │       └── kustomization.yaml   ← replicas: 3, resources: high, PDB

  Argo CD:
    jobpost-dev  → path: k8s/overlays/dev     (auto-sync)
    jobpost-stg  → path: k8s/overlays/staging  (auto-sync)
    jobpost-prod → path: k8s/overlays/prod     (manual sync)

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Repo structure is a fundamental GitOps design decision. For anihpj/jobpost, base + overlays keeps DRY principles while allowing per-environment customization across all 6 clusters.

Argo CD Q168 of 200
What is the recommended approach for managing Secrets in an Argo CD GitOps workflow?
A) Commit base64-encoded Secrets to Git — it's version-controlled if the repo is private
B) Argo CD encrypts Secrets automatically with its own encryption key
C) Sealed Secrets or External Secrets Operator (ESO). Sealed Secrets: encrypt Secret in Git (one-way). ESO: sync Secrets from AWS Secrets Manager/Vault. Argo CD deploys these external Secret references as regular resources
D) Secrets are managed outside GitOps — create them manually in each cluster
✅ Correct Answer: C

Sealed Secrets encrypt Secrets with a cluster-specific key — safe to commit to public repos. External Secrets Operator syncs from cloud secret managers (AWS, GCP, Azure, Vault). Argo CD syncs these as regular Kubernetes resources.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Secret Management in GitOps
  Sealed Secrets:
    Git: sealed-secret.yaml (encrypted) → Argo CD syncs → Sealed Secrets
    controller decrypts → kubernetes.io/Secret in cluster

  External Secrets Operator (ESO):
    Git: ExternalSecret CRD → Argo CD syncs → ESO reads AWS Secrets
    Manager → creates kubernetes.io/Secret

  For anihpj/jobpost: AWS Secrets Manager stores DB passwords, API keys.
  ESO ExternalSecret CRDs in Git → ESO syncs to cluster Secrets.

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Secret management is a key GitOps concern. For anihpj/jobpost, ESO with AWS Secrets Manager ensures no plaintext secrets in Git while maintaining full GitOps compliance.

Argo CD Q169 of 200
How do you implement a deployment pipeline where Argo CD deploys to staging after dev succeeds, and to prod after staging smoke tests pass?
A) All environments sync simultaneously — environment ordering is not an Argo CD feature
B) Chain Application syncs with webhooks — dev triggers staging triggers prod
C) Use Argo Events to orchestrate Argo CD syncs across environments
D) Promotion pipeline: Git pull request from dev→staging overlay triggers staging sync. After PostSync smoke tests pass, PR from staging→prod overlay triggers prod sync. Argo CD Image Updater handles automatic image promotion
✅ Correct Answer: D

The promotion pipeline uses Git PRs as gates. Dev auto-deploys. A PR promotes the tested image tag to staging. After staging smoke tests pass (PostSync hook), another PR promotes to prod. This ensures every promotion is reviewed and versioned.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Promotion Pipeline
  Dev push → Argo CD auto-sync dev
       │
  PR: dev overlay → staging overlay (image tag promotion)
       │
  Merge → Argo CD auto-sync staging
       │
  PostSync smoke tests pass
       │
  PR: staging overlay → prod overlay (approved promotion)
       │
  Merge → Argo CD manual sync prod (prod requires manual sync)

  Image Updater: auto-updates image tags in env overlays

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Promotion pipelines are the enterprise GitOps delivery pattern. For anihpj/jobpost, image promotion flows: kind-cl1 (dev) → kubeadm (staging) → kind-cl2/3/minikube (prod).

Argo CD Q170 of 200
What is the recommended Argo CD disaster recovery strategy for fast restoration?
A) Git as the single source of truth: backup Argo CD config (Applications, Projects) with argocd-util export + Velero for Secrets. On DR: git clone repos → reinstall Argo CD → argocd-util import → Velero restore Secrets → all Applications reconcile from Git automatically
B) Full cluster backup with Velero — restore the entire cluster including Argo CD
C) Argo CD is stateless — no DR plan needed. Just reinstall and point to Git repos
D) Multi-region active-active Argo CD — no DR needed with HA across regions
✅ Correct Answer: A

DR strategy: Git repos are the ultimate backup — all desired state lives there. argocd-util export captures the Argo CD Configuration (Applications, Projects, settings). Velero backs up runtime Secrets (repo credentials, cluster credentials). Recovery: Argo CD import → Secrets restore → automatic reconciliation.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Argo CD Disaster Recovery
  PREPARATION (daily/weekly):
  • argocd-util export > argocd-backup-$(date).yaml
  • velero backup create argocd-secrets --include-namespaces argocd
  • Git repos replicated (GitHub/GitLab already handles this)

  RECOVERY (RTO ~15 minutes):
  1. helm install argocd (or argocd-util import)
  2. kubectl apply -f argocd-backup.yaml  (Applications, Projects)
  3. velero restore (Secrets, cluster credentials)
  4. Argo CD auto-reconciles all Applications from Git

  For anihpj/jobpost: RPO = 24h (daily export), RTO = 15 min

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: DR strategy tests understanding of GitOps principles: Git IS the backup. For anihpj/jobpost, weekly exports + daily Velero + Git replication ensure business continuity.

3.4 Advanced Canary StrategiesQ171-Q176

Rollouts Q171 of 200
How do you configure a canary deployment that increases traffic in small increments (10%, 25%, 50%, 75%, 100%) with automated analysis between each step?
A) Create 5 separate Rollouts — one per traffic percentage. Chain them manually
B) Define canary steps in the Rollout spec: steps: [{setWeight: 10}, {pause: {duration: 5m}}, {analysis: {...}}, {setWeight: 25}, ...]. Each step specifies weight, pause, or analysis. Argo Rollouts executes sequentially
C) Argo Rollouts only supports 50% then 100% — intermediate percentages require custom logic
D) Use Argo CD sync waves — they control canary weight progression
✅ Correct Answer: B

Canary steps define the rollout progression: setWeight changes traffic percentage, pause waits, analysis validates metrics. The Rollout proceeds to the next step only after the current step completes (pause expires or analysis passes).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Multi-Step Canary with Analysis
  strategy:
    canary:
      steps:
      - setWeight: 10           ← 10% traffic to canary
      - pause: {duration: 5m}   ← Wait 5 min, observe
      - analysis:                ← Prometheus analysis
          templates:
          - templateName: error-rate
      - setWeight: 25           ← 25% traffic
      - pause: {duration: 10m}
      - setWeight: 50           ← 50% traffic
      - pause: {duration: 15m}
      - setWeight: 100          ← Full rollout

  Each analysis step must pass before proceeding.
  Any failure → auto-rollback.

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Multi-step canary is the core Rollouts pattern. For anihpj/jobpost, Django API canary starts at 10%, validates error rate, then progresses to 100% over 30+ minutes.

Rollouts Q172 of 200
How does Argo Rollouts work with Kubernetes Services to split traffic between stable and canary pods?
A) The Rollout CRD replaces the Service object — no Service needed
B) The Rollout creates a random traffic split using iptables rules
C) The Rollout controller manages two ReplicaSets: the stable ReplicaSet (existing version) and the canary ReplicaSet (new version). The Service selector matches both, and the controller scales ReplicaSet replicas to achieve the desired weight percentage
D) Traffic splitting requires a Service Mesh — Argo Rollouts doesn't handle traffic routing itself
✅ Correct Answer: C

Argo Rollouts creates two ReplicaSets: stable (current) and canary (new). Pod count determines traffic split percentage: 2 canary pods + 8 stable pods = 20% canary. The Service selector spans both ReplicaSets — a shared label like rollouts-pod-template-hash.

📝 Detailed Explanation
ReplicaSet-Based Traffic Splitting
  Service: jobpost-api (selector: app=jobpost)
  ├── Stable ReplicaSet: 8 pods (v1.0)  → 80% traffic
  └── Canary ReplicaSet: 2 pods (v1.1)  → 20% traffic

  As canary weight increases:
    setWeight: 50 → stable: 5 pods, canary: 5 pods
    setWeight: 100 → stable: 0 pods, canary: 10 pods

  Rollback:
    Scale canary to 0 → 100% back to stable

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Understanding ReplicaSet-based traffic splitting is fundamental. For anihpj/jobpost, this enables gradual Django API canary deployments without a service mesh.

Rollouts Q173 of 200
How do you integrate Argo Rollouts with a Service Mesh (Istio/Linkerd) for precise traffic splitting?
A) Argo Rollouts only uses ReplicaSet scaling — service mesh is not supported
B) Install a separate Istio controller that overrides Rollouts behavior
C) Configure the Rollout with trafficRouting: nginx — it auto-detects the mesh
D) Set trafficRouting.istio in the Rollout spec. The Rollout controller manipulates Istio VirtualService and DestinationRule to shift traffic percentages — no ReplicaSet scaling needed. This provides precise byte-level traffic splitting vs pod-count approximation
✅ Correct Answer: D

With a service mesh, the Rollout controller updates VirtualService weight routes instead of scaling ReplicaSets. This gives precise traffic splitting at the networking layer. For example, 10% means exactly 10% of requests, regardless of pod count.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Istio Traffic Routing with Argo Rollouts
  Rollout spec:
    strategy:
      canary:
        trafficRouting:
          istio:
            virtualService:
              name: jobpost-vsvc
              routes:
              - primary     ← Stable route
            destinationRule:
              name: jobpost-dest
              canarySubsetName: canary

  Argo Rollouts updates VirtualService weights:
    Step 1: stable 90%, canary 10%
    Step 2: stable 75%, canary 25%
    Step 3: stable 0%, canary 100%

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Service mesh integration is an advanced Rollouts feature. For anihpj/jobpost in production clusters, Istio + Rollouts enables precise canary deployments with header-based routing for beta testers.

Rollouts Q174 of 200
How do you configure automatic rollback when a canary or blueGreen deployment fails?
A) Automatic rollback is built-in: if an analysis step fails (error rate, latency), Argo Rollouts automatically scales canary to 0 and returns traffic to stable. For blueGreen, it switches the active Service back to the previous ReplicaSet. No manual intervention needed
B) Rollback is manual — run argocd app rollback after failure
C) Write a PostSync hook that detects failure and runs kubectl rollout undo
D) Argo Rollouts pauses on failure but doesn't rollback — you must manually decide
✅ Correct Answer: A

Automatic rollback is a core Rollouts feature. Analysis failure triggers immediate rollback: canary pods are scaled to 0 (canary) or active Service points back to the original ReplicaSet (blueGreen). The rollout is marked Degraded.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Automatic Rollback Flow
  Canary progressing happily:
    10% → 25% → 50%
             │
             ▼ (AnalysisTemplate checks error rate)
    50% step: error rate 3.2% > threshold 2%
             │
             ▼
    AUTO ROLLBACK:
    • Scale canary ReplicaSet → 0 pods
    • Stable ReplicaSet → 100% traffic
    • Rollout status: Degraded
    • Notification sent (argocd-notifications)

  No manual action required — safety is automatic.

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Automatic rollback is the key safety mechanism. For anihpj/jobpost, if the Django canary starts returning 5xx errors beyond the threshold, Rollouts automatically reverts within seconds.

Rollouts Q175 of 200
What is the difference between pause: {duration: 10m} and pause: {} in a canary step?
A) They are identical — both pause indefinitely until analysis passes
B) duration pause: waits exactly N minutes, then proceeds automatically. Indefinite pause: pauses until manually promoted via argocd app promote or via an Argo CD UI "Promote" button. Used for manual approval gates
C) The duration pause runs analysis during the pause — indefinite doesn't
D) Indefinite pause requires a webhook to unpause — only external triggers work
✅ Correct Answer: B

Duration pause ({duration: 10m}) auto-advances after the time elapses. Indefinite pause ({}) requires manual promotion — a human or external system must approve before the rollout continues.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Pause Types
  DURATION PAUSE:                      INDEFINITE PAUSE:
  - setWeight: 10                      - setWeight: 10
  - pause: {duration: 5m}   ←──auto   - pause: {}           ←──manual
    5 min later, next step...              Needs: kubectl argo rollouts promote

  Use Case:                            Use Case:
  "Wait 10 min for metrics to         "Require SRE approval before
   stabilize, then auto-advance"        proceeding to 100% production"

  For anihpj/jobpost:
    Dev/Staging: duration pauses
    Production: indefinite pause at 50% — requires manual SRE promote

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Pause types distinguish automated vs manual approval gates. For anihpj/jobpost production, an indefinite pause at 50% ensures SRE approval before full rollout.

Rollouts Q176 of 200
How do you configure header-based routing so beta testers get the canary version while regular users get stable?
A) Create two separate Rollouts — one for beta, one for production
B) Use Kubernetes labels on pods — pods with beta=true get beta traffic
C) Istio header-based routing: configure a VirtualService with HTTP match rules (e.g., headers: {x-beta-user: "true"}). Argo Rollouts respects these matches during canary — beta users are routed to the canary subset regardless of global traffic percentage
D) Use ConfigMap to list beta user IPs — Argo Rollouts reads this to route traffic
✅ Correct Answer: C

With Istio traffic routing, you can add HTTP match rules for beta users. Argo Rollouts preserves these matches during canary progression. Beta testers always hit the canary subset; regular users follow the weight-based split.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Header-Based Canary Routing
  Istio VirtualService:
    http:
    - match:
      - headers:
          x-beta-user:
            exact: "true"            ← Beta testers
      route:
      - destination:
          host: jobpost-api
          subset: canary             ← Always canary

    - route:
      - destination:
          host: jobpost-api
          subset: stable              ← Regular users following weight
        weight: 80
      - destination:
          host: jobpost-api
          subset: canary
        weight: 20                    ← Regular users 20% canary

  Argo Rollouts updates weights but preserves beta header match.

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Header-based routing is an advanced progressive delivery pattern. For anihpj/jobpost, internal team members with x-beta-user: true get early access to canary features for testing.

Rollouts Q177 of 200
You deploy a new Django release with database migrations via a PreSync hook. The canary starts, but analysis detects increased error rates from database schema mismatch. What happens?
A) The canary continues — schema errors resolve automatically when migrations run on the new pod
B) Argo Rollouts retries the analysis — it may be a transient error
C) The Rollout pauses indefinitely — manual investigation required
D) AnalysisTemplate fails (errorRate > threshold) → Argo Rollouts auto-rolls back: canary pods scaled to 0, traffic returns 100% to stable v1.0. The Rollout is marked Degraded. PreSync hook failure would have prevented deployment, but this is a post-deploy analysis failure
✅ Correct Answer: D

Analysis failure triggers immediate rollback. The scenario describes a canary that passed PreSync (migrations succeeded) but discovered a schema mismatch under real traffic. The analysis framework catches this and auto-rolls back.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Canary Failure: Schema Mismatch Detected
  Timeline:
  T0: PreSync hook → migrate succeeds (no error)
  T1: Canary 10% traffic → some 500 errors (schema mismatch)
  T2: AnalysisTemplate checks errorRate → 4.7% > 2% threshold
  T3: AUTO ROLLBACK:
      • Canary ReplicaSet → 0 pods
      • Stable v1.0 → 100% traffic
      • Rollout: Phase=Degraded
      • Notify #dev-team Slack

  Investigation: Migration was backward-compatible but a new NOT NULL
  column had no default. Fix migration → new Rollout.

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Analysis catches problems PreSync hooks miss. For anihpj/jobpost, this demonstrates why both hooks AND analysis are needed — hooks check deployment readiness, analysis validates runtime behavior.

Rollouts Q180 of 200

  Argo Rollouts → /metrics endpoint
  │

  │       ├── rollout_phase == Degraded > 1min → PagerDuty (Critical)

  Argo CD Notifications:
    trigger: on-rollout-step-completed
    trigger: on-rollout-aborted

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Observability completes the progressive delivery picture. For anihpj/jobpost, alerts on Degraded rollouts ensure the team knows immediately when a canary fails.

Rollouts Q181 of 200
Your Rollout is stuck in "Progressing" for 45 minutes during a canary. How do you diagnose and debug?
A) Delete the Rollout and recreate — it's faster than debugging
B) Check Argo CD Web UI — Rollouts appear as standard Applications
C) Rollouts don't provide debugging information — check pod logs manually
D) kubectl argo rollouts get, describe, promote, abort. get shows rollout status and step progress. describe shows detailed step-by-step state and analysis results. promote manually advances a paused step. abort triggers rollback
✅ Correct Answer: D

The Argo Rollouts kubectl plugin provides rich debugging commands. get shows visual step progress. describe reveals stuck step and analysis results. promote or abort allows manual intervention.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Debugging a Stuck Rollout
  $ kubectl argo rollouts get rollout jobpost-api
  Name:    jobpost-api
  Status:  ◌ Progressing  (Step 3/6: analysis running for 45min)
  Message: analysis step "error-rate" is Inconclusive

  $ kubectl argo rollouts describe rollout jobpost-api
  → Shows: AnalysisRun status, metric results, error messages

  $ kubectl argo rollouts promote rollout jobpost-api
  → Manually advance (if stuck on indefinite pause)

  $ kubectl argo rollouts abort rollout jobpost-api
  → Trigger immediate rollback (if analysis is failing repeatedly)

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: The kubectl plugin is essential for Rollouts operations. For anihpj/jobpost, SREs use kubectl argo rollouts promote after manual approval on production canary gates.

Rollouts Q182 of 200
How do you combine Argo CD's GitOps reconciliation with Argo Rollouts progressive delivery for a complete deployment pipeline?
A) The Rollout CRD is stored in Git. Argo CD syncs it to the cluster (GitOps). Once synced, Argo Rollouts controller picks it up and executes the progressive delivery. Argo CD monitors the Rollout's health. The pipeline: Git push → CD sync → Rollouts execute → CD report health
B) Argo CD and Rollouts cannot be combined — they conflict on resource ownership
C) Use Argo CD for deployments and Rollouts only for canary — they operate on different resources
D) Rollouts replaces Argo CD — the Rollout controller handles both GitOps and progressive delivery
✅ Correct Answer: A

The CD + Rollouts integration: Git holds the Rollout CRD (desired state). Argo CD syncs it to the cluster (declarative deployment). Argo Rollouts controller executes the canary/blueGreen steps. Argo CD reports health. This combines GitOps governance with progressive delivery automation.

📝 Detailed Explanation
CD + Rollouts: Complete Deployment Pipeline
  Git Push (Rollout CRD + image tag update)
       │
       ▼
  Argo CD detects change → syncs Rollout to cluster
       │
       ▼
  Argo Rollouts controller executes canary strategy:
    ├── 10%: Analysis (error rate, latency via Prometheus)
    ├── 25%: Analysis passes → continue
    ├── 50%: Indefinite pause (waiting for SRE approval)
    │   └── kubectl argo rollouts promote
    └── 100%: Rollout complete
       │
       ▼
  Argo CD: Rollout Health = Healthy, Sync = Synced

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: CD + Rollouts integration is the enterprise progressive delivery pattern. For anihpj/jobpost, every Django deployment follows this exact pipeline across all 6 clusters.

3.6 Production AnalysisQ183-Q188

Rollouts Q183 of 200
What is an AnalysisTemplate and where is it defined?
A) A YAML section inside the Rollout spec — inline analysis configuration
B) A standalone Kubernetes CRD (AnalysisTemplate) that defines reusable analysis logic. Metrics (Prometheus, Datadog, Wavefront, web, Job) with success/failure conditions. Referenced by Rollouts, but can also be used independently for background analysis
C) AnalysisTemplate is a Helm chart template for Prometheus queries
D) AnalysisTemplate is defined in Argo CD ConfigMap — shared across all Applications
✅ Correct Answer: B

AnalysisTemplate is a standalone CRD. It defines metric queries and success/failure conditions. Rollouts reference it by name. It can also run as a background analysis (independent of Rollouts) for continuous metric validation.

📝 Detailed Explanation
AnalysisTemplate CRD
  apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
  kind: AnalysisTemplate
  metadata:
    name: error-rate-check
  spec:
    metrics:
    - name: error-rate
      interval: 30s
      count: 5
      successCondition: result <= 0.02
      failureCondition: result >= 0.05
      provider:
        prometheus:
          address: http://prometheus:9090
          query: |
            sum(rate(http_requests_total{status=~"5.."}[2m]))
            /
            sum(rate(http_requests_total[2m]))

  Used by:
    Rollout reference: analysis.templates.templateName: error-rate-check
    Standalone: AnalysisRun can be created independently

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: AnalysisTemplate is a core Rollouts CRD. For anihpj/jobpost, the error-rate-check template is shared across dev, staging, and prod Rollouts with different thresholds per environment.

Rollouts Q184 of 200
How does a ClusterAnalysisTemplate differ from AnalysisTemplate?
A) They are identical — ClusterAnalysisTemplate is a deprecated name
B) ClusterAnalysisTemplate runs on multiple clusters simultaneously for global analysis
C) ClusterAnalysisTemplate is for production only — AnalysisTemplate is for development
D) AnalysisTemplate is namespace-scoped. ClusterAnalysisTemplate is cluster-scoped — available to Rollouts in all namespaces. Use ClusterAnalysisTemplate for organizational standards shared across teams
✅ Correct Answer: D

The only difference is scope: AnalysisTemplate is namespaced, ClusterAnalysisTemplate is cluster-wide. This follows the same pattern as ConfigMap vs ClusterRole — namespace isolation vs global availability.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Namespace vs Cluster Scope
  AnalysisTemplate (namespace-scoped):
    Defined in: jobpost-dev, jobpost-staging, jobpost-prod
    Each namespace can have different thresholds

  ClusterAnalysisTemplate (cluster-scoped):
    Defined once, available cluster-wide
    Example: organization-standard-error-check
    All Rollouts in all namespaces can reference it

  For anihpj/jobpost:
    ClusterAnalysisTemplate: company-standard-latency-p99
    AnalysisTemplate (per-ns): error-rate-check (dev: 5%, prod: 0.5%)

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Scope distinction is tested. For anihpj/jobpost, ClusterAnalysisTemplate enforces company-wide SLI standards; namespace templates allow per-environment thresholds.

Rollouts Q186 of 200
How do you run a Kubernetes Job as a metric test during analysis? (e.g., run Django integration tests as a quality gate)
A) Use the Job provider in AnalysisTemplate: define the Job spec inline (image, command). The analysis creates the Job, waits for completion, and reads the exit code. Exit 0 = pass, non-zero = fail. This allows running integration tests, migrations checks, or schema validation as analysis steps
B) Create a separate Argo Workflow for tests — trigger it before the Rollout
C) Jobs are not supported — use Web provider to call an external test service
D) Use PreSync hooks — they run Jobs before sync, not during analysis
✅ Correct Answer: A

The Job provider runs a Kubernetes Job as part of analysis. This is powerful for custom validation: integration tests, schema checks, API contract tests. The Job's exit code determines pass/fail.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Job Provider: Integration Test Quality Gate
  metrics:
  - name: integration-tests
    interval: 60s
    count: 1                    ← Run once
    provider:
      job:
        spec:
          template:
            spec:
              containers:
              - name: test-runner
                image: anihpj/django-tests:v1.1
                command: [pytest, tests/integration/]
                env:
                - name: API_URL
                  value: http://jobpost-canary:8000
              restartPolicy: Never

  Exit 0 → analysis passes → rollout continues
  Exit 1 → analysis fails → auto-rollback

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: The Job provider is a unique Rollouts feature. For anihpj/jobpost, integration tests run against the canary before it receives more traffic — catching functional regressions that metrics alone would miss.

4.3 Advanced Event PatternsQ189-Q194

Argo Events Q189 of 200
How do you chain multiple events into a pipeline? (e.g., S3 upload → data processing → notification → Workflow trigger)
A) Chain events in a single Sensor — sequential triggers work automatically
B) Event dependency chaining: Sensor A (S3 upload) triggers Workflow A (data processing), which on completion triggers EventSource B (Workflow status webhook), which triggers Sensor B (processing complete), which triggers Workflow B (notification). Each stage is a separate EventSource→Sensor→Trigger chain
C) Use Argo Workflows DAG only — Events are not needed for pipelines
D) Combine all triggers into one Sensor — a single Sensor handles the entire pipeline
✅ Correct Answer: B

Event chaining creates pipelines: each stage emits an event that triggers the next. The output of one operation becomes the input event for the next. This creates loosely coupled, event-driven pipelines.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Event Pipeline Chaining
  S3 bucket upload (jobpost-daily-report.csv)
       │
       ▼
  EventSource: S3 (AWS SNS notification)
       │
       ▼
  Sensor: s3-upload-sensor
       │
       ▼
  Trigger: Argo Workflow (data-processing-pipeline)
       │  ├── Validate CSV
       │  ├── Transform data
       │  └── Load to PostgreSQL
       │        │
       │        ▼ (Workflow emits completion event)
       ▼
  EventSource: Webhook (Workflow status)
       │
       ▼
  Sensor: processing-complete-sensor
       │
       ▼
  Trigger: Argo Workflow (generate-report + Slack notification)

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Event chaining builds complex pipelines from simple components. For anihpj/jobpost, daily report processing chains: S3 upload → validate → transform → load → notify — fully event-driven.

Argo Events Q190 of 200
How do you handle event filtering so a Sensor only triggers on specific conditions? (e.g., only CSV files with prefix "reports/" from S3)
A) All events trigger the Sensor — filter inside the triggered Workflow
B) Configure EventSource filtering — the source filters before sending to Sensor
C) Use Argo CD resource filters — Events don't have native filtering
D) Use Sensor dependency filters: filters section with data and expr fields. Data filters match event payload fields (e.g., S3 key prefix). Expr filters use complex logic expressions. Only matching events activate the trigger
✅ Correct Answer: D

Sensor dependency filters allow precise event matching. Data filters match payload fields (prefix, suffix, exact). Expr filters use boolean expressions for complex logic. Only filtered events trigger the action.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Event Filtering in Sensors
  Sensor: s3-csv-sensor
    dependencies:
    - name: s3-dep
      eventSourceName: aws-s3
      eventName: object-created
      filters:
        data:
        - path: body.s3.bucket.name
          type: string
          value:
          - "jobpost-reports"          ← Only this bucket
        - path: body.s3.object.key
          type: string
          value:
          - "reports/*.csv"           ← Only CSV in reports/
        expr: "s3.bucket == 'jobpost-reports' && s3.key matches 'reports/*.csv'"

  Result: Only CSV files in jobpost-reports bucket with reports/ prefix
  trigger the Sensor. All other S3 uploads are ignored.

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Event filtering prevents noisy triggers. For anihpj/jobpost, only daily report CSVs (not raw data or temporary files) trigger the processing pipeline.

Argo Events Q191 of 200
How do you configure a Sensor to trigger multiple actions on a single event? (e.g., S3 upload triggers both a Workflow AND a Slack notification)
A) A Sensor can have multiple triggers in the triggers array. Each trigger is independent and executes in parallel. One event → multiple actions: Argo Workflow + HTTP notification + Slack message — all from one Sensor
B) Create separate Sensors for each action — one event, one Sensor, one action
C) Multiple triggers require a parent Workflow that orchestrates sub-actions
D) Sensors only support one trigger — use Argo Workflows DAG for parallel actions
✅ Correct Answer: A

A single Sensor can have multiple triggers. When the dependency conditions are met, ALL triggers execute in parallel. This enables one event to kick off multiple independent actions.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Multi-Trigger Sensor
  Sensor: report-upload-sensor
    dependencies:
    - name: s3-report-dep
      eventSourceName: aws-s3
      eventName: object-created

    triggers:                        ← Multiple triggers, one event
    - template:
        name: process-workflow
        argoWorkflow:
          operation: submit
          source:
            resource: workflow-report-process.yaml
    - template:
        name: slack-notify
        slack:
          slackToken:
            name: slack-token
            key: token
          channel: "#data-team"
          message: "New report uploaded: {{event.body.s3.object.key}}"
    - template:
        name: update-dashboard
        http:
          url: https://dashboard.anihpj.com/api/report-status
          method: POST

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Multi-trigger Sensors reduce event infrastructure. For anihpj/jobpost, one S3 upload triggers Workflow processing + Slack notification + dashboard update — all from one Sensor.

C) Use the Webhook EventSource: creates an HTTP endpoint that GitHub sends webhooks to. Configure it with a github endpoint type and a webhook secret. GitHub pushes events → EventSource receives → Sensor triggers. No polling needed

Argo Events Q193 of 200
How do you parameterize a Sensor trigger with data from the event payload?
A) Parameters are fixed in the trigger template — event data cannot influence trigger configuration
B) Use trigger parameters: define parameters in the trigger template that reference event payload fields via src.dependencyName and dataKey. These parameters are passed to the triggered resource (Workflow arguments, HTTP body, Slack message)
C) Write a Lua script to extract parameters — the trigger template does not support parameterization
D) Parameters are environment variables set by the EventSource — not configurable per trigger
✅ Correct Answer: B

Trigger parameters extract values from event payloads. src.dependencyName identifies which event source, dataKey specifies the JSON path. These parameters become Workflow arguments or HTTP body fields.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Trigger Parameterization
  Sensor: s3-upload-sensor
    triggers:
    - template:
        name: process-report
        argoWorkflow:
          operation: submit
          source:
            resource: workflow-report-process.yaml
          parameters:
          - src:
              dependencyName: s3-dep
              dataKey: s3.object.key          ← Extract from event
            dest: spec.arguments.parameters.0.value

  Event payload:
    { "s3": { "object": { "key": "reports/2025-01-15.csv" } } }

  Result: Workflow triggered with parameter "reports/2025-01-15.csv"
  → Workflow processes the specific file that triggered the event

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Parameterization makes event-driven workflows dynamic. For anihpj/jobpost, the S3 file path from the event becomes the Workflow input parameter.

Argo Events Q194 of 200
How do Argo Events handle event deduplication and exactly-once processing guarantees?
A) Argo Events guarantees exactly-once processing — built-in deduplication
B) Events are processed at-most-once — duplicates are silently dropped
C) Use an external message queue (Kafka, NATS) for deduplication — Argo Events doesn't handle it
D) Argo Events provides at-least-once delivery. Deduplication must be handled by the triggered resource (idempotent Workflows, conditional logic). The EventSource may redeliver events on failure. Trigger idempotency is the consumer's responsibility
✅ Correct Answer: D

Argo Events uses at-least-once semantics. Events may be redelivered if the trigger or notification fails. The triggered action must be idempotent — checking if the work was already done before acting on a duplicate event.

📝 Detailed Explanation
At-Least-Once Semantics & Idempotency
  Approach: Design triggered Workflows/actions to be idempotent

  Idempotent Workflow pattern:
    Step 1: Check if file was already processed
            if exists(processed_files/{{file_name}}):
              exit 0  (already done, skip)
    Step 2: Process file
    Step 3: Mark as processed
            create processed_files/{{file_name}}

  This ensures:
    • If event is delivered once → normal processing
    • If event is redelivered → check marker → skip duplicate

  For anihpj/jobpost:
    Report processing Workflow checks a "processed" table in PostgreSQL
    before processing — preventing duplicate report generation.

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Understanding event delivery semantics is critical. For anihpj/jobpost, idempotent Workflows prevent duplicate report processing from at-least-once event delivery.

4.4 Production Event PatternsQ195-Q200

Argo Events Q195 of 200
How do you deploy Argo Events in a highly available configuration?
A) EventSource controller: 2+ replicas (leader-elected, active-passive). Sensor controller: 2+ replicas (leader-elected, active-passive). EventBus: NATS with 3+ replicas (clustered, active-active). Webhook EventSource endpoints must be exposed via a load-balanced Service or Ingress
B) Argo Events is stateless — single replica is sufficient for production
C) Only the EventBus needs HA — controllers are stateless and auto-recover
D) Argo Events HA requires an external Kafka cluster — NATS is single-node only
✅ Correct Answer: A

HA Argo Events: EventSource and Sensor controllers use leader election (only leader processes events). NATS EventBus clusters for message persistence and delivery. Webhook endpoints need load-balanced exposure.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Argo Events HA Architecture
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  COMPONENT            REPLICAS   MODE            NOTES           │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  EventSource Ctrlr    2+        Leader-elected  Standby ready    │
  │  Sensor Controller    2+        Leader-elected  Standby ready    │
  │  EventBus (NATS)      3+        Clustered       Persistent msgs  │
  │  Webhook Svc          LB        Active-Active   Ingress + TLS    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  For anihpj/jobpost:
    HA NATS cluster ensures no events lost during pod failure
    Leader election prevents duplicate event processing

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: HA configuration is essential for production event-driven systems. For anihpj/jobpost, HA ensures critical report processing events are never lost.

Argo Events Q196 of 200
How does the EventBus work and what backend does it use?
A) EventBus is a REST API that Events use to communicate — no message broker
B) EventBus uses Kafka — it requires an external Kafka cluster to be installed
C) EventBus is NATS — a lightweight, high-performance messaging system embedded in Argo Events. EventSources publish events to NATS topics. Sensors subscribe to relevant topics. NATS provides at-least-once delivery with persistence
D) EventBus uses Redis pub/sub — events are published to Redis channels
✅ Correct Answer: C

The EventBus is backed by NATS, a CNCF-graduated messaging system. It's embedded — Argo Events manages NATS pods, configuration, and persistence. EventSources publish to NATS; Sensors consume from NATS. This decouples event production from consumption.

📝 Detailed Explanation
EventBus (NATS) Architecture
  EventSource (S3)
      │
      ▼ (publish to NATS topic: "s3-events")
  ═══════════════════════════════════════════════
  NATS EventBus (Clustered, Persistent)
    ├── Topic: s3-events
    ├── Topic: github-events
    ├── Topic: calendar-events
    └── Topic: webhook-events
  ═══════════════════════════════════════════════
      │ (subscribe to "s3-events")
      ▼
  Sensor: s3-upload-sensor → Trigger: Workflow

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Understanding EventBus/NATS is fundamental. For anihpj/jobpost, NATS ensures reliable event delivery between AWS S3 notifications and report processing workflows.

Argo Events Q197 of 200
How do you monitor Argo Events — EventSource health, Sensor status, event processing metrics?

C) Check EventSource and Sensor status manually via kubectl — no monitoring integration
D) Argo Events exposes Prometheus metrics: event_source_events_received, sensor_triggers_total, eventbus_messages_published. Grafana dashboard for visualization. AlertManager for event processing failures and Sensor errors

📝 Detailed Explanation
Argo Events Monitoring
  Prometheus Metrics:
  • event_source_events_received_total     ← Events received by source
  • event_source_events_filtered_total     ← Events filtered out
  • sensor_trigger_total                   ← Triggers fired by sensor
  • sensor_trigger_failure_total           ← Failed triggers
  • eventbus_messages_published_total      ← NATS messages published

  Alerts:
  • sensor_trigger_failure_rate > 5% → PagerDuty
  • event_source_no_events > 30min → Slack (source may be down)
  • eventbus_messages_pending > 1000 → Warning (backpressure)

  For anihpj/jobpost:
    Grafana dashboard tracks event pipeline health across all stages

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Event monitoring is essential for production reliability. For anihpj/jobpost, Prometheus alerts on event pipeline failures ensure no report processing is missed.

Argo Events Q198 of 200
How do you troubleshoot a Sensor that is not triggering despite events arriving at the EventSource?
A) Diagnostic steps: 1) kubectl get eventsource — check status for errors. 2) kubectl get sensor — check sensor status. 3) kubectl logs — sensor controller logs show dependency matching and trigger execution. 4) Check NATS EventBus — messages may be stuck in queue. 5) Validate dependency filters match the event payload
B) Delete and recreate the Sensor — configuration issues are usually transient
C) Sensors don't have logs — check the triggered Workflow status instead
D) Restart the EventSource controller — it reconnects to event sources
✅ Correct Answer: A

Systematic troubleshooting: check EventSource status (is it receiving events?), check Sensor status (are dependencies matching?), check controller logs (why aren't triggers firing?), validate NATS (are messages stuck?), verify filters (does the event match?).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Sensor Troubleshooting Flow
  Event arrives at EventSource?
  ├── kubectl get eventsource → Status: Active? Events received count?
  │   └── If 0 events: check EventSource config, network, credentials
  │
  Event published to NATS?
  ├── kubectl logs eventbus-controller → messages published?
  │   └── If no messages: EventSource → EventBus connection broken
  │
  Sensor receives event?
  ├── kubectl get sensor → Status: Active?
  │   └── kubectl logs sensor-controller → "dependency matched" or
  │       "dependency filter rejected event"?
  │
  Trigger executes?
  └── kubectl get workflow → was Workflow created?
      └── Check trigger template → parameter mapping, resource reference

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Troubleshooting methodology is tested. For anihpj/jobpost, this systematic approach quickly identifies why report processing wasn't triggered.

Argo Events Q199 of 200
How do you secure Argo Events webhook endpoints exposed to the internet?
A) Webhooks are internal-only — they cannot be exposed to the internet
B) Multiple layers: 1) TLS via Ingress (cert-manager). 2) Webhook secret validation in EventSource (HMAC signature verification). 3) NetworkPolicy to restrict EventSource ingress. 4) RBAC on Sensor/EventSource resources. 5) Rate limiting via Ingress annotations
C) Use a VPN — webhooks only work within a private network
D) Argo Events handles security automatically — no additional configuration needed
✅ Correct Answer: B

Secure webhook endpoints with defense in depth: TLS encryption, HMAC signature verification (validates the event came from the expected source), NetworkPolicies restrict traffic, RBAC controls who can create/modify EventSources, rate limiting prevents abuse.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Webhook Security Layers
  Internet
      │
      ▼
  Ingress (TLS termination + rate limiting)
      │
      ▼
  NetworkPolicy: allow only from Ingress controller
      │
      ▼
  EventSource Webhook endpoint
      │
      ├── HMAC signature validation (webhook secret)
      └── Payload size limits

  EventSource YAML:
    webhook:
      github:
        - endpoint: /push
          method: POST
          url: https://events.anihpj.com
      githubSecret:
        name: github-webhook-secret
        key: token

  For anihpj/jobpost:
    • TLS via Let's Encrypt
    • GitHub webhook secret validated
    • NetworkPolicy: only from ingress-nginx
    • RBAC: only platform team can modify EventSources

Why this matters for the CAPA exam: Security is essential for internet-facing event endpoints. For anihpj/jobpost, multi-layered security protects the event pipeline from unauthorized triggers.

Argo Events Q200 of 200
What is the complete Argo Events implementation for anihpj/jobpost's automated report pipeline?
A) Use CronWorkflows only — scheduled report generation is simpler than event-driven
B) Use a single Sensor with all triggers combined — keep event infrastructure minimal
C) Run Argo Events only in the management cluster — centralize all event processing
D) Complete pipeline: S3 EventSource (report upload) + GitHub EventSource (code pushes) → Sensors with filters (CSV only, main branch only) → Triggers: Argo Workflow (report processing), Slack notification, dashboard update. NATS EventBus for reliable delivery. HA + monitoring + security. Events integrate with Argo CD for deployment triggers and Workflows for processing
✅ Correct Answer: D

The full anihpj/jobpost Argo Events implementation: S3 EventSource for daily reports, GitHub EventSource for CI/CD triggers, filtered Sensors with parameterized triggers, NATS EventBus for reliable delivery, HA deployment, Prometheus monitoring, and TLS-secured webhooks.

📝 Detailed Explanation
anihpj/jobpost — Complete Argo Events Architecture
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    ARGO EVENTS — ANIHPJ/JOBPOST                  │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                                                                  │

  │  │ (daily CSV)   │    │ filter: *.csv    │  │ report-process   │  │
